The spine is structured with raised bands that divide it into several sections. In the second section from the top, there is a damaged red leather label with gold lettering, though it is mostly worn away.

## NOTICE
## FROM THE BOOKSELLERS

The Author of this <term>esteemed Work</term> <note>original: "Ouvrage estimé"; a common 18th-century marketing phrase indicating the book was well-received by scholars</note>, residing in Berlin and occupying his leisure with more important labors; a well-known <term>Man of Letters</term> <note>original: "Homme de Lettres"; a professional intellectual or scholar</note> has kindly taken it upon himself to oversee this second Edition, and to make it worthy of the Public. Here is what his work consists of.

The <term>Preliminary Discourse</term> <note>original: "Discours Préliminaire"; an introductory essay setting the theoretical framework</note>, along with the notes, is entirely by the Editor.

He has added throughout the course of the Work many observations on <term>Natural History</term> <note>the study of plants, animals, and minerals</note>, <term>Physics</term> <note>original: "Physique"; in this period, it often referred to natural sciences and the study of the physical world in general</note>, etc.; and usually these new articles are placed between two brackets. The Ambition of private individuals is
not the same as that of Kings;
the one aspires to invade everything, the other
limits itself to knowing everything.

Such is the activity of the human
spirit, that the larger the horizon of its
ideas, the more it seeks
to extend it further. Columbus finds
himself cramped in Genoa, and
he travels across Europe; soon he
believes himself cramped in Europe,
and he goes to discover the New
World.

defense of these <term>Falkland Islands</term><note>original: "Isles Malouines"; named after the mariners from Saint-Malo, later known in English as the Falklands</note> which were found inhabited only by long-legged spiders and <term>sea-snails</term><note>original: "grelots"; likely referring to a type of univalve shell or bell-shaped marine life found on the coast</note>, and which even the Court of France has just ceded to the Spanish Crown; it is of a more general utility, whether for Sovereigns or for all thinking men, by the ease it provides to penetrate into the <term>Southern Lands</term><note>original: "Terres Australes"; the hypothesized Great Southern Continent, Terra Australis Incognita</note>, and to verify what so many travelers have written regarding the existence of those <term>Giants of the Pole</term> who are called the <term>Patagonians</term><note>original: "Patagons"; a legendary race of giant humans rumored to inhabit the tip of South America</note>.

To leave nothing to be desired on this curious matter—important and still new, despite so many travelers who have dwelled upon it—I shall supplement the silence of <term>Dom Pernety</term><note>Antoine-Joseph Pernety (1716–1796), a Benedictine monk and naturalist who accompanied Bougainville on his 1763 expedition</note> regarding three objects:

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>PRELIMINARY.</header>

the ancient notions regarding the Falkland Islands; the accurate ideas one can form of the Patagonians; and the research to be conducted in the Southern World.

Having only travelers as guides in this career, one can only proceed with the greatest circumspection; it is necessary to weigh their testimonies, to compare them with one another; and even when they are unanimous, to make them yield to that of reason: for in the examination of an obviously absurd fact, the authority of a single Philosopher ought to prevail over the suffrage of all mankind.

There are prejudices which seem inseparable from travels; such is the opinion that one will encounter nothing but the marvelous;

<summary>The author introduces a discussion on the Falkland Islands and the rumored giants of Patagonia, emphasizing the need for rational skepticism when evaluating traveler accounts of the Southern Hemisphere.</summary>
<keywords>Falkland Islands, Patagonia, Southern Lands, Dom Pernety, giants, exploration, scientific reasoning</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discourse regarding the objectivity required of travel writers and begins the historical account of the Falkland Islands.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>->DISCOURSE<-</header>

one then becomes accustomed to seeing it where it is not, and sometimes to inventing it.

A Traveler also believes himself obliged to adopt the prejudices of his Nation: if it is at war with maritime Powers, he will speak of their colonies only with the gall of hatred; his partiality will pierce through even in the idea he gives of various governments. A Dane will speak of a foreign King as a demigod; to an Englishman, he will hardly be a man.

There are also personal prejudices belonging to Travelers, of which one must be wary: such is the enthusiasm for certain talents, which leads respectable Writers to relate everything to this idol of their imagination; the Merchant <note>The author is criticizing writers who view the world only through the narrow lens of their own profession.</note> is too apparent

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>->PRELIMINARY.<-</header>

in Paul Lucas <note>Paul Lucas (1664–1737) was a French merchant and naturalist who traveled extensively in the Ottoman Empire.</note>; one recognizes the Antiquarian too much in Spon <note>Jacob Spon (1647–1685), a pioneer of archaeology.</note>, and the Naturalist in Tournefort <note>Joseph Pitton de Tournefort (1656–1708), a famous French botanist.</note>.

It would be desirable if all Travelers resembled Dom Pernety <note>Antoine-Joseph Pernety (1716–1796), the author of this work and a Benedictine monk who accompanied Bougainville.</note>; that they adopted, like him, no prejudice; *that they saw objects clearly, in order to make them clearly seen, and above all that they wrote with that tone of candor and truth which announces the confidence of the Writer, and which inspires it in his Readers.*

### *Of the first discoveries of the Malouines Islands.* <note>The Malouines are known in English as the Falkland Islands.</note>

<margin>(a) Here is Frezier's text: "If I have suppressed imaginary lands in my Map, I have added effective ones at 51 degrees of latitude, to which I have given the name of New Islands, for having been discovered since the year 1700, mostly by the vessels of St. Malo. I have placed them based on the Memoirs of the *Maurepas* and the *Saint-Louis*, vessels of the East India Company <note>original: "Compagnie des Indes"</note>, which saw them up close; and even this latter vessel took on water there in a pond, which I have marked near Port St. Louis. The water was a little reddish and flat, but otherwise good for the Sea. Both traversed different places; but the one who coasted them most closely was the *Saint-Jean-Baptiste*, commanded by Doublet of Le Havre, who sought to pass through an indentation he saw toward the middle. Navigators of all nations who have landed there have wished for the glory of giving them a name; this species of sovereignty flatters sailors just as astronomers are flattered by the right to name the stars.

I do not speak of Hawkins, who as early as the year 1593 gave, it is said, the name of Virginia to the Malouine Islands; but around 1714 Mr. Fou-

<margin>attributed to the Map of Frezier, on the position of the Patagonian coast.</margin>

Finally, regarding those islands that the Knight Hawkins perceived in 1593 at 50 degrees East of the deserted coast of the Patagonians <note>The Patagonians were the indigenous people of southern South America, often described in early accounts as giants</note>, it is not probable that they are the northern part of the Malouine Islands; Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville, the French explorer who established the first settlement on the islands</note> sailed along this coast for at least 60 leagues like Hawkins, and perceived no fire there, nor any appearance of habitation, although he was often only half a league or a league away from it.

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>PRELIMINARY.</header>

-quet of Saint-Malo called them Anican, after the name of his ship-owner <term>Armateur: a person or company that fits out and commissions a merchant ship</term>; Admiral Roggewin, who in 1721 coasted the main island on the eastern side, gave it the name of Southern Belgia; some English Captains have made them known under the name of the Falkland Islands; our ship-owners have sometimes called them the New Islands of Saint-Louis; and it appears that Europe today consents to leave them the name of the Malouine Islands <note>original: "Isles Malouines," derived from the sailors of Saint-Malo</note>.

Near the great Malouine Island on the northern side, are three small islands arranged in a triangle, which were for some time confused with those that Mr. de Bougainville identified; but it appears that these are the Sebal- <note>Likely referring to the Sebald Islands, now known as the Jason Islands</note>

<margin>(b) Furthermore, Dom Pernetty doubted for some time whether these Islands, which he recognized, were truly the Sebald Islands <note>The Sebald Islands (or Sebald de Weert Islands) are a small group of islands at the northwestern tip of the Falklands, named after the Dutch explorer who sighted them in 1600.</note> from Frézier's Map; here is what he says about it in a note in the Preliminary Discourse of the first edition.
"We discovered three Islands about half a league long, quite elevated, and placed roughly in a triangle, as the Sebald Islands are said to be. This resemblance of position and shape made us take them for them at first; but then having discovered near them some flat Islands almost at water level, we judged that these three Islands were not the Sebalds, but Islands slightly forward from the Great Malouine <note>The French name for the Falkland Islands, derived from the sailors of Saint-Malo.</note>, and we had reason to be confirmed in this opinion. If these three Islands were indeed the Sebalds, they would be only two leagues away from the great Island, and not 7 to 8, as Frézier says. See the Map of our route along the coast. However, in the two voyages of the *Aigle* and the King's store-ship the *Etoile*, which surveyed these three islands after us while going from the Malouine Islands to the Strait of Magellan, the Captains, having found no other islands</margin>
in

# PRELIMINARY.
in the beautiful Map of the Strait of Magellan, drawn by Mr. de Vaugondy <note>Didier Robert de Vaugondy (1723–1786), a leading French geographer and hydrographer.</note>, for the understanding of the history of the Southern Lands.

Admiral Roggeveen <note>Jacob Roggeveen (1659–1729), the Dutch explorer who also discovered Easter Island.</note> appears to be one of the Navigators who, before Dom Pernetty, shed the most light on the true position of the Malouine Islands (c); he recognized that what had been taken for a vast continent was only a large Island of about two hundred leagues in

<margin>(c) His voyage was written in French by a German, embarked on his fleet, and was printed at The Hague in 1739, in 2 volumes in-duodecimo <note>A book size where the sheet is folded into twelve leaves.</note>. The fleet he commanded was destined by the Dutch East India Company for the discovery of the Southern World. Roggeveen's voyage is perhaps the most curious of all those undertaken to survey this third Continent.</margin>

->...<- one finds woods and fresh water in abundance, and whose principal port is vast enough to contain a thousand vessels (e); an Island which the best Navigators are tempted to rank alongside Plato's Atlantic Island <note>The mythical lost continent of Atlantis.</note> and the beautiful country of El Dorado <note>The legendary "City of Gold" sought by explorers in South America.</note>.

Woods Rogers, at the very beginning of this century, commanded a small squadron charged with reconnoitering the South Sea; he had as second Captain the celebrated Physician Dower, and as Pilot William Dampier, who had already immortalized himself by two voyages around the world; Rogers ran along the Northwest coast of the Malouines Islands <note>The Falkland Islands.</note> and determined their position; but he judged their extent poorly (f).

<margin>(e) This Pepys Island, according to Admiral Anson, is at forty-seven degrees South latitude; and, according to Doctor Halley, eighty leagues from Cape Blanco on the Coast of the Patagonians. Captain Cowley named it Pepys Island in honor of Samuel Pepys, Secretary to the Duke of York, who was later James II, and who at that time was Great Admiral of England. Commodore Byron, in his voyage around the World; and Mr. de Bougainville, in two voyages to the Malouines Islands, have vainly sought this Pepys Island, which was probably, for Captain Cowley, nothing but a cloud or a large ice bank.</margin>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>PRELIMINARY.</header>

<column-break/>

(f) According to the account of Admiral Anson, "Rogers ran the Northeast Coast of these Islands in 1708; he saw that they extended about the length of two degrees, that they were composed of heights which sloped gently one before the other; that the terrain was good and covered with wood; and that, according to appearances, there was no lack of good ports."

Dom Pernety <note>Antoine-Joseph Pernety (1716–1796), a Benedictine monk and naturalist who accompanied Bougainville.</note> here corrects Captain Rogers. "If this Englishman," he says, "only ran the Northeast Coast of the Malouines Islands, how can he know if these Islands only extend..."

"If one of these places was found, after this examination, to be suitable for what I propose, it is inconceivable how useful a place of refreshment could be, so far advanced towards the South, and so near Cape Horn. The *Duke* and the *Duchess* of *Bristol* <note>The author refers to the privateering ships *Duke* and *Dutchess*, commanded by Woodes Rogers, which circumnavigated the globe between 1708–1711.</note> took only thirty-five days from the time they lost sight of the *Falkland* Islands until their arrival at the Island of *Juan Fernandez* in the South Sea; and as the return from there is even easier, because of the West winds that prevail in those latitudes, I do not doubt that one could make this voyage from the *Falkland* Islands to that of *Juan Fernandez*, going and returning, in a little more than two months."

volcanic eruptions, earthquakes, and sometimes even the mere force of the sea waters are sufficient to tear the earth apart in this way, and violently separate men whom interest constantly tends to reunite.

This observation applies particularly to the Southern Continent; the Land of Fire <note>Tierra del Fuego</note> takes its name from its volcanoes: this region also—
<margin>between this last Cape and the corresponding shore of America. One sees, by the appearance of the coasts, that they were separated with violence, and the Islands, which are between the two, form a kind of chain like the Maldives. The inhabitants of America corresponding to the Eastern extremity of Asia are of small stature, tanned, and have little beard, like the Kamchadals <note>Indigenous people of the Kamchatka Peninsula</note>, etc. See the proofs of this opinion in the work of Krasheninnikov <note>Stepan Krasheninnikov, a Russian explorer of Siberia and Kamchatka</note> himself, translated in the second quarto volume of the *Journey to Siberia* by Abbot Chappe. These proofs are too strong to serve only as support for a system.</margin>

civilized men and books, there has never been an agreement on the existence of Giants; it is especially in relation to the Patagonians <note>The "Patagonians" were a mythical race of giants first reported by Magellan; the name likely derives from "Patagón," a dog-headed monster from a Spanish romance novel.</note> that this problem has long seemed insoluble to Philosophers. For a hundred years, Navigators of all Nations agreed in saying that the tip of South America produced Colossi; in the following century, Sailors saw nothing there but ordinary men; and Naturalists, from the depths of their studies, asserted today that the Patagonians, as neighbors of the Pole, ought to be nothing but pygmies.

This most curious question now appears decided by the authentic account of Commodore Byron <note>John Byron (1723–1786), a British naval officer whose 1764 voyage sparked renewed rumors of nine-foot-tall giants in Patagonia.</note>, and by those which one will read

In every century, it has been observed that, ordinarily, to be the strongest signifies being the most unjust.

Magellan, the first mariner who navigated the coasts of Patagonia, saw with his own eyes some of those giants so dreaded in the New World (m), but his artillery restrained 

<margin>...wanted to enjoy, etc. The Inca <note>Referring to the rulers of the Incan Empire</note> did not observe the proportions of his giants any better than Muhammad did those of his angel who had seventy thousand heads: but because the Peruvians exaggerated, it does not follow that they saw nothing.</margin>

(m) The account of the Knight Pigafetta <note>Antonio Pigafetta, the chronicler of Magellan's voyage</note>, who was on Magellan's vessel and who edited his Voyage, is too well-detailed for one to believe its author was either a dupe or a rogue. "A giant came to us, singing, dancing, and throwing dust upon his head. The Captain ordered the same thing to be done. These gestures reassured the savage. He came to us on a small island, showing the greatest marks of surprise at our sight:

<page-num>35</page-num>

them, and the thunder of the Europeans was enough to make the Titans of America <note>A classical reference to the giants of Greek mythology, used here to describe the tall Patagonians</note> tremble.

<margin>he raised a finger toward Heaven, meaning to say that we came from there. Our people hardly reached his waist. Magellan had him given food and drink. A mirror was presented to him; he was so terrified to see his face there that, with a leap backward, he threw four of our men to the ground. His companions appeared to be ten palms high, about seven feet; they were signaled to come to the vessels: then they made their women, of whom they appeared jealous, mount upon animals shaped like donkeys, and sent them away. Another time, six of these savages appeared on the shore, signaling that they wished to come to the vessels; which gave us great pleasure. The skiff <term>skiff: a small boat used for rowing or sailing, often carried by a larger ship</term> was sent to fetch them. They boarded the flagship, where the General had a cauldron of porridge served to them, large enough to satisfy twenty sailors. They ate it all; likewise, the smallest among them was taller than the tallest of us. As soon as they had eaten, they asked to be returned to shore. Half a century after Magellan, Drake—the first Englishman to circumnavigate the globe, and the same man who was devoured alive by crabs <note>This refers to a legendary and likely apocryphal account of Sir Francis Drake's death; historically, he died of dysentery.</note>—saw eight giants on the coast of Patagonia, beside whom the tallest Europeans appeared no taller than Lapps <note>A historical term for the Sámi people of northern Scandinavia, often used in this period as a shorthand for people of short stature.</note> (n).

<insert>
" [They have] huts made of skins, which they transport at will from one place to another. They live on raw meat and a root called *capas* in their language. The prisoner we had on board ate a full basket of biscuits in one meal and drank half a bucket of water in one gulp. They have their hair cut in a circle like monks, and their heads are bound with a cotton cord in which they tuck their arrows."

(n) At least such is the account of Argensola in his *History of the Moluccas*, book 3. This author adds that the bulk of the Patagonians' bodies did not hinder their agility; they did not run, they flew.

<header>PRELIMINARY.</header> <page-num>37</page-num>

Around the year 1592, the Knight Cavendish crossed the Strait of Magellan; he testified to having seen two Patagonian corpses on the American coast that were fourteen spans long <note>A "span" or "palm" (palme) was a unit of length; fourteen spans would suggest a height of roughly 10 to 11 feet.</note>. He measured the footprint of one of these savages on the shore, and it was found to be four times longer than one of his own. Finally, three of his sailors were nearly killed even while in the sea by chunks of rock that a giant threw at them (o): here is the Polyphemus <note>A reference to the one-eyed giant from Homer's Odyssey who threw boulders at Odysseus's ship.</note> of the West.

(o) See the account of Anthony Knivet in the collection by Purchas, volume IV, book 6. The author of *Philosophical Researches on the Americans* says, volume 2, page 295, that in the time of Cavendish, the belief in the existence of giants was universal, and that Kni... <note>The text cuts off mid-name, likely referring to Knivet.</note>

About ten Spaniards skillfully surrounded one of the savages and took him, despite his resistance; the others immediately ran for their weapons, and returned upon us so promptly, that we hardly had time to get back into the longboat... The Indian, whom our people had taken, was a Giant among the other Giants, and resembled a Cyclops <note>A one-eyed giant from Greek mythology, used here to emphasize the subject's intimidating size.</note>. His compatriots were three ells high <note>An 'ell' is an archaic unit of measurement; three ells would be roughly 11 to 12 feet tall.</note>, large and strong in proportion... A few days later, another landing was made; but the artillery frightened the Giants: they fled with such lightness, that one would have thought they went as fast as a harquebus ball." *History of the Conquest of the Moluccas* by Leonardo de Argensola, book 3. One must, however, be wary of Sarmiento, who lived in the age of Chivalry, and who had the visionary mind of Don Quixote.

Furthermore, one must be very wary of those enormous skeletons presented as human remains. Sir Hans Sloane <note>1660–1753, a physician and naturalist whose collection founded the British Museum.</note> has proved in an excellent dissertation that these alleged Giants were nothing but the remains of elephants, whales, and hippopotamuses; this is what he thinks in particular of the skeleton of forty-six cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 inches.</note> encountered, according to Pliny, in a cave in Crete, and of another of sixty cubits found, according to Strabo, in Mauritania, which the people took for the body of that Antaeus who was smothered in the arms of Hercules in heroic times—that is to say, in the time of fables.

->## PRELIMINARY.<- <page-num>57</page-num>

among us, these varieties of the human species are accidental; one never sees an entire family whose stature is colossal; and a European Giant is regarded less as an individual of a particular race than as a monster.

But what is a monster? Is it truly certain that nature herself disturbs the invariable order of her laws? Are the combinations of the elements of animality, which appear defective to us, actually so? And does the variety of forms change the essence of beings? As soon as a being breathes, as soon as it grows, as soon as it can multiply, it must not be placed outside the great scale of beings; but the "people of Philosophers" who make up the World love to disrupt it. --- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and scientific justification for the existence of a Southern Continent, following the discussion of global geography.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE</header>

Southern <note>original: "Austral"</note>, cannot be occupied solely by the Ocean; earth is almost twice as specifically heavy as water; and if there were not in the Antarctic hemisphere an unknown mass of land, which corresponded to that of the Arctic hemisphere, the rotational movement of the globe would be hindered, and our planet would lose its equilibrium.

It is difficult to believe that the Ancients did not suspect the existence of the Southern World; the Astronomers of Egypt and Babylon, the great Navigators of Phoenicia, and the Sophists <note>In this context, "Sophists" refers generally to Greek philosophers and thinkers</note> of Greece, speak constantly of the globe of the Earth, and of its distribution into five zones; they had a confused notion of a hemisphere

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>PRELIMINARY.</header>

Southern which they named *Antichthon* <note>Greek: "Antichthon" (Ἀντίχθων), meaning "Counter-Earth," a hypothetical landmass on the opposite side of the world</note>, and which they believed was separated from us by an impassable Ocean. This is roughly the basis upon which we found our reasoning regarding the equilibrium of the Earth: the philosophical spirit for the last two hundred years has added very little to the mass of ancient ideas regarding this part of Astronomy.

Thus, when the celebrated Maupertuis <note>Pierre Louis Maupertuis (1698–1759), a French mathematician who famously led an expedition to Lapland to measure the shape of the Earth</note>, in his letter to the King of Prussia on the progress of the Sciences, proposed the discovery of the Southern World, no one took his idea for a paradox; we know of the Geometer who, after having flattened the Pole <note>A reference to Maupertuis's proof that the Earth is an oblate spheroid, flattened at the poles</note>, provided a plan for piercing the core of the Earth (e),

<header>HISTORY</header>
<header>OF A VOYAGE</header>
<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header>

<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

The peace having been concluded by means of the cession that France had made of Canada to England, Monsieur de Bougainville, Knight of St. Louis and Colonel of Infantry, proposed to compensate France for this loss through the discovery of the Southern Lands <note>original: "Terres Auſtrales"; a term then used for the hypothesized "Great Southern Continent" or unknown regions of the South Pacific and Atlantic</note>, and of the islands which might be found along the route. The reading of Admiral Anson's *Voyage Around the World* fixed his ideas upon the reconnaissance of the Malouine Islands <note>The Malouine Islands are now commonly known as the Falkland Islands; the name "Malouine" was derived from the French port of Saint-Malo</note>. He shared his project with the Ministry, which approved it.

<summary>This introductory page outlines Louis-Antoine de Bougainville's proposal to explore the Southern Lands and the Falkland Islands to restore French prestige following the loss of Canada in the Seven Years' War.</summary>
<keywords>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville, Falkland Islands, Malouine Islands, exploration, Seven Years' War, Southern Lands, Admiral Anson</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page begins the narrative of the expedition's departure from France, following the preparations for the settlement of the Falkland Islands.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
->HISTORY OF A VOYAGE<-

proved. To execute it, Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville (1729–1811), a celebrated French admiral and explorer.</note> had a frigate and a corvette built at his own expense in St. Malo, under the direction of Messrs. Guyot du Clos and Chénart de la Gyraudais, who were to command them under his orders; and when he was on the point of departing, I received the King's orders, via a letter from Mr. the Duke of Choiseul, Minister of the Navy, to embark with him; such a choice could only flatter me, and I eagerly seized this opportunity to make myself useful to my country.

I left Paris on August 17, 1763. On the 25th, we went to the Port of Saint-Servant <margin>It is called *Solidor*; it is the place where ships are built.</margin> to attend the ceremony of the christening of our frigates: it was performed with all the pageantry customary in such circumstances; and the two ships, during the Mass, fired two general salvos, one for God, and another for the King.

On the first of September, our crews and our provisions were entirely embarked; and, from five o'clock in the morning, a

<page-num>77</page-num>
->TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.<-
<note>The "Isles Malouines" is the French name for the Falkland Islands, named after the mariners of St. Malo.</note>

North-West wind having risen, we left the roadstead of Solidor. The frigate *The Eagle* (*l'Aigle*), which I boarded, carried twenty cannons and contained a crew of one hundred men. She was commanded by Mr. Duclos-Guyot, of Saint-Malo, a Fireship Captain <term>Capitaine de brûlot: a naval rank originally designating a commander of a ship intended to be set on fire and steered into enemy fleets.</term>: this ship was followed by the corvette *The Sphinx* (*le Sphinx*), with a crew of forty men, mounted with eight cannons and six swivel guns <term>pierriers: small cannons used for firing stones or grapeshot at close range.</term>, and commanded by Mr. Chénart de la Gyraudais, of Saint-Malo, a Frigate Lieutenant: the small squadron was under the orders of Mr. de Bougainville.

We were only waiting for a favorable wind to set sail, when difficulties were raised at the Admiralty of Saint-Malo regarding our departure. Mr. de Bougainville immediately dispatched a courier to inform the Minister of War: this courier, who was his own servant, acted with such diligence that he was back in Saint-Malo with the response in the fifty-ninth hour after his departure. Free then from all anxiety, we took advantage of a South-South-West wind, and on the 8th of September, we set sail for the Malouine Islands.

# HISTORY OF A VOYAGE

## CHAPTER ONE.

*Sea Route until the crossing of the Equator.*

From the third day of our departure, the sea became heavy; rain and hail fell with violence; however, there was no true storm, and the ship was not damaged.

I took advantage of my leisure to attempt an experiment on a drug provided by Mr. Seguin, intended to preserve water from corruption <note>original: "corruption," referring here to stagnation or bacterial growth</note> during long-distance voyages. A chemist had given another composition to Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville (1729–1811), the famous French explorer leading this expedition</note> for the same purpose. It was a grayish paste, which seemed to be composed of clay and powdered raw antimony <term>Antimony: a brittle, silvery-white metalloid often used in historical medicinal and chemical preparations</term>. Some said it contained a mixture of Mercury. As Mr. de Bougainville only showed it to me once aboard the frigate, I did not attempt to analyze it. As for Mr. Seguin’s preparation, since I knew that spirit of salt <note>original: "l'esprit de sel," an archaic term for hydrochloric acid</note> formed its essence, and that it made the water suitable for preventing scurvy, or

<column-break/>

# TO THE MALOUINA ISLANDS. <page-num>79</page-num>

even for curing it, I did not hesitate to put it to the test: what resulted from this experiment will be seen in the following pages.

There were on the vessel, under the title of passengers, two Acadians <note>French settlers from Acadia (modern-day Canadian Maritimes) who had been deported by the British and were now seeking resettlement</note> who were on the verge of sowing discord within our small society; they refused, under the worst pretexts, to help with the ship's maneuvers; and in a moment of storm, when danger makes everyone active and industrious, one was found standing with his arms crossed on the quarterdeck <term>Quarterdeck: the part of the upper deck of a ship, usually reserved for officers and navigation</term>, calmly watching the distress of the sailors and passengers. Mr. de Bougainville could not help but reproach him for it. The Acadian withdrew below deck without answering, and having gathered his wife, his father, and two other Acadian families, he sought to pass his discontent on to them; he made them understand that they had only embarked as passengers, and not to perform maneuvers, and that it would have been better for them to have remained in France than to be constantly exposed to such vexations.

The Acadian families, whom a turbulent and factious spirit thus sought to sub-

Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville, the famous French explorer leading this expedition</note> had proposed to take them on board, to transport them to a country where he would give them lands in full ownership, and a thousand other advantages that they could not hope for in France. He had even provided them with advances in goods and money. Upon the report made to him of the speeches of the fiery Acadian, he said: "We have only to put them all back on shore and send them back to Saint-Servan; since misery pleases them, let them go live there miserably."

The Acadian and his father, informed of the intentions of the Commodore <term>Chef d'escadre: a senior naval rank equivalent to Commodore or Rear Admiral</term>, asked to return to Saint-Servan; and that very afternoon, the father, the son, and his wife were landed near Saint-Cast with everything belonging to them. Mr. de Bougainville even had the generosity to let them keep the advances of money he had obtained for them

->TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.<- <note>The Falkland Islands; named "Malouines" by the French after the sailors of Saint-Malo</note>
<page-num>81</page-num>

from the King. The two other families earnestly requested to remain on the ship: it was even noted that they were delighted to be rid of those restless and stirring spirits. The wife had a somewhat sour disposition; the husband was so jealous of her that he almost never left her side for a moment; he watched even her slightest gestures, and would have inevitably disturbed the good harmony upon which our happiness depended. This much-desired union was maintained between the two families who made the voyage with us, and whom we landed and established at the Malouine Islands. They were composed of: the first, the husband, his wife, two children (a boy aged three and a girl aged one), and the wife's two sisters (one aged twenty, the other seventeen). The second family consisted of the husband, the wife, a four-year-old boy, and the wife's sister, aged sixteen.

The woman was ready to give birth when we departed from these Islands to return to France. Never was a colony founded under better auspices.

On September 18, the waves having calmed and the wind barely rippling the surface of the sea, we landed at Agot Island <note>A small island off the coast of Brittany, near Saint-Malo</note> to kill rabbits there; but the hunters traversed the plain in vain for three hours. As for me, who could only go in search of plants, I occupied myself with botanizing <note>original: "herboriser"; the practice of collecting and studying plants</note>. Toward the hour of noon, hunger began to make itself felt; as nothing had yet been killed, we took the course of going to ask for dinner from the Prior of the Abbey of Saint Jacut: we were received with magnificence; and, after the meal, we were further given the liberty of loading our canoe with vegetables from the garden.

On Monday the 25th, we set a hook with two barbs, and hardly had it been thrown into the sea than we caught a fish weighing thirty pounds which had the shape and color of a Mackerel. Its flesh was firm like that of the Tuna; it also had the same taste. We found this fish excellent; it is a little dry, but less so than the Bonito: it is named the "Big-Ear" <note>likely a species of Kingfish or large Mackerel</note>.

The hook with which it was caught is of a particular shape. It is composed of

<column-break/>

<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header> <page-num>83</page-num>
two iron hooks the thickness of a writing quill, joined one to the other. One covers the shank of these two united hooks with tow <note>coarse flax or hemp fibers</note>, giving it the shape of a spindle: one covers this tow with a strong white cloth and a lead plate; one then adjusts two or four white feathers to it, in such a way that they are placed like extended fins. In this state, the hook roughly represents a flying fish. The end of the shank is turned into a ring, through which one passes a brass <note>original: "léton" (laiton)</note> wire a little less thick, and about two and a half feet long; the whole is thrown into the sea, attached to a cord as thick as the little finger and about six fathoms <note>original: "braffes"; a unit of depth/length roughly equal to 6 feet</note> long. This cord is attached on one side to the rear of the ship and on the other to the hook which follows the wake of the vessel.

The boredom of the voyage was charmed from time to time by the sight of some vessels. On the 26th, we perceived several from afar; but they did not approach close enough to us for us to be able to distinguish to which Nation they belonged; it was only judged that they

The next day, we discovered another which approached the *Sphinx* <note>The *Sphinx* was one of the two ships in this expedition, a frigate accompanying the *Aigle*</note>: it was of about three hundred tons burden, without a gun deck, and manned by a crew of fifty men; it was bound for Bayonne and returning from Newfoundland.

On October 1st, the sea being very heavy, we perceived a dismasted ship, and that humanity which necessity renders even more essential to sailors than to the rest of men, engaged us to go toward it, to give it all the assistance that depended on us. We spoke to it at ten o'clock. It was a Dutch merchant ship coming from Curaçao <note>original: "Curaſol"</note>, and which, having been struck by a gale one hundred leagues from the Bermudas, had been obliged to cut away its mizzenmast and its mainmast. We asked if they had need of anything; the captain replied that he had five French ladies on board whom he was taking to France; but that he could not put his boat to the sea. take charge of these ladies; but that if they had need of rigging, or other provisions, we could supply them. The interpreter repeated that they could not put the boat to the sea. It was indeed quite heavy; and not having dared to expose our own to it, we had the regret of being unable to be useful to this ship except by wishing it a happier voyage.

On October 5th, the sight of another vessel threw us into justifiable alarms. We were in the latitudes where the Saletins <note>Pirates or corsairs from the Republic of Salé, a famous corsair port in Morocco</note> sometimes make their cruises, and we knew that they had at sea a frigate named the *Bird* <note>original: "l'Oiseau"</note>, of thirty-six guns and a crew of three hundred men, which the English had sold to the Saletins. These latter had given the command of it to a Captain from Provence, a renegade <note>A Christian who had converted to Islam, often to serve with the Barbary pirates</note>, a seaman and a brave man. They also had a corvette of twelve guns and a crew of one hundred men. Consequently, the Commander of our two frigates gave his orders, so that they could act in concert in case of attack. The order of battle was designated; the cannons and weapons were in readiness; everyone took his

post that had been assigned to him, and we sailed on with confidence. It had been agreed that, if it were the Saletine frigate <note>Saletine refers to the corsairs or pirates from Salé, a famous port in Morocco known for its privateers.</note>, the *Sphinx* would fly the English flag and appear to make every effort to retreat under the enemy's cannon. We were, consequently, to fly the French flag and make a show of pursuing the *Sphinx* by firing cannon shots at her, as if to tell her to strike her colors <note>To "strike colors" means to lower the flag as a sign of surrender.</note>. When the Saletine frigate found itself between the *Sphinx* and us, the *Sphinx* was to hoist the French flag and release her full broadside; in this way, the corsairs would find themselves caught between two fires. It was hoped that, by this maneuver, we might compensate for our numbers and batter the Saletines with a vigorous combat, to the point of forcing them to surrender.

Our crews showed a cheerful and determined air. They had, in fact, great confidence in the skill and bravery of our Captains and the other Officers, with whom they had made cruises during the last war <note>Likely referring to the Seven Years' War (1756–1763).</note> and captured several English ships by boarding.

As we approached the ship we had discovered, we thought

Then we fired two cannon shots at her at different intervals and advanced upon her: finally, when she was close to us, she hoisted the English flag, and it was recognized that the Captain was from Guernsey <note>original: "Guerney"</note>, and that he had served as a coastal pilot for the English when, during the last war, they had made their landings at Cancale and Saint-Cast <note>These were sites of British amphibious assaults on the French coast in 1758.</note>. He was asked the usual questions in the French language, namely where he was from, where he was coming from, where he was going, and the name of his ship. He answered nothing. had found himself encumbered by his merchandise. It was a two-masted merchant ship coming from Lisbon and going to the Azores. For that one, we did not wish him a happy journey <note>The author likely implies the ship was a rival or perhaps a vessel of a nation currently at odds with his own.</note>.

On the 13th, we caught a *Pilot-fish* and three *Bonitos*. One can find a life-like illustration of them in *Plate I, fig. 8*. The first of these fish was only eight inches long; the others weighed at least twenty pounds each.

[The fish known by the name of *Pilot* is one of those species of *Remora* <note>The Remora, or suckerfish, was famously believed in antiquity to be able to stop ships by attaching to the hull.</note>, celebrated among the Poets of antiquity (I do not say among the Naturalists) for the property of stopping a vessel even when it sails with full canvas. As for the *Bonito*, it is a very healthy and delicate fish in the European seas. It is not the same on the coasts of Africa. Its flesh is a very dangerous food. Nevertheless, the Negroes of the Gold Coast worship the Bonito which poisons them].

Naturalists claim, no doubt based on the reports of some sailors, that the *Pilot* always precedes the *Shark*, and that it is for this reason that they have given

<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header> <page-num>89</page-num>

to this fish the name of *Pilot*, as if it directed the route of the other. I have sometimes observed one or two *Pilots* in front of or near every *Shark* that we caught; but we have often seen *Pilots* without *Sharks*, just as we have seen *Sharks* without *Pilots*.

Father Feuillée <note>Louis Feuillée (1660–1732), a French Minim friar, explorer, and botanist.</note>, on page 173, confuses the *Pilot* with the *Suckerfish* <note>original: "Succet"</note>, and makes only one fish of the two. "The *Sharks*," he says, "are accompanied by small fish, which are inseparable from them, and which would rather perish with them than abandon them; they are always placed on their bodies, at such a distance that the *Sharks* cannot catch them; which has caused them to be given the name of *Pilots*. We caught no *Shark* without having found these small fish stuck to their backs, by means of a yellowish, cartilaginous pellicle of round shape which they have on top of their head, which has an infinity of small holes filled with fibers, which serve them, according to all appearances, to draw some subsistence for their nourishment from the skin of the *Shark*."

This traveler attributes only three rows of teeth to the *Shark* <note>original: "Requin"</note>, one of which, he says, is composed of triangular teeth that are longer than the others; however, I counted seven rows of teeth, all mobile and triangular, in the mouths of all the Sharks we captured. The *Remoras* <note>original: "Succets"; these are suckerfish known for attaching to larger marine animals</note> also did not have a round sucker, but rather one of a long, rounded shape, as it appears in the Plate. [ One sees in *fig. 11* the side of the sucker located on the head. *Fig. 12* represents the fish from the belly side. The *Remora* which served as the model for this engraving was seven inches long ].

On the 14th, the corvette *Sphinx* <note>A corvette is a small, fast warship</note> caught our attention by hoisting a white flag on the foremast, which was the agreed-upon signal for a sighting of Land. Indeed, we soon recognized the Island of Palma, the northernmost and westernmost of the *Canary Islands*. It appeared to us at a distance of about fifteen or eighteen leagues, just as it is represented in the figure of the first Plate.

At the same time, we discovered another island further to the South-West, presenting roughly the shape *B*.

<column-break/>

<header>TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS. 91</header>
The sighting of these lands served to correct our positions. We recognized that we were about twenty leagues further West than our estimation, and our navigation Journal was adjusted accordingly.

However, we had noticed for a long time that the *Sphinx* was not as good a sailer as we were; its slow and measured pace had delayed our route by at least a hundred leagues. We had not wanted to separate from it sooner, so as to provide mutual assistance in case we encountered the *Saletins* <note>Pirates or corsairs from the port of Salé in Morocco, famous for raiding European shipping</note>; but as soon as we saw ourselves out of the waters where they cruise, we decided to go ahead, both to arrive sooner at the rendezvous and so that all the refreshments the corvette might need would be ready upon its arrival, ensuring our stay would not be prolonged. We gave a signal to the *Sphinx*. Immediately we set full sail, and by evening, we had already lost sight of it.

It had already been a long time since we had reached the degree of latitude where, according to all Navigators, one should find the trade winds; and, instead of them, we only...

we saw only weak and variable winds prevailing; sometimes we were even surprised by calms. Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville (1729–1811), the famous French admiral and explorer leading this expedition.</note> complained loudly about the confidence with which the Hydrographers <note>Specialists in mapping bodies of water and charting winds and currents.</note> assert that the trade winds never fail in these latitudes: he fully intended, upon his return to Paris, to submit a Memoir to the Academy of Sciences to demonstrate their non-existence. While he was forming these plans, we were halted by a tedious calm; the sky was not disturbed by the slightest cloud, nor the surface of the sea by the lightest wind: these conditions, so praised by the Poets, are the despair of Navigators.

We tried to console ourselves by fishing for Bonitos, Dolphinfish <note>original: "Dorades"; likely the Coryphaena hippurus, known for its vibrant colors.</note>, and Tuna. On the 22nd, we were presented with about a dozen flying fish which, in trying to pass over the frigate, had struck the sails and fallen into the ship. They were served for dinner, and we found them very delicate. I preserved one to paint it from life; its illustration can be found in *Plate I, figure 4.*

This fish is, in these regions, of a

<column-break/>

-># TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS<- <page-num>93</page-num>

beautiful blue on the back, which fades or lightens gradually down to the bottom of the belly, which is a silvery blue. Its two wings are two elongated fins that extend in length, in the majority of them, as far as the tail; in others, only to the middle of the body, although all fish of this species are of the same shape, size, and length: the one shown in the figure here was about ten inches from the tip of the head to that of the tail.

[ This amphibious <note>The author uses "amphibious" here in a pre-modern sense to mean an animal that exists in two environments (air and water), rather than the biological class Amphibia.</note> being, which nature seems to have so greatly advantaged by providing it the ease of living in two elements, is in itself very miserable. It counts among its enemies all birds of prey, all voracious fish, and men. It can neither fly nor swim without peril to its life; thus its species is destroyed every day, and now it is only found between the Tropics ].

known only to entertain us. They made singular leaps out of the water. Several, in these capers, jumped at least three to four feet high, and turned up to three times in the air, just as the Italian performers most skilled in acrobatics do on the fairground stages. One can judge from this what the strength of this fish is.

[ The <term>Porpoise</term><note>original: "Marsouin"</note> is placed by Naturalists in the class of Whales. However, there is quite a distance from a fish of five to six feet to those organized colossi which are two hundred feet long and which reign proudly in the icy seas. Could all beings be subject to degenerations which would subsequently form constant varieties: for example, is the <term>Persian</term> a debased <term>Patagonian</term>, and the <term>Lapp</term> a degenerate Persian? ]

There are different species of Porpoises; some have a gray back, almost black, and a belly of a much lighter gray; others are of an almost white gray, which has caused them to be given the name of **white Porpoises**. We later took one weighing a hundred pounds: it had a head

<column-break/>

# TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS
<page-num>99</page-num>

shaped not like the snout of a pig, but almost similar to the head of a bird: it was covered with a thick gray skin, and the beak was armed from one end to the other with sharp, white teeth in the shape of those of a pike. This fish had an opening on its head [ *Plate II, fig. 1* ] **A**, through which it spouted water, after which air came out which made a sound similar to the grunting of a pig: its tail was arranged horizontally, contrary to the custom of other fish, in which it is perpendicular when they are placed on their bellies. It undoubtedly serves as a point of support for the Porpoise to spring so high out of the water and give it the ease to perform its acrobatic turns in the air. For this, it is enough for it to lean more on one side of the tail than on the other while leaping. From this arrangement of the tail apparently also comes its manner of swimming, as if it were coming out of the water and plunging back into it alternately. The one of which I give the description here, [ and all those we caught resembled it ] is, I think, of the species that is called the **Monk**.

It has a blackish back and a pearl-gray belly, slightly yellowish, speckled with black spots and others of iron-gray; it has three arched and very thick fins, one on the back, and two others under the belly. These are, like the tail, covered with a membrane under which appear five white cartilages, arranged like the fingers of a hand, and articulated in **phalanges** <note>the small bones that make up the fingers and toes</note>.

The **Porpoises** <note>original: "Marfouins"</note> almost always travel in troops and swim abreast, as if they were arranged in battle order. They seem to go in search of the wind. We have noticed that they always took their route toward the side from which the wind was rising. There is perhaps no fish that has as much strength as the Porpoise, in proportion to its size. Among the number of those we **harpooned**, two or three freed themselves from the harpoon, either by tearing their backs or by breaking the harpoon itself; although the iron bar of which it was composed was as thick as

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header>
the thumb. Those we took always bent this bar, and one of them had twisted it like the start of a screw. The flesh of this fish exhales an odor so strong and so persistent that my hands, after the anatomy I performed on it, retained this smell for more than three days, even though I had washed them many times with vinegar. It is the same with that of the **Shark**.

On the 3rd of November, a Shark of medium size and weighing about one hundred and fifty pounds came to swim around the rear of the ship. It bit the **swivel-hook** <note>original: "émérillon," a large fishing hook used for heavy sea creatures</note> as soon as it was presented. When it was already lifted out of the water, it gave a jerk that released it from the hook, by means of a piece of its **jaw** which it left as a pledge. Without being astonished or discouraged by this setback, the Shark, having perceived the same piece of **lard** <note>pork fat used as bait</note> that had been offered as bait the first time, lunged at it, and devoured both the lard and the piece of its own jaw, without being caught by the hook. We put on another piece of lard; the Shark undoubtedly had a good appetite; for it returned

to seize it. But, as this fish <note>The author is referring to the shark, spelled "Reguin" in the original French, a variant of the modern "Requin."</note> is neither healthy nor appetizing food, instead of seeking to catch it, we amused ourselves for nearly an hour by letting it scent the bait. Whenever it wanted to swallow it, we quickly withdrew it from the water—an experiment we repeated at least a dozen times without the Shark ever leaping out of the water to seize its prey, despite what Naturalists say.

I also did not see it turn onto its back to swallow the bait, but only slightly onto its side. Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville (1729–1811), the famous French explorer who established the first settlement on the Falkland Islands.</note>, during this amusement, fired two musket balls at it; but, whether he missed it or the ball could not penetrate the fish's skin, the Shark was no more moved by it; it continued to prowl around the bait, and finally swallowed a second piece without being hooked. A squall <note>original: "grain" — a sudden, violent gust of wind or localized storm at sea.</note> having come up, we left the Shark to attend to the ship's maneuvers.

[ The Shark, it is said, has a mouth vast enough to devour a man whole. The Traveler Dampier <note>William Dampier (1651–1715), an English explorer and hydrographer.</note> reports that one of his Sailors, having fallen into

<column-break/>

<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header> <page-num>103</page-num>

the sea, was swallowed by one of these monsters. The crew, to avenge his death, threw a harpoon with bait to the Shark; this voracious fish swallowed it immediately, and the harpoon having caught in its entrails, they pulled it aboard; they hastened to slit its belly, and there found the unfortunate Sailor almost whole. Based on Dampier's account, people did not fail to conclude that the Shark was the monster that once swallowed the Prophet Jonah <note>A reference to the biblical story of Jonah and the whale/great fish.</note>. ]

On the 7th of November, the sun rose quite beautifully, though amidst some clouds. Before appearing, its rays darting upon these clouds presented one of the most beautiful sights in the world, through the variety and brilliance of the colors. I was very mortified not to be able to paint such a dawn, which would have made one of the most brilliant pictures. I was only able to preserve a very faint sketch of a sunset, which we had all admired for nearly half an hour; but it is not possible to present, with gum-based colors <note>A reference to gouache or watercolor using gum arabic as a binder.</note>, a painting from which one could form an exact idea of it. As the fair weather continued, we took advantage of it to dry the crew's clothing, which had been soaked during the storms of the preceding days. This dampness of the clothing is a proximate cause of scurvy <note>original: "scorbut"; a disease caused by vitamin C deficiency, though at the time often attributed to dampness or "bad air."</note> and several other diseases, much more so than the salty food given to the crews. A Captain cannot pay too much attention to maintaining cleanliness among the crew, and to airing out the hammocks, the bed-frames, etc., if he wishes to prevent illness. Our Captain had me make this observation based on his own experience in the various voyages he has made to China, the Indies, Peru, and Canada. He always paid this attention, he told me, and he attributed to it,

<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header><page-num>105</page-num>
as well as to the choice of food, the few illnesses with which his crews were afflicted during voyages of such long duration.

On the 9th, a bird roughly as large as a pigeon, but more elongated, having come to perch on the yard of the foremast <term>foremast: the forward-most mast on a sailing vessel</term>, a Sailor caught it by hand. This bird, which I painted at half its natural size, and whose figure can be seen in Plate II, fig. 2, is of a light reddish-brown, almost the color of a hazelnut. The largest feathers of the wings and the tail are of a darker brown, even a bit blackish. Its beak is black, straight, pierced through from side to side in the middle, slender with a small swelling underneath, and as long as the bird's head. The top of the head is white near the beak, and of a pearly white that grows increasingly darker until the neck, which is quite long for its size. Its feet are of a blackish-gray, webbed like those of a moorhen. I put it in a small cabinet in my poop deck cabin <term>poop deck: the highest deck at the aft/rear of a ship</term>, where I found it the next day very much alive, and so little frightened at being caught that, having placed it on my table, it settled there in the posture in which I

I painted it. I offered it food; it ate, remaining crouched, and stayed in this position for three days, which gave me plenty of time to paint it from life. Some of our sailors said it was a *Booby* <note>original: "Fou"; a seabird known for its perceived lack of fear</note>; but it did not have, like the bird of that name, the narrow beak of a Duck, or the hooked beak of a Parrot.

[ It is probable that the name *Booby* <note>original: "Fou," meaning "madman" or "fool"</note> given to all these species of birds comes from the fact that they have the folly of perching on the yards of the ships they encounter, and allowing themselves to be caught by hand without offering any resistance. This name seems more appropriate to me than that of *Sea Pirates*, which some *Naturalists* have given them ].

On Thursday, November 10th, at about five o'clock in the morning, we crossed the line <note>The Equator</note>.

### CHAPTER II.

### Baptism of the Line.

THE ancients, who had no compass, who never strayed from the coasts during their longest journeys, and who rarely navigated beneath the Tropics, did not know the bizarre ceremony that I am about to describe. It is a custom that dates back no further than that famous voyage of Gama <note>Vasco da Gama, the Portuguese explorer</note>, which provided Camoëns with the subject of the *Lusiad* <note>An epic poem celebrating Portuguese discovery</note>. The idea that one cannot be a good sailor without having crossed the equator, the boredom inseparable from a long navigation, a certain republican spirit that reigns in all small societies—perhaps all these causes combined gave birth to these sorts of Saturnalia <note>Ancient Roman festivals characterized by role reversal and wild revelry</note>; be that as it may, they were adopted in an instant by all Nations, and the most enlightened men were forced to submit to a custom whose ab—

absurdity; for everywhere, as soon as the common people speak, the wise man must put himself in harmony with them.

I am going to describe this ceremony with simplicity; this takes nothing away from the merit of the account.

It is the Masters, the Boatswains, and the Sailors, who have already crossed the Line <note>The Equator</note>, who baptize all new Navigators without distinction of rank, sex, or quality. They appoint a President for the ceremony, and they call him "Old Man Equator" <note>original: "le bonhomme la Ligne"</note>.

It was nearly seven o'clock, and we were at supper, when we heard the cracking of a whip which announced to us the arrival of the messenger of Old Man Equator; this messenger was the head Boatman, and he had been dressed very neatly. He knocked at the chamber door; someone asked who was knocking? "It is," he replied, "an envoy from Old Man Equator, Lord and President of these waters." "Let him in," said Mr. de Bougainville. The door was opened, the Envoy dismounted and entered, while his mount remained at the door. This mount was formed of two Sailors tied

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header>

to one another and walking on all fours. One had a swab (a) on his head to represent the tail of the animal; the other also had one to form its mane, and furthermore a cardboard mask shaped like a horse's head. The harness was the waist-cloth of the large boat—that is to say, a large strip of blue fabric, decorated with yellow fleurs-de-lis.

The envoy, having been introduced, addressed our Commander in these terms: "The President of these Waters, Old Man Equator, having learned that the brave Chevalier de Bougainville, Commander of the frigate *The Eagle*, has arrived here, has ordered me to come and greet him on his behalf, to testify to the joy he feels at his arrival, and to deliver to him a letter, in which his sentiments are faithfully expressed."

Mr. de Bougainville read the letter, which was conceived in these terms: "Brave Chevalier, your high deeds have made the French name—"

It was first taken for an artificial bird. It showed by pinching with its beak that it was full of life. It was indeed a water bird, & the surprise was only the greater for it.

After supper, we went up to the quarterdeck <note>original: "gaillard d'arriere"</note>, where we danced to the sound of the tambourine; then to the sound of two violins, we danced minuets, country dances, etc. until nearly ten o'clock when we retired.

The day of the festival was celebrated with solemnity; they began by arranging a tub full of seawater and buckets on the quarterdeck <note>original: "gaillard d'arriere," the raised deck at the stern of the ship</note>; then, on both sides of the vessel, they stretched one of those ropes used for casting the lead, which is called the *line* <note>The "line" refers to the Equator</note>. I have descended from my Palace expressly to receive you into my society. I hope that you will find no difficulty in submitting to the ceremony of baptism, customary in these latitudes. The Vicar, assuming a grave air and with his book in hand, approached Mr. de Bougainville. He was accompanied by a Cabin Boy <term>Mousse: a young apprentice sailor</term> who carried a plate covered with a folded napkin to receive the tribute they call the "ransom" <note>rachat: a payment made to avoid the physical ordeal of the ceremony</note>; for they are content to pour a little seawater on the heads of those who ransom themselves, instead of plunging them into the sea, as is

<column-break/>

<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS. 117</header>
done when giving the "keel-hauling" (a). Furthermore,

<margin>(a) Keel-hauling <term>la cale</term> is a punishment inflicted on those of the crew who are convicted of having stolen, blasphemed, or incited some revolt. There are two kinds of keel-hauling: the ordinary and the dry. Ordinary keel-hauling consists of leading the criminal beneath the main yardarm. There, a stick is passed between his legs upon which he is made to sit, for support. He embraces a rope attached to this stick, which passes through a pulley suspended from one of the ends of the yardarm. Three or four Sailors hoist this rope as quickly as they can, until they have raised the patient to the height of the yardarm. They then suddenly release the rope, which plunges the criminal into the sea. Sometimes, to increase the penalty by increasing the speed of the fall, a cannonball is attached to his feet. This torture is often repeated up to five times.
It is called "dry" keel-hauling when the criminal is suspended by a shortened rope so that, in his fall, he only descends to the surface of the water and is not plunged into the sea. It is a kind of strappado <note>estrapade: a form of torture where the victim is dropped from a height and jerked to a stop by a rope</note>. This punishment is made public by a cannon shot, to warn everyone in the Squadron to be spectators.
The Dutch practice another keel-hauling, which they call the "great keel-haul." To administer it, the culprit is led to the edge of the ship, and a rope is tied around the middle of his body. One end of this rope is attached to the side of the vessel, or to the end of the lowered yardarm; the other end passes under the keel, and is held on the other side of the ship by some of the strongest Sailors. Something heavy is placed around the body or at the feet of the criminal to make him sink deeper into the water.
The culprit being thrown into the sea, at the order given...</margin>

When they reached a Midshipman <term>Garde-Marine: a young naval officer in training</term>, who was a bit of a rogue and hated by everyone, the Vicar told him that the President ordered him to be received with all the customary ceremonies. Consequently, he placed a piece of his stole on his head, mumbled a few words, and then made him kiss this oil-painted stole. They untied him from the line and made him sit on a stick placed crosswise over the tub. fitted a noose into the tub; so that when the Initiate <term>Cathécumene: literally 'catechumen,' a person receiving religious instruction; here used for a sailor being 'baptized' at the Equator</term> fell, he found himself caught by the middle of his body and secured without being able to free himself. They took advantage of his situation to smear his face with black and red. They poured at least five or six buckets of water over his head, then let him go.

They then came to two Acadian young ladies <note>Refugees from Acadia (modern-day Nova Scotia) who were being resettled in the Falklands.</note>, and the Vicar asked them naively if they were virgins? They replied, yes. "Do you promise," he added, "not to fail in your marital faith, if you should marry a sailor?" The promise being made, he baptized her much as he had us. The sister of this young lady had hidden herself to avoid being exposed to this ceremony. She was found, and they wanted to force her to come receive the baptism; but the Vicar, warned that there were reasons <note>Likely a polite euphemism for pregnancy or illness.</note> why she should not expose herself to the water baptism, told her he would be satisfied with placing beauty marks <note>mouches: small black decorative patches fashionable in the 18th century.</note> on her face. She presented herself, and he kept his word. Two married women were not baptized because their children—

young children, whom they could not abandon, shrieked in fear inspired by the grotesque figures of the followers of the **President of the Line**. <note>The "President of the Line" refers to a sailor dressed as a mythical figure, often Neptune or a similar character, who presided over the crossing ceremony.</note>

Some passengers were then baptized and smeared with black and red paint, but they were not made to sit upon the tub <note>original: "bagne"; here referring to the water-tub or "bath" used in the initiation</note>, because having begun to throw several buckets of water on those being baptized, the latter threw some back at the sailors to have their revenge. Those who had been soaked wanted to soak the others; the disorder increased, and everyone found on the quarterdeck was as drenched as if they had fallen into the sea. Thus this farce usually ends, and one is still quite fortunate when it is settled with just money and a few buckets of water.

Baptism on ships crossing the Line is a custom among all the nations of Europe; but there is no uniformity in the ceremonies. Each nation devises them according to its own spirit and character, and the festival becomes more or less gay, depending on the greater

<column-break/>

->TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS<- <page-num>123</page-num>

or lesser wit of those who preside over it. Sometimes the one who baptizes gives the **Catechumen** <note>A term for someone receiving religious instruction; used here mockingly for the sailor being initiated.</note> the name of a city, a cape, or a sea, and they try to match this name in a way that expresses the character, humor, appearance, or inclination of the person being baptized. This ceremony is called the **Baptism** or the **Ransom**: the Baptism, because of the water with which those crossing the Line for the first time are flooded; the Ransom, because of the tribute paid by those who do not wish to be flooded. This tribute is usually voluntary on the part of the one who pays. Sometimes it is the pranksters themselves who impose it, while nevertheless maintaining a proportion suitable to the means of the taxpayers.

When the ship's route does not cross the Line, but only the **Tropic**, the sailors—not wishing to lose their tribute—pretend that the Tropic is the eldest son of **Old Man Line** <note>original: "Bon-homme la Ligne"</note> and the heir presumptive to his rights. Consequently, at the passage of the Tropic, they perform the same farce as others do under the Equator. They have even devised a way to make...

<header>CHAPTER III</header>
<header>Voyage from the Equator to the coasts of Brazil.</header>

AFTER having crossed the Line <note>The Equator</note>, we encountered several of those birds called *Frigates* <note>Frigatebirds, known for their immense wingspan and aerial agility</note>; they are commonly found four hundred leagues from land, and yet it is claimed that they cannot rest upon the water without perishing. Their legs are short, thick, and stout. Their feet are not webbed, but armed with very sharp claws. One sees Frigates that have a nine-foot wingspan when their wings are extended. By means of the size of these deployed wings, this bird easily supports itself in the air and glides there. It sometimes rises so high that even the sharpest eye loses sight of it. When it approaches ships, it flutters around the weather vanes, moving away and returning many times, but without ever landing. Its size is roughly that of a hen. Its gaze is piercing and

assured. It swoops upon its prey with incredible speed. The males have a red and warty membrane <note>The gular sac, which the male frigatebird inflates during courtship.</note> that descends from the beak to about the middle of the neck. The feathers of the belly are of a grayish white; those of the back and wings are brown. It lives on flying fish, which it seizes skillfully while skimming the surface of the sea when they fly to avoid being the prey of Bonitos <note>A type of predatory fish related to tuna.</note>. It is said that it also pursues Gulls <note>original: "Goëlans"</note> and other seabirds to make them disgorge the fish they have swallowed, so that it may seize them for itself (a).

(a) <margin>I do not know exactly why this bird was named a "frigate," unless it be by comparison of the speed of its flight with the lightness of the ships that bear the same name, and which are usually better sailors than others.</margin>

Having been unable to see them closer than the top of the mast, I can only give a description based on those who have seen and touched them. Father Labat (*New Voyages*, *Volume VI, page 395*), adds to what I have said that these birds have large black eyes. It rarely descends to the ground and stays perched, because the size of its wings and the space it needs to set them in motion would give it too much difficulty in rising from the earth. He says that the feathers of the back and wings of this bird are black, thick, and strong; those covering the stomach and thighs are more delicate and

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header>

less black: the one I described is perhaps the female or a young one. I killed, he adds, some of them on the island where we were, to obtain their fat.... It is said that this fat is admirable for the pains of sciatic gout <note>A historical term for sciatica or nerve pain in the hip and leg.</note>, for numbness of the limbs, and other ailments that occur from humors <note>In early modern medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids believed to control health; "non-circulating humors" refers to congestion or stagnation.</note> that do not circulate. One must heat the fat; and while it is on the fire, perform strong rubbing on the afflicted part in order to open the pores, and mix good brandy or spirits of wine with this fat at the moment one wishes to apply it.

One can place a piece of blotting paper soaked in this mixture upon the diseased part, with compresses and a bandage to keep them in place.

Our stay in the seas of the Torrid Zone <note>The tropics, the area of the Earth between the Tropic of Cancer and the Tropic of Capricorn.</note> has given rise to many doubts in me regarding the accounts of common Navigators; here, with the greatest exactitude, are the phenomena of which I was a witness.

The month of November appeared to us in these regions to have the same temperature as the month of May in France; the mornings and evenings were cool, and we suffered none of those burning heats of which so many Travelers complain in their accounts. It is true that since we passed the Equinoctial Line <note>The Equator.</note>, we were not

The cleanliness, or the good condition of our frigate <note>A fast, medium-sized warship</note>, also protected us from insects; there were no sick men among the crew. To contribute to maintaining health, every evening after supper, the sailors were made to dance on the quarterdeck <note>The rear part of the upper deck, usually reserved for officers but here used for recreation</note>. This is the ordinary custom when the weather does not require any maneuvering; they then play some game that provides exercise and fosters cheerfulness. Some, quite comical by nature, dress up and mask themselves in the most grotesque figures, and present themselves successively, or enter in groups onto the quarterdeck, where they dance minuets, country dances, German and English gavottes, and sailor’s jigs <note>original: "matelotes"</note>. Most of our men had learned these dances while they were prisoners of war in the ports of Great Britain. The greatest number had escaped at the peril of their lives in neutral vessels, in fishing boats, and even in small canoes that they had taken from the ports. Several assured me that the

<column-break/>

# TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS

<page-num>129</page-num>

the English favored these escapes, sometimes by negotiating for the passage of these prisoners with the captains of neutral ships; sometimes by selling them their boats. Some lent clothes for disguise; others advanced money; others gave it out of charity; finally, there were those who entrusted them with letters of recommendation for their friends in London, or for those in the ports where they thought these prisoners could embark with less risk. They did even more; to facilitate a certain well-being in the prisons where they were held, they encouraged them with liberalities, paid very generously for the small hand-crafted works that some of these prisoners made, even buying from them at a high price small figures of the Virgin and Saints, made of wood and sculpted as poorly as one can do with a knife or a penknife when one is not an artist. Such beautiful traits must not be buried in oblivion: it is the triumph of beneficence: they are less to the glory of the English than to that of human nature.

<page-num>130</page-num>

<header>HISTORY OF A VOYAGE</header>

Cheerfulness and cleanliness are things to which Captains should pay a great deal of attention. They contribute in no small way to preventing all the diseases that usually afflict sailors.

It is worth observing that the water we took on board at Saint-Malo <note>A major port city in Brittany, France</note> has not suffered the slightest alteration, as usually happens between the two Tropics. The biscuit was also very well preserved. Only some pickled cabbage and a few small barrels of veal stews <note>original: "daubes de veaux"; a traditional French method of slow-cooking meat, here preserved for the long journey</note> were slightly spoiled; this should likely be attributed to a defect in preparation rather than to the climate of the Tropics.

On November 20, we noticed a change in the color of the sea water; we then decided to cast the lead <note>term: sounding; the act of measuring the depth of the water by dropping a lead weight attached to a line</note>: a precaution all the more necessary in these waters, as one can hardly rely on the charts. The Dutch charts place the coasts of Brazil nearly sixty leagues further East than the French charts. Moreover, according to our estimates, we found ourselves very

<meta>catchword: near</meta>

---

<page-num>131</page-num>

<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header>

near the banks of rocks and gravel named the *Abrolhos* <note>A dangerous archipelago and reef system off the coast of Brazil; the name is said to derive from the Portuguese "Abra os olhos," meaning "Open your eyes"</note>, the length, width, and position of which are not known exactly enough, nor determined on the charts, for one to trust them. We therefore cast the lead at around half-past seven in the evening, and we let out one hundred and thirty-five fathoms <note>original: "brasses"; a fathom is a maritime unit of depth equal to about six feet</note> of line without finding the bottom.

A moment later, one of those birds, of which I have given a figure in *Plate II, fig. 2*, and which I believe to be one of those called *Tropicbirds*, circled the ship, and having landed on the forecastle <note>The forward part of the upper deck</note>, a sailor caught it by hand. It was shut in a cage, with the intention of tying a ribbon to its neck the next day, on which we planned to write: *I was caught on the French frigate l’Aigle on November 20, 1763, at latitude 16 deg. 44 min. longitude 35 = 10*, and set free on the morning of the 21st. But an inattentive observer, having taken it out of its cage, let it escape us.

On the 21st, we thought we saw the corvette *Le Sphinx*; it was a ship that seemed to be coming toward us and steering West-
