### CHAPTER IV.

#### *Landing at the Island of Santa Catarina.*

In the bay where we anchored, there are three forts that defend its entrance; the first is located on the tip of the Island and is named the *Fort of the Large Point* <note>original: "Fort de la Grosse Pointe"</note>; opposite is the second, and it is called the *Fort of the Island of Santa Cruz* <note>original: "Fort de l'Isle Sainte-Croix"</note>. Its appearance is very impressive, because it is built in terraces supported by arches: it is there that the Commander resides. The third fort is further toward the City side. It is given the name *Fort of Raton Island* <note>original: "Fort de l'Isle Ratonne"</note>. The plans of these forts can be seen in *Plate IV* <note>This refers to an engraved map or illustration included in the original printed edition of this travelogue</note>. We were anchored between the three; and the Commander signaled to us that this was the best anchorage: he had his own reasons; for the anchorage a little further toward the mainland is much more convenient.

Upon entering the bay, we perceived the Portuguese flag on a hei-

[gover]nor of the Island. He was withdrawn after we had anchored and saluted the fort of Santa Cruz <note>original: "Sainte-Croix"</note>.

At that same moment, we launched our boat to take Mr. Alexandre Guyot, our second Captain, who speaks the Portuguese language, to the fort to greet the Commander and ask if he would return our cannon salute shot-for-shot <note>A "coup pour coup" salute was a matter of naval etiquette, ensuring the local fort acknowledged the visiting ship as an equal by firing the same number of honorary shots</note>. Upon Mr. Guyot's return, the Commander had an officer from the garrison accompany him to return the courtesy and see who we were. As soon as he was aboard, we anchored and saluted the fort with nine cannon shots, which were returned to us in the same number.

The next day, our same deputy was charged again with going to greet the Governor of Santa Catarina <note>original: "Sainte-Catherine"</note>, who resides in a small town located at the bottom of a cove, five leagues <note>approximately 15 miles or 24 kilometers</note> south of our anchorage. He was also to ask him for permission to take on water and wood.

This Governor was Don Antonio Francisco de Cardoso y Menezes y Souza, Colonel, Knight of Christ <note>A member of the Military Order of Christ, a prestigious Portuguese order of knighthood</note>, and of a very illustrious family of Portugal. He gave

The Commander of the fort of Santa Cruz, having noticed this, sent an express prohibition to all the huts <gloss>(*b*)</gloss> against carrying anything whatsoever to our frigate, or even approaching it. <gloss>(*a*) This is a type of boat made from a single hollowed-out tree, which the Indigenous peoples <note>original: "Sauvages"</note> of South America are accustomed to using. Boards are added to the back of the larger ones to raise the sides. Sometimes figures of Indigenous people or grotesque shapes are painted on them. I have seen up to twelve men in one of these canoes. It is said there are large ones that carry up to fifty people, along with their war and food supplies.</gloss>

They are usually covered with canes and banana leaves, or other large leaves from a type of cane or reed. One does not commonly see a chimney there. The enslaved Black women <note>original: "Négresses Esclaves"</note> prepare the dishes over a fire lit in the middle of their living space, and they live without concern in the midst of the smoke.</gloss>

No doubt from the moment of our arrival, he had dispatched a pirogue <note>A dugout canoe or small boat</note> to the Governor to give him notice; for the next morning, the Oidor <term>Chief of Justice; from the Spanish 'oír', meaning 'to hear'</term>, arrived aboard our frigate to make an official report of our anchorage, the quality of our ship, & the motives that had brought us. Mr. de Bougainville satisfied him on all these points, & he departed around noon. We saluted him, upon his departure, with seven cannon shots, which the fort of Santa Cruz <note>original: "Sainte-Croix"</note> returned to us immediately.

After dinner, Mr. de Bougainville, accompanied by Messrs. de Nerville, de Belcourt & Lhuillier de la Serre, went to see the Commander of this latter Fort. They found there a General Officer from Rio de Janeiro <note>original: "Rio-Janeïro"</note>, held prisoner for four years for not having punctually executed the orders he had received from the Court of Lisbon regarding the expulsion

<column-break/>

of the Jesuits from Brazil. This Prisoner had as a Secretary a Portuguese man, a man of wit, who had been Page to a Portuguese Ambassador in France, & had lived four years in Paris. He was charmed to see Frenchmen again, & took pleasure in serving as an interpreter <term>truchement: an old term for a translator or intermediary</term> for Mr. de Bougainville. In the account he gave us of the causes of the Prisoner’s detention, he exonerated him as much as he could, & told us that he was indeed guilty of not having immediately executed the orders of his Court; but that the Archbishop, who favored the Jesuits, had prevented him, asserting that he had received contrary orders on his side. Whether out of respect for the Archbishop, or for other motives unknown to me, the Commander delayed too long in the execution, & he was punished for it by the loss of his liberty.

Almost all the officers of the garrison had come down to the seashore to meet us. They welcomed us as we stepped out of our boat with all imaginable politeness, and accompanied us to the Government House.

The Governor came to receive us at the outer gate and introduced us into a large hall, where we found the cloth laid and the table served. Besides himself and his son *(a)*, the Major of the Place, the <term>Oïdor: A high-ranking judge or administrative official</term>, two other officers, and a Franciscan Father dined with us. Many other officers of the garrison remained standing, and some served us.

---

*(a)* This son was a Captain in his father's regiment, and one of fifteen children he told us he had fathered—not by his legitimate wife, for he has never been married, but by one or several mistresses. His other living children are in Lisbon, where they have, according to the laws, the same honors and the same prerogatives as noble and legitimate children. Bastards there are said to be gentlemen by birth. One of the daughters of this Governor married one of the Ministers of the Court of Portugal, and another of his sons holds one of the primary positions in the government there.

<column-break/>

These officers play this subordinate role to curry favor with the Governor; he invites them in turn to eat with him, and they serve one another.

The dishes were prepared in the style of the country, but rather poorly for French tastes. The bread, above all, seemed very bad to us; the surface was only slightly dried out and had hardly felt the heat of the fire. The interior was not aerated <note>original: "œilleté"; literally "eyed," referring to the holes in well-risen bread</note> and resembled that solidified buckwheat porridge that makes up the diet of many inhabitants of Limousin, and which is called <term>galette: a thick, flat cake or pancake, often made from buckwheat</term>.

The side dishes <note>original: "entremets"</note> consisted of many plates, all prepared with sugar. They put it in almost all the sauces, as well as safflower, or "bastard saffron" flowers. The plates were made of pewter and of an antique shape. The cutlery was of a very old fashion, but made of silver and very heavy, as were the dishes and some drinking vessels, which had the shape of an octagonal cylinder, seven to eight inches high. We found nothing good in this meal except the wine; it was Port.

I proposed to strike up a conversation in Latin <note>Latin was the common academic and liturgical language of the era, used here as a potential middle ground between a Frenchman and a Portuguese monk.</note> with the Franciscan; but he

While coffee was being poured, a dozen Officers of the Garrison entered, and we were treated to a small concert of instrumental music. They had almost all the pieces by our best French Musicians, and they appeared to perform them as well as Corelli <note>Arcangelo Corelli, a famous Italian violinist and composer</note> or Gavinies <note>original: "Gaviniez"; referring to Pierre Gavinies, a celebrated 18th-century French violinist</note>.

We then walked through the Town, which seemed to me to be composed of about one hundred and fifty houses, all having only a ground floor; the garrison occupies a part of it; it is inhabited partly by Whites, and partly by Blacks or Mulattoes <note>A historical term used here to describe people of mixed European and African descent</note>; for the rest, one sees on the Island of Santa Catarina men of all sorts of shades, from black to white. Mulattoes are the greatest in number of both sexes, and are generally of an unattractive appearance and a wild air, as if they came from a mixture of Brazilians and Black women.

<column-break/>

# 141 TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS. #

They almost all go barefoot, bare-headed, and with very poorly combed hair; their clothing consists of a shirt, breeches, and sometimes a cloak that they throw over the shoulder in the Spanish fashion. Those who are more well-to-do have a very high-crowned hat on their heads, with brims about ten inches high, turned down horizontally. These men are shod, and wear a vest and an ample cloak that descends to their feet, the corners of which they sometimes tuck up over the opposite shoulder; instead of a hat, some have a hood of the same fabric as the cloak, and to which it is attached, to cover the head; this singular dress prevents even their friends from recognizing them.

The Governor, the Officers, and the Garrison are dressed in cloth, and in the French style. I was even very surprised to see, in such a hot country, Officers dressed in a cloth at least as coarse as that of our soldiers.

The <term>Oïdor: A high-ranking judge or administrative official in the Portuguese colonial system</term> and the Officers of justice are distinguished by a large cane or by

The Enslaved people go almost naked; most are covered only by a simple <term>pagne<gloss>a wrap or loincloth</gloss></term> around the shoulders. It is rare to see any who have a shirt and a vest. But, when they have received their freedom, they may wear the coat and the cloth cloak, like the Whites. The Enslaved Black women are naked except for a strip of cloth, which covers them from the waist to above the knees; those who are free act like other women, dressed in a skirt, and a shirt of which the top is open at the front, much like our men's shirts: when they leave the house, they put on a large <term>pagne</term>; this is a piece of fine wool fabric, most often white, and bordered with a ribbon of gold, silver, silk, or thread, according to the status and means of the person. This piece of fabric is about two <term>ells<gloss>a historical unit of measurement; in France, an "aune" was roughly 1.2 meters</gloss></term> long by one wide. It is adjusted in such a way

<meta>catchword: that a</meta>

---

# TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS. 143 #

that one of the angles is in the middle of the back, and produces an effect much like that of the <term>hood<gloss>original: "coqueluchon"</gloss></term> of the Great Carmelites <note>The Great Carmelites were a branch of the Carmelite religious order; their traditional habit included a distinctive pointed hood</note>. The opposite angle is draped over the head; the two others, after covering the shoulders and arms down to the elbow, come to cross over the chest, like the <term>mantelet<gloss>a short, cape-like garment</gloss></term> of our French Ladies. Sometimes also, instead of crossing them over the chest, they pass these ends under the arm that is covered by them, and reveal their throat. This manner of dressing is very inconvenient, because the wrap is disarranged by the slightest movement of the body.

The Portuguese women established, or born on the Island of Santa Catarina, and on the coasts of the Mainland that we have traveled, have very white skin, despite the heat of the climate. They commonly have large, well-shaped eyes, but little color in their faces. The inhabitants generally live, both men and women, in great idleness, and leave to their slaves the care of the household, and the little work that is done in the country. The earth produces for them almost everything necessary for life, without them

In the Town, one sees almost no merchant shops. I only noticed that of a <term>Locksmith<gloss>original: "Serrurier"</gloss></term> and another of an <term>Apothecary<gloss>original: "Apothicaire"; a medical professional who formulated and dispensed remedies</gloss></term>. The Commandant had pointed out a place to us near the fort of <note>The Fortress of Santa Cruz de Anhatomirim was a primary defense for the island of Santa Catarina.</note> Sainte-Croix to gather water and wood. The crew was sent there to proceed with this operation; but, after several attempts, many difficulties were found. Furthermore, a small whale, which had been stranded near the fort for some time, exhaled such a foul odor that we decided to ask the governor for permission to take on water elsewhere on the Island. He granted it very graciously, as well as permission to fish, to hunt, and to walk wherever we wished. One inhabitant showed us near his hut a small stream in which <note>George Anson (1697–1762) was a British Admiral famous for his circumnavigation of the globe; he stopped at Santa Catarina in 1740.</note> Admiral Anson had taken his water, and an oven built a few steps from there seven or eight years ago by some Frenchmen whom he had seen there during a layover. The water was very good: we provided ourselves with it abundantly. As for the wood, after

having it cut on the slope of the mountain, we slid it down to the edge of the sea, and we loaded it into our longboats. Almost all that we cut was cedar, sassafras, cinnamon tree, and that <term>Brazilwood<gloss>original: "bois de Brésil"; a tropical timber highly valued in Europe for producing red dye</gloss></term> which is used for dyeing.

On the 4th, Monsieur de Bougainville, with several officers, went to dine a second time with the Governor, and was treated splendidly there; the bad weather also compelled them to take supper there. After the meal, there was a great ball, for which they had the gallantry to make preparations without informing them. Our Officers, arriving at the assembly room, were pleasantly surprised to find several Ladies there, by whom they were perfectly well received. They had not imagined, based on the reputation the Portuguese have for being extremely susceptible to jealousy, that they would have allowed the Ladies to be present in such gatherings. A sort of ball was therefore held, where the Ladies performed just like the Gentlemen: and we retired around two or three o hours after midnight, very pleased with one another.

In this meeting, M. de Bougainville obtained from the Governor general permission to make all the arrangements he deemed suitable, whether for hunting and fishing, or for taking on water and wood wherever we wished. Our Commander then invited him to come to dinner on board with the <term>Oidor<gloss>original: "l'Oidor"; a judge or royal magistrate in the Spanish and Portuguese colonial systems</gloss></term> and whichever Officers he wished to bring with him.

As a result of this permission, we sent out fishing parties almost every day, and our boat always returned laden with fish of many species, and in great enough quantities to distribute them to the entire crew. The appearance of some of these can be seen in *Plate II. fig. 4, 7, & 8.*

We also went hunting every day, either on the <term>Mainland<gloss>original: "Terre-ferme"</gloss></term> or on the Island. We soon abandoned the former, because we found almost nothing there but Parrots, Toucans, and some Turtledoves. On the Island, besides these birds, <term>Sea-larks<gloss>likely sandpipers or other small shorebirds</gloss></term>, Plovers, and Snipes were found in abundance. perils to be faced from ferocious beasts and snakes, prevented the Officers of the garrison and the inhabitants of the Country from going hunting, and they exhorted us to imitate them.

Less fearful than they, and much better armed, we penetrated more than once into the most inaccessible parts of the Island. We never went alone, but always two or three together, in order to help one another in case of an attack from some monstrous snake or some ferocious beast; we especially apprehended the <term>Onces<gloss>original: "Onces"; a term used by early explorers for the jaguar (Panthera onca)</gloss></term>, some of whose claws we had been shown encased in silver, and which the inhabitants told us were very common, and more cruel than even the <note>Early European travelers often referred to any large, predatory feline as a "Tiger," though they were actually encountering jaguars or pumas.</note> Tigers themselves.

One day when we had gone to hunt on the Island, and had split into different bands, I followed, with M. de Belcourt and his servant, the edge of a cove that reaches deep into the land, which we called the River. While still coasting along the edge, I perceived on the sand the fresh tracks of a four-footed animal, which appeared to me

It must be, M. de Belcourt told me, that these tracks belong to an animal that I only caught a glimpse of at the moment it was disappearing into the brush. It is tall on its legs like the largest Great Dane <note>original: "chien Danois"</note>, and of a grayish color.

The heat at the time was stifling. We made a halt, sitting on the ends of branches with our backs leaning against a tree. We were dazed by the whistling of the snakes that surrounded us, and we were forced to always keep our bare sabers in hand to defend ourselves. We saw several as thick as the lower part of a leg; there were others that were smaller. Some were the color of the dawn <note>original: "aurore"; a bright golden-yellow or orange-pink hue</note>; others were red and yellow, and some were gray, looking much like large grass-snakes <note>original: "couleuvres"</note>. But these reptiles, far from attacking us, fled before us.

M. de Bougainville then invited them to spend the night on the ship. Being very well-acquainted with Brazil, which he was then traveling through to make his regular visitation <note>Likely a religious or administrative tour of inspection</note>, he was kind enough to answer all my questions, and gave me all the explanations about the country and its inhabitants that I will detail in the remainder of this work <note>original: "ou-vrage"</note>.

As our destination was for a country where our sailors had not yet been, and whose seas and climate are known to be stormy; before departing from Santa Catarina, our Captain judged it appropriate to provide himself with small <term>topmasts<gloss>the second section of a mast above the lower mast</gloss></term> and <term>topgallant masts<gloss>the mast section above the topmast, used for smaller sails</gloss></term>, to serve as "winter poles" <note>original: "bâtons d’hyver"; these were shorter, sturdier masts used to replace taller ones during the rough weather of the southern winter</note>. For this purpose, he addressed the freed Black man whom I have mentioned more than once. He rendered us every service that depended on him with the best grace in the world, and even had a proposal made to M. de Bougainville to take him along, promising to go wherever we wished to lead him. He was strong and worked hard; and M. de Bougainville would have willingly acquiesced to his request, had he not feared that the Portuguese would have complained that we had put in at their port to entice away their Black servants, and that such a rumor might have harmed French ships that might subsequently put in at Santa Catarina.

the forest for the trees he believed most suitable for our Captain's design. When he had found them, he alerted him and led us there through the thickets and brushwood. We cut the trees and transported them to the seashore: fortunately, we found only three large snakes in the forest, which we killed.

When all our provisions were made, we left the Island of Santa Catarina; it was the 14th of December that we departed, much more satisfied to have put in there than Admiral Anson <note>George Anson, a British Admiral whose 1740 visit to the island was marked by disease and poor relations with the Portuguese governor</note> had been. It seems to me that this island would become an excellent settlement if one took the trouble to clear it; for, except for the small town where the Governor resides, there are only a few small huts scattered along the coast, and the entire island appears to be but one vast forest.

### CHAPTER V.

#### *Natural History of Santa Catarina Island, & of the Coast of Brazil.*

[Santa Catarina Island is far from being an enchanted residence like that Island of Tinian mentioned by Admiral Anson <note>George Anson (1697–1762), a British Admiral whose account of his voyage around the world was a bestseller in the 18th century. Tinian is an island in the Northern Mariana Islands.</note>. Lions, Panthers, and Tigers <note>The author likely refers to pumas and jaguars, as true lions and panthers are not native to the Americas.</note> dominate its vast forests. The air there is unhealthy; the men live in a singular state of inertia despite themselves, and nature there possesses activity only to devour its inhabitants].

The unhealthy air of this climate is likely the cause of the paleness of the White people who reside there. From these woods, where the sun never penetrates, rise coarse vapors that form eternal mists upon the tops of the mountains by which the Island is surrounded. The lowlands, which are very marshy, are likewise covered by them from six or seven o'clock in the evening until eight o'clock the next morning, when the sun dissipates them. These vapors often 

<column-break/>

have a smell of mud, and as the circulation of air is not free there, they seem to dissipate only to make room for those that succeed them. This unhealthy air is only barely corrected by the quantity of aromatic plants whose sweet scent can be smelled three or four leagues out at sea when the land wind carries it there <margin>(*a*) Our dogs announced the approach of the island to us at least at this distance, by sniffing in that direction for nearly half an hour. It is noteworthy that dogs are a great resource on a ship for recognizing the approach of land; during the night at least, they take the place of telescopes.</margin>. However, one is compensated for this abandonment by nature by the singularity of the animals and plants produced by this climate. The Island is cursed by the rich man who wishes to enjoy himself, but it is very dear to Naturalists.

The monkey, that four-handed creature <term>quadrumane: an archaic biological term for primates, meaning "four-handed," referring to their ability to use both feet and hands for grasping</term> which appears to fill the gap between man and four-legged animals, is found on Santa Catarina Island; and we owed our knowledge of this animal to a singular chance. While passing near a hut situated along the coast, we heard a noise similar to that of a Woodcutter felling

woods. We asked a freed Black man <note>original: "Negre affranchi"</note> what it could be. "It is," he said, "a monkey wandering around the garden to eat the fruit, and warning its comrades to come and profit from the discovery. He has been making this racket for three or four days." One of our boatswains <term>boatswain: *Contremaître*, a senior crew member responsible for the ship's hull and gear</term> lent him his gun; the man loaded it with large lead shot, went toward the noise, and fired at the monkey twice without it fleeing; at the third shot, it fell dead at the foot of the tree. The boatswain brought the monkey aboard the frigate where we had plenty of time to consider it at leisure. It was two feet and nearly eight inches high, standing on its hind legs; its fur was long and of a tawny-brown color over the whole body, except under the belly, which tended toward a light tawny. Its brown beard started from the ears and descended nearly five inches onto its chest; its feet were black like its hands; its hairless ears were well-separated, and the tanned down covering its face appeared so short that one could barely distinguish it from its skin. Its darker eyebrows were prominent; its tail

<column-break/>

<page-num>157</page-num>
-># TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS.<-

was as long as its body, including the head.

I do not know by what chance it had lost its left eye. One had to examine it closely to notice that it was one-eyed <note>original: "borgne"</note>. In place of the globe of its lost eye, it had substituted a ball composed of a resin unknown to us, of rotten wood, and a bit of very fine moss, all kneaded together. The eyelid covered this ball just as it would have the eyeball. Had it imagined this artificial eye <note>original: "œil postiche"</note> to appear less deformed, or to heal its diseased eye, or to protect it from the insults of flies and other insects? I leave it to be guessed. This monkey, moreover, appeared old; for the skin of its face was quite wrinkled, and there were a few white hairs in its beard. We only saw that one during our stay at Santa Catarina Island <note>An island off the coast of Brazil, now known as Florianópolis</note>; we were told, however, that there were many of them, and that people ate the young ones because their flesh is quite delicate. Some even tried to persuade me that the Governor had some served to us during a meal, and that we all took this dish for rabbit.

[ These Monkeys, moreover, are mischievous animals without being harmful; it is not the same with snakes, which in this climate possess all the ferocity of their nature. Our Frenchmen at first walked among them without suspicion, but they soon learned to know them by their bites. ]

A Sailor, after having cut grass for our livestock, having sat down nearby with bare legs, was bitten near the ankle by a snake about a foot and a half long, whose skin was tiger-striped. He took no account of this bite; and as soon as he arrived on board, he dined heartily and without concern. Half an hour later, he was seized by a feeling of nausea; and seeing his leg very swollen and painful, he came to warn me. I began by giving him confidence, to cure him of the fear that had taken hold of his mind. While I was notifying the two Surgeons of the Frigate, he vomited, which he repeated again once or twice within the span of an hour. We made him swallow two *gros* <term>gros: an old French unit of weight, roughly 3.8 grams</term> of theriac <term>theriac: a famous ancient medical concoction, often containing dozens of ingredients, used as a universal antidote</term> mixed with ten drops of volatile spirit of sal ammoniac <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used here as a stimulant to revive the patient</term>, in a glass of

---

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS.</header>

wine. They applied to the wound—which had already turned blackish—after having scarified <term>scarified: the medical practice of making small incisions in the skin to help draw out venom or apply medicine</term> it, a plaster of theriac crushed with garlic. The nausea nevertheless continued; he vomited again two or three times. The remedy was repeated once more. In the meantime, a Portuguese Officer from Fort Santa Cruz <note>Fort Santa Cruz: likely the *Fortaleza de Santa Cruz de Anhatomirim* in Brazil, where the expedition stopped</note> came aboard, to whom we recounted what had happened. The Sailor’s report and the description of the reptile led the Officer to judge that this snake was one of the species that the locals call *Jararaca* <note>The Jararaca (*Bothrops jararaca*) is a highly venomous pit viper common in South America</note>.

> “Its venom is so dangerous,” he said, “that it causes inevitable death to those in whom it does not induce vomiting within twenty-four hours. But, since your Sailor has vomited, you should be reassured on his account. Continue, however, to give him the same remedy, and add an emetic <term>emetic: a substance used to induce vomiting</term> to it. There are several other species of *Jararaca* which must also be mistrusted; one especially, which is the color of earth, or ash-colored, with some darker stripes on the head.”

The next day, the blackness of the wound having not increased,

In the time that followed, no other accidents happened to him; the wound was treated like an ordinary wound. He was also purged twice, and he has always remained well. Going into the woods and the countryside is almost always to expose oneself to the bite of dangerous reptiles, which are found there in great numbers. We many times saw a sort of wavy furrow on the sand by the seashore, formed by the tracks of snakes that had passed by. If, when one has had the misfortune of being bitten by one, it is not promptly remedied, one must expect to die in the most cruel pains. Some species, especially those of the *Jararacas* <note>original: *Jararacas*. These are highly venomous pit vipers native to South America</note>, exhale a strong smell of musk: this odor is of great help to those who know it, to protect themselves from being taken by surprise.

The *Rattlesnake* [*Serpent à sonnettes*], is one of the most terrible that originates in Brazil; its length reaches up to three feet, rarely exceeding that by more than half a foot. Its color is an ashy iron-gray, and it is regularly patterned with waves. At the end of its

---

<page-num>161</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS.</header>

tail is attached what the Spaniards call its *Cascabel* [*Cascabelle*], from which comes the name *Cascabella* snake. This *Cascabel*, which it has pleased us to call a *Rattle* [*Sonnette*], because of the noise it makes, resembles the pod of peas dried on the plant. It is likewise divided into several segments or mounds, which contain round ossicles <term>ossicles: small bone-like structures; here referring to the hard, interlocking segments of the rattle</term>, the friction of which produces a sound quite similar to that of two or three somewhat muffled bells, or hawk-bells. Its hissing also has much in common with the noise made by cicadas. The bite of this snake is so dangerous that the inhabitants of the places where it is found are very fortunate that nature has given this reptile a sign that warns them of its approach. It is also called *Boicininga* <note>original: *Boicininga*. A Tupi-Guarani word for the rattlesnake, literally meaning 'ringing snake'</note>.

[ One does not usually have a correct idea of the danger there is in inhabiting a climate where the *Rattlesnake* is found; it clears rocks with a singular rapidity: coiling itself in a circle, leaping upon its prey, distilling its poison, and withdrawing, are for it the work of an instant: it swims with ease,

& and attacks men in the sea, just as in the forests; a light blow struck upon its back kills it: one is informed of its death by the silence of its rattle.]

I saw only a single lizard at Saint Catherine Island; it might have been two feet long and three and a half or four inches wide. Its skin was black, spotted with white from the head to the end of the tail. The belly was nearly the same, but the white dominated more there; whereas the black and the white were distributed almost equally in spots of regular shapes over all the rest of the body; its form was otherwise that of our green lizards of France. Mr. de Nerville, who was with me, was preparing to fire a shot at it when I recognized that the animal was dead. We approached it; but as it exhaled a fetid odor, we did not judge it appropriate to examine it with more attention. Could it be the *Maboya* <note>original: *Maboya*. A term often used in the 18th century for various New World lizards, particularly geckos or skinks</note>, or the *Tejuguacu* <note>original: *Tejuguacu*. This likely refers to the Black and White Tegu (*Salvator merianae*), common in Brazil</note>, so named by the people of the country, and *Iguana* by Piso and Marcgraf <note>Willem Piso and Georg Marcgraf were 17th-century naturalists whose work on Brazilian flora and fauna remained the standard reference for over a century</note>?

The Brazilians experience the inconve-

<column-break/>

<page-num>163</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS.</header>

nience of all hot countries, which is to be tormented by insects whose smallness prevents one from avoiding their bite. One of our Acadians <note>The Acadians were French settlers from North America (modern-day Nova Scotia) who had been deported by the British in 1755; many joined French colonial expeditions like this one to the Falkland Islands</note>, having returned on board, found himself much inconvenienced by a small tumor that had appeared on the big toe of his left foot a few days prior. This tumor increased, as did the pain it caused. It was recognized to be the *Nigue* of Brazil, which is called *Pique* in Peru <note>original: *Nigue* and *Pique*. This is the chigoe flea (*Tunga penetrans*), also known as a jigger, which borrows into the skin of the feet to lay eggs</note>. It is an insect so small that it is almost imperceptible. See the description made of it by Mr. d'Ulloa <note>Antonio de Ulloa (1716–1795), a Spanish scientist and naval officer who wrote extensively about South American natural history</note> in his voyage to Peru, which perfectly fits the one we saw at Saint Catherine Island. Our Acadian was cured by the extraction of the nest and by the application of tobacco ash. The legs of this insect, says the author I just cited, do not have the spring of those of fleas; which is no small favor from providence, since if it had the faculty of jumping, there is no living body in the places where this insect is found that would not be filled with them. which it could cause them. It is always found in the dust, especially in dirty places. It attaches itself to the feet, even to the soles and the toes.
   The Nigua <note>original: *Nigua*. Now known as the chigger or jigger flea (*Tunga penetrans*), a parasitic insect distinct from the North American harvest mite</note> pierces the skin so subtly that it introduces itself without being felt. One only notices it when it begins to expand. At first, it is not difficult to pull it out; but even if it has only introduced its head, it establishes itself so firmly that one must scarify <term>scarify: to make small, shallow incisions in the skin</term> the surrounding parts to make it let go. If one does not notice it soon enough, the insect pierces the first layer of skin without obstacle and lodges there. There it sucks the blood and makes itself a nest from a white and delicate tunic <note>The "tunic" is the abdomen of the female flea, which distends to many times its original size as it fills with eggs</note>, which has the shape of a flat pearl. It crouches in this space in such a way that the head and feet are turned toward the outside for the convenience of its feeding, while the other part of its body corresponds to the interior of the tunic to deposit its eggs there. As it lays them, the tunic expands; and within an interval of four to five days, it reaches up to two lines <term>line: a historical unit of measurement equal to 1/12 of an inch, or about 2.25 millimeters</term> in diameter. It is very important to pull it out,

<column-break/>

<page-num>165</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

otherwise, bursting by itself, it spreads an infinity of germs <note>In this context, the author refers to the eggs or larvae of the insect</note>, which multiply the insects and the pain; this vermin sometimes penetrates as far as the bone; and when one has succeeded in ridding oneself of it, the pain lasts until the flesh and the skin are entirely restored.
   This operation is long and painful. It consists of separating, with the point of a needle, the flesh touching the membrane where the eggs reside, without bursting the tunic.
   After having detached even the smallest ligaments, one pulls out the "pearl," which is larger or smaller in proportion to the time the insect has spent in the area. If by misfortune the tunic bursts, one's attention must redouble to tear out all the roots, and especially not to leave the main Nigua. It would begin laying eggs again before the wound was closed, and by digging into the flesh, it would cause even more trouble to remove. In the hole left by the pearl, one puts a little hot ash or chewed tobacco <note>The author likely refers to a common folk remedy using the alkalinity of ash or the nicotine in tobacco as an antiseptic to prevent reinfection</note>. During the great heat, one must guard—

   Although the insect is not felt at the time it creeps in, by the next day it causes a burning and very painful itching, especially in certain parts, such as under the nails. It is observed that the **Nigua** wages a relentless war against some animals, especially the **Deer**, which it devours by degrees, and whose front and hind feet are found full of holes after its death.

It spoils everything with its filth and its foul odor. In the Antilles Islands, it is known by the name of **Ravet**.

I have noticed very few peculiar fish in the sea of Brazil; there is however one named *Panapana* <term>Panapana: a Tupi name for the hammerhead shark</term>, of which I provide the illustration here in *Plate III. fig. 4*. They were mobile, opening and closing like the fingers of a hand, in such a way that each row folded over its neighbor in an overlapping fashion; that is to say, the upper row, in curving, occupied the gap or the space between the teeth of the lower row. The teeth of the Shark <note>original: *Requin*</note> are arranged in its maw like slates on a roof, or if one prefers, like the leaves of an artichoke.

It is said that the Shark is always preceded by another fish called the *Pilot* <note>The Pilot fish (*Naucrates ductor*), famous for its symbiotic relationship with sharks</note>. We can assert the contrary; at least, we have several times seen Sharks without this forerunner.

The Pilot is one of the beautiful and good sea fish. It is of a blue arranged in bands; some of a beautiful very dark blue on the back, descending by lightening insensibly until under the belly; they are six in number. Four other bands, as well as the head and the tail, are of a very light blue, or a white washed with blue. The globe of the eye is of a beautiful gold color, except for the pupil,

We did not fish for many beautiful shells there; the only one that deserved our attention was a **Helmet shell** <note>original: *casque*. These are large, thick sea snails from the Cassidae family, often used for carving cameos.</note> which was at least eight inches in diameter. We found a **hermit crab** <note>original: *soldat*. In this period, hermit crabs were often called "soldiers" because they "occupy" shells like a fortress.</note> and some small **sea horses**. We always fished with fear; for this roadstead is the usual sanctuary of **Sharks**.

A Spaniard brought us a few hundred **oysters** one day: they were much larger than the white oysters of **Saintonge** <note>A historic province on the Atlantic coast of France, famous for its oyster production in the Marennes-Oléron basin.</note>; for their shells were at least five inches in diameter. One cannot eat any fatter or better in France. They were a true cream, novel in both taste and whiteness. We did our best to persuade the Spaniard to reveal the place where he fished for them, but all our efforts were useless, and he kept his secret as if it were a matter of State.

Our research into the shells of **Sainte-Catherine** <note>Santa Catarina Island, Brazil.</note> was not, however, always fruitless: we found in a damp and marshy ground a prodigious quantity of *Tourlouroux* <note>A term used for various species of land crabs, likely the *Gecarcinidae* family common in tropical regions.</note>;

it is a species of crab that stays on land, where it forms a retreat for itself. The largest are no more than two inches wide. The shape of their shell is almost square, of a reddish-brown color that lightens imperceptibly down to the belly, which is a light red. This shell or casing is quite strong, though thin. Their eyes are a brilliant black, hard as horn; they extend and retract just like those of **Crayfish**.

The **Tourlouroux** have four legs on each side, each composed of four segments, the last of which is flat and ends in a point. They use them to walk sideways, like ordinary crabs, and to dig into the earth. They also have two legs or pincers that are larger, but one in particular is at least double the size of the other; it is the right one. These pincers or nippers are a bright red, like those of sea crabs, and they use them to cut the leaves and roots of the plants which serve as their food. When they see something that frightens them, they strike these two nippers against each other, as if to terrify their enemy,

These biting claws, as well as their legs, hold so loosely to their bodies that they detach and remain in the hands of those who try to catch the animal, while the **land crab** <note>original: *Tourlouroux*. This name was commonly used by French travelers in the 18th century for various tropical land crabs, specifically those of the *Gecarcinidae* family.</note> escapes.

The two sexes have their tails folded under the belly, where they fit so precisely into a cavity in the belly shell that one can hardly distinguish them. That of the male constantly diminishes in width toward the tip. That of the female is equally wide all the way to the end. As the female lays her eggs, they attach themselves to the long, rough hairs with which the underside of the tail is provided. She supports them, envelops them, and prevents them from falling, and ensures that the sand or grass, or other irregularities encountered in her path, cannot detach them. These **land crabs** were in such great numbers in this marshy ground that it was not possible to place one's foot there without crushing several of them. The part of the animal kingdom that must most occupy a **Naturalist** <note>A scholar of natural history who studies plants, animals, and minerals.</note> at **Saint Catherine Island** and in **Brazil** is the **History of Birds**; it is here that nature seems to have displayed the greatest magnificence and variety.

The first **Parrot** I had the occasion to examine was a gift from the Governor to **Mr. de Bougainville** <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville (1729–1811), the famous French explorer leading this expedition.</note>. The Governor told us that this variety was due partly to art and partly to nature. A Spaniard at another time came to bring us four of these birds, two of which were already raised and spoke the Portuguese language: the two others had been taken from the nest, and did not yet eat on their own. I bought one of the latter, in exchange for a head ribbon, and I kept it until the beginning of May when it died of a catarrh <note>A 18th-century term for an inflammatory cold or respiratory infection.</note> in the head. This catarrh had caused its eyes to swell. It fell into the chest, and having made it asthmatic, I could not save it.

Among the number of these Parrots there were three species which differed in plumage and in size. One of the two had the feathers of the neck and stomach of a tawny and iridescent red, mixed with a little gray; the top of the front of the head was of a cinnabar red <note>A bright, slightly orange-red color derived from the mineral cinnabar.</note>, a little faded and dull; the wing tips were of a red more

It spoke Portuguese very well, and easily learned French. It died in the first days of our arrival at the Malouines Islands <note>Also known today as the Falkland Islands.</note>. The second was larger than any of those we had. The top of its head was of a cinnabar red, the two sides of a vivid blue toward the ears, which weakened until becoming gray as the feathers moved further away. The wings and the tail were like those of the first. The others were nearly half as large; otherwise they resembled it in plumage, except that the red of their heads was much more vivid; perhaps they were younger. Mr. de Bougainville's <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville (1729–1811), the famous French explorer and navigator leading this expedition.</note> Parrot perished from the same disease as mine, during our stay in the Malouines Islands; that of Mr. de Belcourt fell into the sea and drowned there; so that out of seven, we only brought two back to France; one very large one and another of the small species, which has— <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing the description of the surviving small parrot on the next page.</note>

Among the presents that the Governor gave to our Commander, one particularly noticed fifty Toucan skins, stripped from the beak down to the thighs, and dried with the feathers, which are partly lemon-colored, partly flesh-red <note>original: "rouge incarnat," a vivid, deep crimson or carnation color.</note>, and partly black in transverse bands from one wing to the other.

The size of the Toucan is roughly that of a wood pigeon <note>The Common Wood Pigeon (*Columba palumbus*), used here as a familiar reference for the reader to judge size.</note>; but it is set higher upon its legs, which are of a bluish-gray, as are its feet, and armed with very long claws. black and rounded at its end, but commonly variegated with blue, purple, and yellow, over a dark brown. The back and the wings are of this latter color, except for some black feathers that trim the wings. Its head is very large, but quite small in proportion to its beak, which is seven to eight inches from the root to the tip. The upper part, near the head, has a base of about two inches, and forms along its length a shape that is roughly triangular and convex on top, of which the two side surfaces are slightly raised and rounded. The upper part, which forms the interior of the beak, is hollow: its edges or lips are notched like a saw. The lower part presents the same shape as the upper, but is slightly concave underneath. These two parts, equal in their length, fit into one another, and diminish gradually until the tip, which is slightly curved downward and pointed. The tongue is a whitish membrane, almost as long as the beak, but very narrow and very flat. The Guara <note>The Scarlet Ibis (*Eudocimus ruber*).</note> is sometimes encountered at Santa Catarina Island. This bird is as large as a large French Magpie. It has a long beak, curved at the tip; the thighs and feet are long. The first feathers that cover it after it hatches are black. This color fades imperceptibly and becomes ash-colored. When it begins to fly, all its feathers become white; they finally take on a rose color; and becoming redder from day to day, they acquire the most vivid scarlet color, which they keep forever. Although this bird is voracious and carnivorous, it nests and lays its eggs under the roofs of houses and in holes in walls, like our sparrows. It flies in flocks; and the Savages <note>A term used by the author to refer to the indigenous peoples of the region.</note> use its feathers for their head ornaments.

Our Frenchmen, while hunting one day, shot a singular bird called a *Spoonbill* <note>original: "Spatule," referring to the Roseate Spoonbill (*Platalea ajaja*).</note>; they broke one of its wings, captured it, and brought it on board. It was a young one, and all its plumage was rose-colored

The following day, while lying in bed, I noticed a light in one of the shelves where I had placed my books. I was no longer thinking about the luminous flies, and I initially imagined that this light was a stray ray from the lamp of the binnacle <note>original: "habitacle"; the wooden housing on deck that held the ship's compass and a lamp for night viewing.</note>, which was located near the window of my poop cabin <note>original: "dunette"; the captain's or officers' quarters at the rear of the ship.</note>; but seeing this light change position, I remembered the fly that had escaped the previous night. I caught it and, having shut it under a glass tumbler, I observed it at my leisure the next day and sketched it.

   Upon leaving Montevideo on the River Plate <note>original: "riviere de la Plata."</note>, the same thing happened to us during the calm that surprised us on the day of our departure. The light that these insects spread, being even more brilliant and dazzling than that of the flies from Saint Catherine Island <note>Santa Catarina, Brazil.</note>, I placed several of them with some fresh grass in a glass tumbler which I covered with another; and having secured it on my table, I brought a book near it, in which—without the help of any other light—I read with great ease, even though the typeface was very small.

The posterior part was composed of five rings <note>In modern entomology, these are referred to as abdominal segments.</note>, of which the two closest to the body were black, covered with a short, velvety hair; the next two were covered in a golden hair; and the fifth—as wide as the other two combined, and which ended the body in a rounded point—was also covered in a black velvety hair, but slightly longer than that of the other rings. The first insect emitted rays of light almost exclusively from its head; this one spread light from all parts of its body, if one excepts the head. Those that I had enclosed with fresh grass lived for four days, and preserved the brilliance of their light in all its strength until their death.

I had for a long time been curious to obtain some hummingbirds <note>original: "oiseaux-mouches"; literally "fly-birds."</note>, and not knowing how to go about catching this pretty animal alive, I risked firing a shotgun at one of them which was fluttering—

Some call this bird the *Lisongere* <note>original: "Lisongere"; likely a variant of "lisonneur" (flatterer), a folk name for the hummingbird's gentle "caressing" of flowers.</note> or *Becquefleurs* <note>original: "Becquefleurs"; literally "flower-pecker."</note>, because it flutters incessantly over flowers, like a butterfly, and likewise siphons their nectar <note>original: "suc."</note>. The entire volume of its body, including its feathers, is no larger than a common hazelnut. It has a tail nearly three times longer than its body; a fairly small neck, a proportionate head, and very bright eyes. Its beak is somewhat whitish at the base and black throughout the rest, as long as its body, slender and very sharp. Its wings are long, delicate, and very ample in proportion: the tips of their feathers reach about a third of the way down the tail; the tail, like the wings, is of a purplish brown. There are several species of hummingbirds <note>original: "oiseaux-mouches"; literally "fly-birds."</note>, which differ in size and color. Sometimes the plumage of this bird appears as black as the finest black velvet; sometimes a bright, fresh green <note>original: "verd naissant"; literally "nascent green," referring to the vivid color of new spring growth.</note>, and sometimes dawn-colored <note>original: "couleur d’aurore"; a poetic term for a pinkish-yellow or golden-orange hue typical of sunrise.</note>. At other times, it looks like a cloth of gold shaded with all these colors. The entire back is a dark golden green. The large feathers of the wings are a deep violet, sometimes leaning toward purple. The tail is composed of nine feathers, as long as the entire body, and of a black mixed with brown, purple, and violet; this mixture creates the most pleasing sight, depending on the spectator's position. The entire underside of the belly is also a mixture of black, violet, green, and dawn-color, which always strikes the observer’s eye differently as the bird or the person changes their point of view. Polished jet is not a more vivid or brilliant black than the eye of this bird; its legs are short and black, as are its feet, which are composed of four toes—three in the front—all armed with a curved, sharp, and

black claw, which is very long in proportion to the rest of the body. When it flies, its wings make a humming sound very similar to that of certain large flies that we see in France flitting from flower to flower. It suspends its nest among tall grasses, from the branches of orange trees, or from shrubs. In our French Islands <note>Refers to the French West Indies (the Antilles), such as Martinique or Guadeloupe.</note> it is called a *Hummingbird* <note>original: "Colibri."</note>, some call it a *Quinde*, and the Spaniards call them *Tomineios*, because the nest and the bird together weigh only one Spanish *tomín* <note>A colonial Spanish unit of weight, approximately 0.6 grams or 1/8th of an ounce.</note>.

[ One can judge the beauty of the Hummingbird while it is alive by the use the Indians make of it when it is dried. The women hang them from their ears, just as our European women wear pearls and diamonds; nature intended for the beauty of these birds to survive them, an advantage she has not granted to the human race. ]

The history of the vegetable kingdom of Santa Catarina Island <note>An island off the coast of Brazil, a frequent stop for explorers heading toward the South Atlantic.</note> is as varied as that of the animal kingdom; the forests are full of fragrant shrubs, and the scent they exhale somewhat corrects the impurity of the atmosphere <note>This reflects the 18th-century "miasma theory," the belief that pleasant smells could "correct" or purify air thought to cause disease.</note>.

I noticed palm trees there of a prodigious height, about a foot in diameter, and as straight as reeds; I also saw a species of tree whose trunk and branches are covered in small thorny growths, six to eight lines <note>A "line" (French: *ligne*) is a historical unit of measurement equal to 1/12th of an inch, or approximately 2.25 millimeters.</note> in diameter at their base, protruding by four or five, and the thorn planted in the middle is up to four lines in length. The bark of this tree is gray and resembles that of the Beech. Could it not be the same tree that, in our West Indies, is called *thorny wood*?

The entire coast of Brazil is furnished with high-forest timber, and the base of the trees there is as if smothered by thickets and brushwood. Those on the Island are enveloped in a species of thorny Aloe. Along the edges that approach closest to the coast, there are many different species of trees the height and thickness of apple trees, but whose leaves are smooth, of a beautiful green, and have the shape of common laurel <note>original: "laurier-franc"; referring to the *Laurus nobilis* or bay laurel.</note> leaves. They differ from one another almost only by their size and their relative thickness. I noticed one that one would have taken at first

# 198 HISTORY OF A VOYAGE

who did not know how to tell me its name or its properties.

Among the fruits that people eat, the *Pineapple* <note>original: "Ananas"</note> is particularly distinguished; this fruit and the plant that bears it are known today in Europe; but in the hottest climates, it has not been able to acquire the flavor and sweet scent of the Brazilian pineapple. It grows there on its own without cultivation, and in great abundance. It blackens and spoils the knives with which it is cut: which perhaps has led some authors to say that the rind of this fruit is so hard that it dulls the blade. It is true that if, after having cut the pineapple into slices, one does not wash and wipe the knife well, after a few hours the blade is found to be altered and eaten away, almost as if one had applied aqua fortis <note>original: "eau forte"; a historical term for nitric acid, a highly corrosive mineral acid.</note> to it. Its juice or sap is an excellent soap for removing stains from clothes. That of Brazil is, they say, a preventative against seasickness.

love-apple <note>original: "pomme d'amour"; historically this term referred to the tomato, but here the author is using it as a visual comparison for a wild fruit.</note>. A Portuguese man, who was with us, named it *Maracuja*; its figure can be seen in *Plate III. figure 2*. The plant that bears it is thorny; the leaf closely approaches that of the *Mad Thorn-apple* <note>original: "Stramonium furiosum"; likely referring to *Datura stramonium*, known for its hallucinogenic and toxic properties.</note>, but it is smaller. This Portuguese man told me that the maracuja fruit was not eaten, although he did not know of any dangerous quality in it.

The Brazilian grenadilla is round, yet a little flattened at both ends, and as large as a hen's egg. Its rind is very smooth, shiny on the outside, and of a flesh-red color when the fruit is at its maturity. Inside it is white and soft; its thickness is about a line and a half. All of this substance is separated from the rind by an extremely thin film. The plant that bears this fruit climbs along trees, and resembles in its leaves and its flower the one called the *Passion Flower* <note>original: "fleur de la Paſſion". Early explorers saw symbols of the crucifixion in the flower’s structure—the crown of thorns, the nails, and the five wounds.</note>. It gives off a very sweet scent. To eat a good grenadilla, one must not let it ripen completely on the plant; it would wither and dry up there. It is picked a little before it is ripe, and kept for a few days.

Besides lemons and oranges, there is on Santa Catarina Island a kind of refreshment for hunters. It is a very common fruit called *Prickly Pears* <note>original: "Pommes de Raquette"; literally "Racket Apples," named for the paddle-shaped pads of the Opuntia cactus.</note>. This fruit very closely approaches the shape of our figs. Its first skin is green: it then turns a little yellow, and finally takes on the red color of lacquer on the side where it is struck by the sun's rays. This skin is all bristled with extremely fine

<column-break/>

### TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS. ###
### 201 ###

spines. One must be skillful to pick this fruit and peel it without making one's fingers into a pincushion filled with these spines, which are almost imperceptible. Fortunately, they cause more worry than harm, until one has found the means to get rid of them.

Under the skin, which is as thick as that of a fig, one finds a white envelope, thinner and more tender than the first. It contains a soft substance of a bright red, dotted with small seeds like figs. This substance has a slightly tart flavor, a little sweet and very pleasant. When one eats a certain quantity of it, the urine is stained red, but without any harm resulting from it. This fruit is even refreshing. Our Captain, his two sons, and I were almost the only ones who ate them: the others did not dare to imitate us, for fear of being inconvenienced by them.

I would have much desired upon embarking to provide myself with a supply of *sweet potatoes* <note>original: "patates"</note> and *yams* <note>original: "ignames"</note>; but these roots were not yet ready to be pulled from the earth.

### 202 ###

The sweet potato <note>original: "patate". In the 18th century, "patate" specifically referred to the sweet potato (*Ipomoea batatas*), while the common potato was often called "pomme de terre".</note> is a kind of potato, or Jerusalem artichoke, but much more delicate.

The *Yam* <note>original: "Igname" or "Iniams".</note> is a climbing plant, furnished with filaments that take root and serve to multiply it naturally; so much so that if one is not careful to remove many of them, it will soon cover all the ground in which previously only one or two had been placed. The stem is square, about the thickness of the little finger. Its leaves are shaped like a heart, with an elongated and pointed tip. They are a dark green, as large as those of the Greater Burdock <note>original: "Lappa major". A common plant known for its large leaves and prickly burrs.</note>. Those further from the root are smaller; but they are smooth, thick, and well-nourished, attached in pairs to the stem by short, square, slightly curved stalks. From the stem emerge some spikes of small bell-shaped flowers, whose pistil becomes a seed pod <term>silique|A long, thin fruit (pod) that splits open when dry to release seeds.</term> filled with small black seeds. These seeds are not usually sown, because the yam grows much better and faster from cuttings. For this, one uses the head of the

<column-break/>

### TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS. ###
### 203 ###

fruit, with a part of the stem that bears it.

The root is more or less large depending on the quality of the soil that nourishes it. Its skin is uneven, rough, thick, of a dark violet, and very hairy. The inside has the consistency of beets, of a grayish-white, sometimes tending toward a flesh color; it has a good taste, is nourishing, and is easy to digest. It is a treat for the Black people <note>original: "Negres". Referring to the enslaved African population who formed the labor force of the colony.</note>, and even for the Portuguese.

In general, wheat bread is only eaten at the home of the Governor of Santa Catarina. In all other dwellings, it is replaced by cassava <note>original: "cassave". A flatbread or flour made from the manioc root.</note>, which is a kind of cooked paste made from the flour of the manioc root. This root is, they say, a poison when eaten raw. However, I have seen children busy removing the bark to make cassava, and eating it raw without being inconvenienced by it. Some roasted it over the embers, then removed the bark and ate it. <note>Pernetty is likely observing "sweet manioc" (aipim), which is safe to eat, as opposed to "bitter manioc," which contains lethal levels of cyanide and requires complex processing.</note>

Laët <note>Jean de Laët (1581–1649), a Dutch geographer and director of the Dutch West India Company, famous for his descriptions of the New World.</note>, cited in the *History of Voyages* collected by the Abbé Prevôt <note>Antoine François Prévost, a prolific French author known for compiling a massive multi-volume collection of travel accounts.</note>, says that there are various kinds, one in particular unique to Brazil, which is called *Aypi* <note>Known today as *Aipim* or "sweet manioc," which contains much lower levels of cyanide than "bitter" manioc.</note>, and which can be eaten raw without any danger. *Some Nations, of the race of the Tapouyras,* he adds, *also eat the common manioc raw, which is a poison to all others, and suffer no ill effects, because they are accustomed to it from infancy.* Those, however, whom we saw eating it raw, were not of the Tapouya <note>A term historically used to describe non-Tupi-speaking indigenous groups in Brazil.</note> race. They were the children of Whites, whose fathers were Portuguese. The leaves of this manioc closely resemble the leaves of the peony in their shape.

The roots are dried over a fire on hurdles; they are scraped with sharpened stones to form a flour, the smell of which is similar to that of starch. This flour is placed in large pots; care must be taken to stir it until it thickens, just as is done in France with buckwheat flour <note>original: "farine de blé noir". Buckwheat was a staple in Brittany, Pernetty’s home region.</note>. Once cooled to the consistency of a solid jelly, its taste

differs little from that of white bread. The flour prepared for provisions for travels and wars is cooked longer, and is thereby harder and easier to transport. Prepared with meat juices, it makes a dish that resembles rice cooked in broth and gravy; and this dish is very nourishing. These same roots, crushed or grated while fresh and before being put to the fire, yield a juice as white as milk, which needs only to be exposed to the sun to coagulate like cheese, and makes a good food, provided it is cooked over a fire. This manner of scraping the manioc roots with sharp stones is that of the Brazilians <note>Referring to the indigenous inhabitants.</note>, who do not have knowledge of the mechanical arts of Europe. The Portuguese born or simply established on Santa Catarina Island and on the surrounding mainland coasts employ for this purpose a large wooden wheel, the outer surface of whose rims is hollowed into a channel. This channel is covered with an iron grater. One approaches this grater, pressing down slightly on it, while another person turns the wheel: which produces the effect

This juice is discarded into a small ditch and flows away into the earth. <note>The "white juice" of the bitter manioc contains high levels of hydrogen cyanide; the process of grating and squeezing is essential to remove these toxins before the starch can be eaten.</note> These roots are then dried to reduce them to flour and to make cassava. They also serve the Brazilians <note>Referring here to the indigenous Tupi-Guarani peoples of the region.</note> for the preparation of their beverage. This process is quite repulsive, as is the drink itself, to those who know the manner in which it is made. It is the women who are charged with this task, especially the elderly. Laët <note>Jean de Laët (1581–1649), a Dutch geographer whose works were a primary source for European knowledge of the Americas.</note> provides the details of this, and the reader is referred there. <note>The "repulsive" process Pernetty refers to is the traditional preparation of *cauim*, where women chewed the manioc and spat it into vats; the enzymes in human saliva converted the starches into fermentable sugars.</note>

At Santa Catarina, several domestic animals are fed with the stems of banana trees; both because they keep very well and because they are very nourishing. It is simply a matter of cutting them with a knife; and the oxen, as well as the sheep, eat them greedily.

The banana tree is a plant whose stem is composed entirely of leaves rolled one over the other; it is a reddish-white in some places, and yellowish and greenish...

---

### TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS ###
### 207 ###

...ish in others. When the root sends up a shoot, only two leaves rolled together emerge from the edge of the ground. They widen as they unroll to make room for two others emerging from the same center. Rolled like the first ones, they unfold in the same manner and are followed by several more, which rise in height and expand in width—always rolled in this way—and form the stem of this tree-like plant, which grows to eight, ten, and even twelve feet; after which it grows no thicker.

Then the leaves emerge from the top and the center of the stem, to which they are attached only by a stalk about an inch in diameter and a foot long, which is round on one side and hollowed into a channel in the middle on the other. This extended stalk forms the midrib of the leaf, which is sometimes up to fifteen and eighteen inches wide by six to seven feet long. This leaf is a beautiful green on the upper side, and underneath a somewhat grayish green, which makes it appear silvery. Its thickness is that of a very strong parchment; but its delicacy and its great size, which give the wind a significant purchase, mean that it is often shredded into many...

When the banana tree has reached its natural height, it measures nine to ten inches in diameter. Its stem is so tender—even though the leaves composing it are very tightly packed against one another—that one can easily cut it with a knife, or even with a single stroke of a billhook <note>A *serpe* is a hooked cutting tool used in forestry and viticulture, similar to a machete.</note> if taken at a slight angle. This is because these leaves are fleshy and full of sap; consequently, the plant only grows well in rich, damp soil.

Having reached the stage where it can bear fruit, it pushes from the center of the top of its stem another stalk about an inch and a half in diameter and three to four feet long. This stalk becomes covered with various rings of buds of a yellow color verging on green. A large heart-shaped bud, six to seven inches long and three inches in diameter, terminates this stalk. It is composed of

---

### 209 ###

several thin skins layered one over the other, the exterior of which is red and covered with a strong, smooth envelope of a flaxen-grey color. <note>original: "gris de lin." This refers to a pale purplish-grey or lilac color, characteristic of the bracts (the leaf-like covers) of a banana blossom.</note> The stalk divides into four to allow this bud to emerge. This stalk is straight at first; but as the fruits succeed the small flowers that line the stalk in rings, the weight the fruit acquires as it grows causes it to bend gradually, leaning more and more toward the earth.

In our Antilles Islands, this stalk laden with fruit is called a *Régime* <note>A botanical term still used today for a cluster or bunch of bananas.</note>. I do not know the name the Portuguese give it. A *régime* sometimes contains so many *bananas* that it would suffice for a man's load. They are attached to the spots previously occupied by the flowers. The bunch is cut as soon as one notices a few bananas changing their color from green to yellow. It is hung in the air inside the house, and the fruit is eaten as it ripens; this is known to happen when it begins to yield under the thumb and turns yellow. We hung at least twenty of them around the quarterdeck; <note>The *gaillard d'arrière* or quarterdeck was the raised deck at the rear of the ship, often where officers gathered.</note> and some of our officers were so fond...

...of this fruit, that they ate it almost without waiting for it to reach sufficient ripeness.

The banana is nearly two inches in diameter; the longest I saw were only six inches long. Both ends are in a rounded point, and its shape is angular, but with very blunt angles. The peel is smooth, flexible, and slightly thicker than that of a fig, though much firmer. The pulp is a yellowish-white, with the consistency of a very rich, creamy fresh cheese, or of newly churned butter. It also resembles the latter when the banana is cooked.

It has a bit of that same taste, but as if it had been mixed with the pulp of an overripe quince. People claim it is a very good food. For my part, I found nothing wonderful about it; I ate them raw and cooked, ripe and unripe, without enjoying their flavor.

A plant extremely common in the woods of the Island, and which covers most of the rocks along the coast, is the *Caraguata*. <note>The author is likely describing a species of Bromeliad, such as *Bromelia pinguin* or a similar epiphytic plant common in coastal Brazil.</note> It is also found in abundance on the branches of tall trees,

...should do with it. He replied to me that it was *Doradilla*; <note>original: "Doradille"</note> that while in Valparaiso, he had heard it called by that name, and that in this city, as well as in the others of Peru where he had been, it was frequently used as an infusion to cure stomach ailments. Our Captain complained of these from time to time. I gathered a fairly large quantity of it, and we occasionally took it as a substitute for tea. Its taste is quite pleasant. Others called it *Vira-Verda*; this is also the name given to it in Montevideo. Frézier, <note>Amédée-François Frézier (1682–1773) was a French engineer and explorer whose maps and descriptions of the South American coast were highly influential.</note> in the account of his *Voyage to the South Sea*, says that a French surgeon had it used with great success to cure tertian fever. <term>tertian fever: a form of malaria characterized by a fever that recurs every third day.</term> However, the *Doradilla* of the Spaniards is a species of *ceterach* <note>a type of fern, specifically miltwaste</note> whose leaf is entirely curled; they attribute great virtues to it. The *Vira-Verda* in question here has a downy stem and leaves, similar to the yellow *immortelle*; <note>the "everlasting flower" (genus Helichrysum)</note> the flower is a cluster of small yellow florets with pointed petals. The flowers of the *immortelle* are shaped like a rose, and their petals are arranged in the same way.

---

### TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS. 213 ###
<insert>O iij</insert> <note>This is a printer's signature mark, indicating the third leaf of the "O" gathering in the book's binding process.</note>

Among the number of plants I gathered then was a species of pepper, or pimento, quite common in the fields along the edge of the woods. Its taste is infinitely more biting than that of the pimentos or long peppers we know in France. Thus, our sailors called it the *angry pepper*. <note>original: "piment enragé"</note> This fruit is the length, shape, and color—but at least twice the size—of the *barberry*. <note>original: "épine-vinette"</note> It is green at first and becomes red upon maturity. The flower it succeeds resembles that of other peppers. The plant that bears it rises to a height of about two feet. It is branchy and knotty; the stem is round, green, and rather slender. The leaves are similar in shape to those of the *garden nightshade*, <note>original: "Solanum hortense"</note> but as small as those of the *stinking goosefoot*, <note>original: "Chenopodium foetidum," also known as "Vulvaria"</note> which they closely resemble. One of the small fruits of the *angry pepper*, placed in a sauce, enhances the flavor as much as an entire fruit of the largest peppers. This is what prompted our sailors to make a large provision of it.

This climate also produces some plants...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the expedition's stay at Santa Catarina, Brazil, focusing on the medicinal knowledge of a freed Black man and the properties of the guava tree before transitioning to the arrival at the Falkland Islands.</meta>

### 214 HISTORY OF A VOYAGE ###

...medicinal properties that I discovered by chance. One of our Acadians <note>Refers to the French-speaking inhabitants of Acadia (modern-day Canadian Maritimes) who were being resettled by this expedition to the Falkland Islands.</note> was tormented by a persistent diarrhea, <note>original: "cours de ventre," a common 18th-century term for dysentery or severe bowel complaints.</note> which had until then resisted all the remedies administered to him by the two surgeons of our frigate. A freed Black man proposed to cure him with an herbal tea; <note>original: "tisane"</note> he might have succeeded had he begun using it sooner; for having taken it for only two days, he found himself greatly relieved. This supposed tea was nothing more than a simple decoction of the tips of tender buds and small fruits of the guava tree, <term>guava tree: (Psidium guajava) a tropical shrub or small tree whose leaves and fruit are still used in traditional medicine for their astringent properties.</term> which were only just beginning to set. <note>original: "noués," the stage in plant growth where the fruit begins to form after the flower falls.</note> If this fruit had been a little more advanced, perhaps it would have produced a more effective result. Since the Acadian had not made a provision of these buds before departing, he could not continue their use; his ailment returned with violence and continued until a fortnight before our departure from the Falkland Islands, <note>original: "Isles Malouines"</note> where he began to feel better a few days after we had landed him there. The quality of the air, and undoubtedly the exercise he took there, strengthened him more and more, and he considered himself cured by the time of our departure.

---

### TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS. 215 ###
<insert>O iv</insert> <note>A printer's signature mark used to help binders assemble the pages of the book in the correct order.</note>

The same Black man had cured in a few days the sister of this Acadian's wife, whose legs had become so swollen that she had great difficulty standing. It was claimed that this swelling was the effect of scurvy. <term>scurvy: a disease caused by a deficiency of vitamin C, common among sailors, resulting in swollen limbs, bleeding gums, and exhaustion.</term> Whatever the case may be, she suffered, she said, great pains in her ankles, which ceased after the Black man applied a fomentation <term>fomentation: the application of warm, moist substances, like cloths soaked in herbal decoctions, to the body to ease pain or swelling.</term> of some local herbs boiled in pure water. She was cured in six or seven days. I was assured that this Black man had used only the guava tree.

The guava tree is a very well-known tree in our islands of the Americas. Those named as such on Santa Catarina Island <note>An island off the coast of Brazil where the expedition stopped for supplies.</note> were no more than eight feet high, and the trunk was seven to eight inches in diameter; I saw none larger. Its bark was a little whiter than that of the apple tree; its branches spread in the same manner, and its fruits, which were very little advanced, resembled apples that have been set for only a month. From the leaves and the shape of the tree, I first took it...

...for a quince tree. <note>original: "coignaffier."
