Furthermore, the Portuguese attribute to theirs the same properties that Father Labat gives to the one in Martinique.

Before embarking, I provided myself with all the ripe seeds of the plants I could find; and having encountered some Portuguese women in a hut who were cleaning cotton to separate it from its seed, they gave me a handful of the latter. They gave me all the more pleasure because I greatly desired to have some, and I would not have been able to gather any from the plants themselves, as the shrub was then only in flower. The wood is soft and spongy; the bark is thin and gray. Its leaves are a bright green when they are new; but this green becomes darker as they approach maturity, or as the shrub grows older. They are large and divided into five parts that end in a point. Those that are closer to the flower are only divided

---

### 217 TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS ###

into three, and look quite similar to those of the castor oil plant. <term>castor oil plant: (Ricinus communis) a plant with distinctive lobed leaves, then often called "ricin" in French.</term> Its flowers are almost similar to those of the small shrub which today serves as decoration for our flowerbeds, and which is called *Althea*: <term>Althea: likely referring to the Rose of Sharon (Hibiscus syriacus), a common ornamental shrub.</term> they are, however, not quite so flared; they appear yellow at the tip and spotted with red at the base. Its petals are five in number, supported by a calyx of small green leaves, hard and pointed. The pistil is succeeded by a bud, or oval fruit, which at maturity is the size of a duck egg. This fruit is divided into three, sometimes four chambers, filled with a white, fibrous substance, which surrounds ten or twelve blackish-brown seeds, attached together in pairs, like wheat in the ear. These seeds are the size of a pea, but three or four lines long. <note>A "line" (ligne) is an archaic French unit of measurement approximately equal to 2.25 millimeters or 1/12th of an inch.</note>

This fibrous substance is what we know by the name of *Cotton*. It swells and acts like a spring within the shell that encloses it, such that it forces it to open when the fruit is ripe. Then the seeds, full of an oily substance, detach with the fluff...

<note>original: "cons" — This is the end of the word "flocons" (flakes or tufts) or "bourrecons" (small pods) from the previous page.</note> tufts that envelop them, and fall from the fruit if one does not take care to gather them beforehand.

The Portuguese are undoubtedly unaware of the machines used in our West Indies <note>original: "Isles Antilles"</note> to separate the cotton from the seeds contained within, to which it is attached; or perhaps the Portuguese women, whom I saw busy at this task, did it as a mere pastime; for they separated it fiber by fiber, pinching it with their fingers alone. They then spin it to make cloth, but I do not know with what instrument; I did not see them at this work.

This cotton plant is the only species I found cultivated on Santa Catarina Island and on the nearby coasts of the mainland. It is quite different from the Brazilian cotton plant mentioned by Dampier <note>William Dampier (1651–1715), an English explorer and naturalist who wrote extensively about his circumnavigations.</note> in these terms: 

> "The flower is composed of small filaments almost as delicate as hair, three or four inches long, and of a dark red; but their tips are ash-colored. At the base of the stem there are five narrow, stiff leaves, six inches long." 

The species mentioned by Frezier <note>Amédée-François Frézier (1682–1773), a French military engineer and explorer who mapped the coasts of South America.</note> is similar in

every way to the one I have described, except that the seeds of the latter are not separated from one another and scattered within the cotton, as this author says, and as he had represented in the illustration of the fruit that he provides. It appears that Father Labat <note>Jean-Baptiste Labat, the Dominican priest mentioned previously, known for his travelogues of the Americas.</note> had the illustration he inserted in the second volume of his *New Voyages to the Islands of America* copied from this figure of the cotton plant; or else Frezier took it from Father Labat. The figures provided by both are absolutely identical.

In a hut a little further away, where we went to take some refreshment, the woman who served us was busy stripping leaves with long, slender spines on their edges, from a type of reed very common along the woods and paths. From these she drew a kind of green and very fine filament, resembling almost scoured silk <term>scoured silk: silk that has had its natural gum (sericin) removed, making it soft and ready for dyeing.</term> dyed a pale green. She told us that she then spun this filamentous substance to make fishing lines and nets, and that they lasted a very long time. Perhaps it could also be employed for other uses.

Not far from there, I also saw for the first time a species of aloe, called *Pithe* <note>original: "Pithe" — This refers to the Pita plant (*Agave americana*), which explorers often called "aloe" due to its similar appearance.</note>, whose leaf is retted <note>Retting is a process of soaking plant stalks to loosen the fibers from the stem.</note> like hemp, and provides a substance suitable for spinning, from which they make cloths in the East.

From the middle of about twenty leaves—which were approximately five feet high, at least three inches thick at the base, thorny along their edges, ending in a point, hollowed out like a channel, and of a beautiful green—there rose a green stem. This stem was about eight inches in diameter at the base, tapering gradually as it rose, and reaching a height of at least thirty feet. At a height of about twenty feet, branches began to emerge from various sides of this stem, continuing up to the summit. These branches, numbering twelve or fifteen, were adorned with clusters of shoots, almost similar to the emerging stem of a lily plant when it is about two inches out of the ground. These tufts of shoots are placed irregularly along these branches, which are devoid of any other foliage and extend almost horizontally. Undoubtedly, when these shoots have acquired a certain maturity, they detach themselves and take root

---

### 221 TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS ###

upon the ground where they have fallen. I collected about fifteen of them with their roots, and I carried them on board, where we planted them in boxes placed on our poop decks <note>original: "dunetes" — the raised deck at the aft of a ship, often used for storage or as an observation area.</note>. They took to the soil very well; and we likely would have preserved the majority of them if, despite all our care, two cats we had on board had not gone during the night to scratch the earth in these boxes and poison it with their urine and excrement.

We realized this, though a bit late, and covered them with fishing nets supported by hoops. We managed to save two plants, as well as some cotton plants grown from the seeds we had sown there. Both were transplanted upon our arrival at Saint-Servan <note>A port town in Brittany, France, near Saint-Malo.</note>.

Perhaps the Portuguese have noticed that the *pithe* shoots which take root on their own do not prosper as well as those one takes care to place in tilled soil. This is apparently what encourages them to make, in the ground beneath the branches and around the base of the plant, holes one foot square, where I found five or six

of these planted shoots, and which indeed seemed to have fared better than those which had been abandoned to the sole care of nature. I do not know if the pita <note>original: "pithe"</note> bears any other fruit, or if it multiplies by other means.

The most singular plant, in my opinion, of Saint Catherine Island, is the one the Brazilians call *Juquiri* and *Caaeo*, and we call the *Sensitive Plant* <note>Likely *Mimosa pudica*, known for its leaves folding inward when touched.</note>. We have seen two species of it; the one I have just mentioned grows its stems to a height of about two feet, branched and with striated, almost four-sided stems, green in color and quite well-furnished with small yellowish thorns. Its leaves are positioned opposite one another on the branches and are supple: the underside is a whitish green, the top a tender green. From along the stem emerge several branches, the tips of which are adorned with a small, round, hairy head of a purplish white. This is the flower, which is followed by a thin, curved, chestnut-colored pod or husk <term>silique: a type of seed capsule</term>, covered with small white hairs when it is ripe.

The second species does not rise far from the ground; I have only seen it in sandy soils along the coast: it
<meta>catchword: it</meta>

---

### 223 TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS ###

seems to creep, and does not differ from the first. Both fold their leaves and let their branches droop almost the moment they are touched by a hand, as if they were withered. A moment later, they reappear in their full vigor. It is from this, no doubt, that it received the names *Chaste Herb* <note>original: "Herbe chaste"</note>, *Chaste Herb* <note>original Latin: "Herba casta"</note>, and *Mimosa*. If the leaves are eaten, they act as a deadly poison, for which no other remedy has been found than the root of the plant itself. These same leaves, when applied as a poultice, heal scrofulous tumors <term>scrofulous tumors: swellings of the lymph nodes, usually associated with a form of tuberculosis</term>.

[In the Indies <note>Referring to the East Indies or India.</note>, there is a sensitive plant that is much the same, which not only bows when any foreign body approaches it, but also follows the course of the sun exactly with its stem, like the heliotropes <note>Plants that turn to face the sun.</note>. A Philosopher of Malabar <note>A region on the southwest coast of India.</note> went mad because he was unable to explain the singularities of this vegetable wonder; a story which recalls the tale told about Aristotle, who supposedly threw himself into the Euripus <note>The Euripus Strait in Greece, famous for its strong and changing currents.</note> because he could not explain the ebb and flow of the tide; but the Teacher of Alexander <note>Aristotle was the tutor of Alexander the Great.</note> was far too enlightened to kill himself out of grief for not being as well-informed as Nature regarding primary causes].

## CHAPTER VI.

### *On the Manners & Customs of the Brazilians.* <note>The author uses "Brazilians" to refer specifically to the indigenous peoples encountered near Rio de Janeiro.</note>

I hold what I am about to say from the First President of the Sovereign Council of Rio de Janeiro, a man full of enlightenment and integrity, who has observed the Brazilians as a Philosopher, and who has governed them in the same spirit.

The laws of each country form the manners of those who inhabit them; this is why the customs of nations are so different from one another. As climate also contributes much to this, a law that is good for Norway would not be so for Guinea. The knowledge acquired among peoples whom we call *Civilized* <note>original: "Policés", meaning "polished" or "governed by civil laws".</note>, has also been the source of many laws that are unknown among those whom it has pleased us to name *Savages*.

Among the Brazilians, before marriage, girls surrender themselves—not only of their own accord and without shame—to free men, but their parents offer them to the first

<meta>catchword: comer ,</meta>

---

<page-num>225</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header>

comer, and greatly caress their lovers; so that there is perhaps not one who enters the state of marriage as a virgin. When they are bound by promises, the sole formality that ties them, people cease to solicit them; they even cease to lend an ear to solicitations of infidelity.

The only education they give to children concerns hunting, fishing, and war. They otherwise live peacefully among themselves, and one very rarely sees private quarrels there. If some do fight, they are left at entire liberty to satisfy themselves; but as the law of retaliation <term>peine du talion: the principle of "an eye for an eye," where the punishment mirrors the crime</term> is rigorously and mercilessly observed, the relatives inflict the same wounds upon the one who has caused injury, and kill him, if he has killed his adversary. All this is done with the very consent of the relatives of both parties, and without protest. This law is likely the source of the implacable hatred they maintain against their declared enemies. If this rule were introduced among us, would we see so many quarrels settled by the shedding of human blood? One would hardly fight except with the tongue or the pen.

<sig>*Volume I.* P</sig>

It is mistaken to view the Brazilians as the most barbaric men of the New World; at the very least, they only show cruelty toward their known enemies. If one excludes a small number of certain nations whose ferocity slightly approaches that of beasts—perhaps due to having been mistreated by their neighbors for too long—the Brazilians are very humane, especially toward Foreigners, whom they welcome with great distinction: here are the details.

If one must go more than once to the same settlement or village, one must stay with the <term>Moussacat</term> <note>A term for the head of a household or family patriarch.</note> or father of the family, because the one to whom you first addressed yourself would be greatly offended if you left him to go to another on a second visit: they are all jealous of the exclusive rights of hospitality.

As soon as the Traveler presents himself at the door, the *Moussacat* urges you to sit in a <term>Hamach</term> <note>A hammock; the text describes it as a bed of cotton suspended in the air.</note> or cotton bed suspended in the air, in which the Traveler is left for some time without a word being said to him: this is to allow time to assem-

---

<page-num>227</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header>
<sig>P ij</sig>

-ble the women, who come to crouch on the ground around the bed, both hands over their eyes.

Deep emotion seizes them; they let fall a few tears of joy, and, still weeping, they address a thousand flattering compliments to their guest.

> "How 
> "good you are! How brave you are! How 
> "indebted we are to you! What trouble 
> "you have taken to come! How handsome 
> "you are! What pleasure you 
> "give us by coming to see us!" 

and other similar exclamations. If the Foreigner wishes to give a good opinion of himself, he must respond with marks of emotion. <term>Léry</term> <note>Jean de Léry (1534–1613), a French explorer and author whose 1578 account of Brazil became a primary source for European knowledge of the region.</note> assures us that he saw Frenchmen truly moved to tears. But he advises those whose hearts are not so susceptible to this impression (that is to say, to the shame of our Europeans, who pride themselves—with so little reason—on having more humanity than the Brazilians) to heave, or pretend to heave, a few sighs. Is this not to reproach us in a few words, showing that we have only the mask of politeness and hospitality, while the Brazilians have the reality?

After this first salutation, the *Mouffacat* <term>Head of the household; likely a transcription of *Moussacat* as seen on the previous page</term>, who had withdrawn into a corner of the hut, pretending to make an arrow or some other work as if he were paying no attention to what was happening, approaches the bed, asks the guest <note>The original French uses *hôte*, which can mean host or guest; here it refers to the traveler.</note> how he is doing, receives his response, and questions him on the reason for his visit. One must answer all these questions when one knows the language. Then, if one has arrived on foot, he has water brought, with which his wives wash the feet and legs of the *Mair*: this is the name they give to Europeans <note>original: "Mair"</note>. Next, he asks if one needs to drink or eat. If one responds that both are desired, he immediately serves everything he has of venison, poultry, fish, and other dishes, along with the drinks of the country.

Does one wish to spend the night in the same place? Not only does the *Mouffacat* have a beautiful white *Inis* (hammock) hung; but, although it is always hot in Brazil, he takes the pretext of the night's humidity to have three or four small fires lit around the hammock, which are maintained during the *Mair’s* sleep with a

---

<page-num>229</page-num>
# TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.

sort of small fan, named a *Tata-pecoun*, which closely resembles our hand-screens.

In the evening, says Léry <note>Jean de Léry (1534–1613), a French explorer whose 1578 account of Brazil is being cited here.</note>, to ensure nothing disturbs the guest's rest, all the children are sent away.

Finally, the *Mouffacat* presents himself upon your waking, comes to ask you if you slept well, and for news of your health. Even when you respond with a satisfied air, he says to you, "Rest
» again, my child, you have
» need of it; for I saw well last night that
» you were tired." It is the custom among Europeans to give them some presents on these occasions; and one should never travel without having something to give them. One therefore provides oneself with some small goods, such as knives, scissors, small tweezers for pulling hair (they are in the habit, both men and women, of plucking the hair from all parts of the body, except the eyebrows), combs, small mirrors, bracelets, small glass beads and buttons, and finally fishhooks for fishing.

One might perhaps doubt this conduct of the Brazilians <note>The author uses "Brasiliens" to refer specifically to the indigenous Tupinamba people encountered by early explorers.</note> regarding foreigners; but one will be easily convinced when it is known that these men, whom we treat as barbarians because of their cruelty toward their enemies, are <term>Anthropophages</term> <note>Literally "man-eaters" or cannibals.</note> only with respect to their declared enemies; that they bear a great affection for their friends and their allies; and that to protect the latter from the slightest displeasure, they would allow themselves to be hacked to pieces.

It is not only toward foreigners that they are tender and affectionate. During their illnesses, the Brazilians treat each other with such humane attention and care that if it is a matter of a wound, a neighbor immediately presents himself to suck that of the patient, and all the duties of friendship are rendered with the same zeal.

Religion, however, plays no part in the ideas of the Brazilians. They know no divinity ($a$); they worship

---

($a$) Let us note that here Dom Pernetty <note>Antoine-Joseph Pernetty (1716–1796), the author of this account and a former Benedictine monk.</note> is merely quoting the traveler Léry <note>Jean de Léry (1534–1613), whose accounts of Brazil were foundational for European views of the Americas.</note>; furthermore, even if the most judicious philosopher told me on this subject, *I have seen it*, I would not

<column-break/>

<page-num>231</page-num>

nothing, and their language does not even have a term that expresses the name or the idea of a God. In their fables, one finds nothing related to their origin or the creation of the world. They only have some history that seems to recall the idea of a deluge <note>A Great Flood myth.</note> which caused all of mankind to perish, with the exception of a brother and a sister who repopulated the earth. They attach some ideas of power to thunder, which they name *Tupan*, since they fear it and believe they receive from him the science of agriculture. It does not occur to them that this life might be followed by another, and they have no terms that express Paradise or Hell. It seems, however, that they think something of them remains after death; for one hears them

---

yet believe it; for nature is more sacred to me than the testimony of the human race itself.

It is with atheists in the moral order, says a modern philosopher, as it is with monsters in the physical order: it is as impossible for a large number of people to agree on denying the existence of God as it is for a mother to constantly give birth to children with two heads; a nation of atheists contradicts the laws of nature more than a nation of hermaphrodites. *Philosophy of Nature, vol. I, page 98. Editor's Note.*

The Indians of Brazil passionately love dogs of European breeds, and they raise them for hunting. Those of the country, though similar to ours, always retain a wild and carnivorous character. A Portuguese man had made us a gift of two of them, one grown and already large, the other still so young it could barely walk. We were forced to dispose of them one after the other; because we noticed that, despite corrections, they were relentless against the sheep and the chickens. The Governor had given <term>Mr. de Bougainville</term> <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville (1729–1811), the famous French explorer leading this expedition to the Falkland Islands.</note> two hunting dogs, only four months old, and of the finest Portuguese breed known.

### CHAPTER VII.
->Route from the Island of Saint Catherine<-

---
On Thursday, December 15, we set sail for the exploration of the Falkland Islands <note>original: *Iſles Malouines*. The French name "Malouines" comes from the sailors of Saint-Malo; the islands were later known in English as the Falklands.</note>; the weather varied greatly during our route, and I relieved the boredom of the journey by studying the peculiarities of Natural History.

During the crossing, I saw several of those birds that sailors call *Dadins* <note>likely referring to a species of booby or petrel</note>, and some *Quebranta-huessos* <note>Spanish for "bone-breaker"; usually refers to the bearded vulture, but in a maritime context, this is the Giant Petrel (*Macronectes giganteus*).</note>, also known as "Sheep." One of these latter birds having approached too close to the side one day, it was killed by a musket shot, and we went to fish it out.

In the South Seas, people are convinced that the *Quebranta-huessos* only shows itself one or two days before a storm. However, we saw a great many of them during the clearest weather, without any storm following afterward. The same tale is repeated regarding the *Halcyons* <note>original: *Alcyons*. These are Storm Petrels, often associated in mythology with "halcyon days," though sailors here had a different view.</note>, which are also called *Stinkers* <note>original: *Puans*.</note>, whether they

<column-break/>

stink in fact, or because people do not like to see them, as they are regarded as birds of ill omen. I must admit, however, that we never saw *Halcyons* without a spell of heavy weather occurring shortly thereafter.

One sees the *Quebranta-huessos* lower themselves and support themselves at the water's surface, skimming the waves and following all their movements without appearing to move their wings, which they always keep spread and extended; when they are not resting on the waves, they flutter around and very close to the ships.

This bird's body is no larger than a sturdy capon; but the long, thick feathers with which it is covered make it appear as large as a turkey <note>original: *coq-d’Inde*, literally "cock of India," the standard 18th-century French term for a turkey.</note>. Its neck is short and slightly curved; its head large, and its beak very singular. I have painted it, and it can be seen in *Plate VIII, figure 3.*

This beak is as if divided into four or five pieces. It has a short tail, a high back, low legs, and black, webbed feet; it has three toes on the front, and a fourth, very short one, on the back.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the voyage to the Falkland Islands, focusing on the author's observations of seabirds like the Giant Petrel and Storm Petrel to pass the time. He provides a detailed physical description of the Giant Petrel and corrects common maritime superstitions regarding their appearance as omens of storms.</summary>

one and all are armed with black claws, which are blunt and not very long.

There are several species of *Quebranta-huessos* <note>Spanish for "bone-breaker." While this name usually refers to the bearded vulture, in maritime accounts of this era, it refers to the Southern Giant Petrel (*Macronectes giganteus*).</note>. Some have whitish plumage, spotted with dark brown or reddish-brown; others have a white breast, underwings, lower neck, and head, but a reddish-brown back, upper wings, and upper neck, speckled with some bluish-gray marks. Such was the one we killed. Perhaps they differ only by sex and not by species. They all have very long wings. Those of our specimen measured seven feet, two inches from the tip of one wing to the tip of the other. They are found more than 300 leagues <note>A league was roughly 3 miles, placing these birds nearly 1,000 miles from any coast.</note> from any land, and it is not known where they retreat to or where they make their nests.

* On the 22nd, we entered the <term>River Plate</term> <note>original: *riviere de la Plata*.</note>, and we recognized <term>Wolf Island</term> <note>original: *l'Isle Lobos*. Named for the abundance of South American sea lions and fur seals found there.</note>, which appears as shown in *Plate VI. fig. 1.* It was so named because of the sea lions that are there in abundance. We anchored near the Island of Mal-

<column-break/>

<page-num>237</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header>

donado <note>The text refers to the "Isle of Maldonade," likely referring to the coastal settlement of Maldonado, Uruguay, or the nearby Gorriti Island.</note> to take on water and gather provisions. The Commandant of the Fort welcomed our deputies very graciously and granted all their requests.

The entire coast of the Island presents low sand dunes, and in the distance, one can see only a few heights called the <term>Maldonado mountains</term>, located several leagues from the coast. No trees are seen there, but many herds of very large oxen and horses. Silver and ox hides also constitute the entire trade of the <term>River Plate</term> region.

We occupied ourselves a great deal with fishing in these waters, and with success; for no sooner was the line in the sea than it was pulled back with a fish caught. Often we took as many fish as there were hooks on the line. There were only four or five kinds. Some were those the Spaniards call *Viagrios* and our sailors call *Catfish* <note>original: *Machoirans*. A common term in early French natural history for various species of catfish.</note>. The others were *Carangues* <note>Jacks or Crevalle fish.</note>, *Dogfish* <note>original: *Roussettes*.</note>, *Damselfish* <note>original: *Demoiselles*.</note>, and *Sharks*. We caught one dogfish, one damselfish, and two small sharks.

The Machoiran <note>A type of sea catfish (*Genidens genidens*), typical of South American estuaries and known for its whisker-like barbels.</note> has a flat belly and several barbels, like a Barbel <note>A common European freshwater fish.</note>; its head is large, and its skin is covered with small, brown, almost imperceptible scales, much like those of a Tench; at the base of the fins, near the head, is a spine shaped like a saw, whose teeth are inclined toward the body. This spine is as long as the fin and moves in the same way. When this fish wants to defend itself from other fish or from a fisherman, it raises these spines and drives them into the bodies of other fish, or into the hand of whoever is catching it, even into wood if it can, and remains attached there. This sting is venomous. Consequently, the fishermen stay on their guard when they are fishing. I do not know if there are any larger than those we caught. The largest was a foot and a half long by four inches wide. This fish has an excellent flavor.

A very large quantity of a species of *Sea Bass* <note>original: *Bar*. Likely the Patagonian sea bass or a similar local variety.</note> was also caught at the same time, enough to supply the entire crew for two days; they decided to salt the

---

<page-num>239</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS. 239</header>

rest and dry it in the manner in which dried Cod or Hake <note>original: *Morue* or *Merluche*.</note> is prepared in Newfoundland. The largest of these Sea Bass was of the same thickness and length as the Machoiran. The *Rouffete* <note>Small-spotted catshark.</note> and the *Demoiselle* <note>Likely a nurse shark or similar small shark species.</note> are species of sharks; they resemble them so closely that it is easy to mistake them by their appearance; they are about two and a half feet long.

On the 24th, at the time we were setting sail for Montevideo, a most violent storm arose; nothing more beautiful can be seen than the spectacle presented to us by the continuous and countless lightning bolts, which shot out from between the clouds as they rose on the horizon. The sky was all on fire; even the most expertly composed, sustained, and varied fireworks display has nothing comparable to what the horizon presented to us for an hour. We did not suspect then that we would soon see another, far less satisfying one. But our Captain, who knew the danger and the consequences better, occupied himself during that time with our

to take cover from it. He had all the yards <note>The horizontal spars on a mast from which sails are hung.</note> hauled into the wind and lowered them, as well as the topmasts and topgallant masts; all the sails were also clewed up and furled.

We hoped that the storm would pass by us: it indeed appeared to be taking that path; but in an instant the most impetuous wind assailed us; lightning and thunder overtook us, and we had all the trouble in the world to strike down the fore-topgallant mast. Once all these precautions were taken, we remained on our two cables struggling all night against the impetuosity of this wind and the roars of an extremely angry sea, which threatened at every moment to submerge us.

In this country they call this wind *Pamperos*, because it comes from the plains of the *Pampas*, beyond Buenos Aires. These plains extend as far as the Andes <note>original: *Cordillieres*.</note>, which separate them from Chile. They span at least three hundred leagues, without any woods or heights that could break the fury of this wind. It swells the River Plate <note>original: *riviere de la Plata*.</note>, raising waves like moun-

<meta>catchword: tains,</meta>

---

<page-num>241</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS. 241</header>

tains, and often causes the ships found there to perish by running them aground on the nearby coast opposite the wind. The anchorage where we were is one of the worst, due to the proximity of the Island of Maldonado <note>original: *Isle de Maldonat*. Today known as Isla de Gorriti, off the coast of Punta del Este, Uruguay.</note> and the surrounding coasts, all lined with rocks and reefs. An English ship, carrying *piastres*, or pieces of eight <note>The Spanish silver *real de a ocho*, a standard international currency at the time.</note>, was lost there thirty years ago. The inhabitants of the island near the place where it was wrecked still seek today to save a portion of that cargo. They had recovered two thousand four hundred of them with a dredge the day before our arrival.

The *Pamperos* wind is much more frequent in winter than in summer, and always blows with violence; this makes the Río de la Plata a very dangerous place of call at all times. It is only good for the trade of pieces of eight and cattle, the largest of which are sold there for five pieces of eight, or twenty-five *livres* of our money. Ordinarily, their price is three *piastres*, or fifteen *livres*. It is very difficult to gather wood there, both because it is extremely rare, and

### CHAPTER VIII.

->Stay at Montevideo, and a Digression<-
->on the Jesuits.<-

As we were about to enter the bay, the Captain of a Spanish ship named the *Saint Barbara* came on behalf of the Governor of the country to offer his services and act as our pilot; thanks to his skill, we entered without danger and saluted the citadel with twelve cannon shots, which were returned to us shot for shot.

During the first days of our stopover, we were occupied only with the arrangements to be made with the Governor of *Montevideo* to maintain good relations with him during our stay. At first, he seemed to find many difficulties, both in allowing us to fish along the coast and in letting our longboat and our skiff come ashore. He required that he be given prior notice every time we wished to send them to land, so that he might put

   Not imagining we would encounter such difficulties, on the day after our anchoring, we dispatched our small boat to fish at the foot of the Mount <note>original: "le Mont." This refers to the Cerro de Montevideo, the prominent hill on the west side of the bay from which the city takes its name.</note>. The Governor, who was alerted to this, gave the order for two dragoons <note>Mounted infantry soldiers</note> from the garrison to go there and seize the men, the boat, and any merchandise, if any had been unloaded. Messrs. de Bougainville, de Nerville, Guyot, and I arrived at the Government house just a moment after this order had been given, which Monsieur de Bougainville was informed of. The Governor, who no doubt feared he could not express himself well in French, spoke in the Spanish language, and used as an interpreter a man from Provence <note>A region in Southern France; the interpreter was a fellow countryman of the travelers who had lived in Montevideo for fifteen years.</note> who had been settled in the city for about fifteen years. This Provençal man translated the Governor's intentions in such a way as to make us understand that he was not disposed to offer us all the services he had promised, and which we had reason to expect from him. This was not, however, his actual way of thinking; and he proved senti-

The Governor replied that his intention was not to be unhelpful; but that the laws and orders of his Court <note>The Spanish Royal Court</note> were to allow no trade to ships that were not Spanish, or authorized by his Court for that purpose, nor even to those of his own compatriots who were merely acting as agents for other nations; he added that a frigate of the East India Company, having anchored three years prior in the same port, had made no difficulty in submitting to what he had just proposed. "There is a great difference," replied M. de Bougainville, "between a frigate..."

We have no merchandise; and we have only come to take on fresh supplies <note>original: "rafraîchissemens" — refers to fresh food, water, and livestock needed to prevent scurvy after long sea voyages.</note> and to wait for the frigate *Sphinx*, from which we were separated, and for which we have set a rendezvous in the Rio de la Plata. — "As soon as you assure me that no merchandise will be landed, you are free to come ashore and to send men there as often as you wish. Furthermore, you can count on the uprightness of my intentions; for, independently of the orders I have to treat the French with the same regard as the Spaniards, I am inclined to do so by my own disposition." The Governor had more than one reason to act this way: he told us some of them; the others were not difficult to guess. Don Joseph-Joachim de Viana (this is the name of this Governor), currently aged (in 1763) about forty-eight years, a Knight of the Order of Calatrava <note>A prestigious Spanish military-religious order dating back to the 12th century.</note>, Brigadier of the Armies of His Catholic Majesty <note>A traditional title for the King of Spain.</note>, was charged by the King of Spain with the command of the troops sent to Paraguay against the Indians. These Indians, at the instigation, it is said, of the Jesuit Fathers—who are despots in those regions—had revolted and refused to submit to the arrangements made by the Courts of Spain and Portugal to fix the boundaries of their respective possessions <note>This refers to the "War of the Seven Reductions" or the Guaraní War (1756), where the transfer of land between the Spanish and Portuguese crowns led to an uprising of the indigenous population and their Jesuit protectors.</note>. Don de Viana behaved as a faithful subject, and all his operations met with a happy success, despite the obsta-

The Governor knew this well, yet he did not become more "political" <note>original: "politique" — here meaning cunning, diplomatic, or cautious for the sake of self-interest</note> because of it (a).

These military monks <note>The Jesuit Order, or the Society of Jesus, was known for its disciplined, hierarchical structure resembling a military organization.</note> have a hospice in Montevideo where two Priests and a Lay Brother reside; they, like their associates, always have their eyes open to spy on what is happening and to keep watch over the Governor's conduct. The Governor of Buenos Aires, who is the Governor General of Paraguay, favors the society in everything and has no qualms about being their slave, serving as an instrument for their vengeance. Informed of the discord

---

(a) Upon our return to Paris, M. de Grimaldi, the Spanish Ambassador to France, asked many questions of M. de Bougainville regarding the conduct this Governor showed toward us. This Commander, having through his answers done justice to the probity of Don Joseph de Viana and his devotion to his Prince, the Ambassador admitted that the Jesuits and their friends had sent reports to Madrid accusing this Governor in order to harm his standing with the King and have him recalled. M. de Grimaldi subsequently cleared Don de Viana, and the Gazettes have informed us that this Governor has been continued in his post.

He is a man estimable in every respect; a man of wit, full of knowledge in the military arts, filled with probity, and having none of the arrogance <note>original: "hauteur" — often used to describe a sense of superiority or disdain</note> that is sometimes held against the Spaniards; he has acquired the esteem and consideration of all those who know him. There is but one voice concerning him, and even the Jesuits are forced to give him their approval, at least in public.

These Fathers number more than sixty in their House in Buenos Aires. The hospice in Montevideo is only a small house, without distinction, separated from those of the other inhabitants by a small bell placed in an arch about three feet high, raised on one end of the roof of the house. # HISTORY OF A VOYAGE

Two or three days after this conversation, I went to see an Ecclesiastic, the Chaplain of a Spanish frigate anchored in the Port of Buenos Aires for five months; I knew him to be very well-disposed toward the Jesuits. It was even said quite openly that he had been sent by them to Montevideo to buy up as much as he could of the <term>trade goods (pacotilles)<note>Small quantities of goods that officers or passengers were permitted to carry and sell for their own profit.</note></term> that were found on our frigate. He did, in fact, purchase everything that anyone wished to sell him.

After the first greeting, he asked me why I had not been to see the Jesuit Fathers, who had sent for me and to whom I had promised a visit. It is true, I told him, I did promise; but I have been assured that one of these Fathers has recently spoken very ill of the King of France, my Master, in a sermon he preached in Buenos Aires; and if this fact is true, it is not fitting for a good Frenchman like me to go and see the colleagues of such a reckless preacher. at this sermon, I added.—Yes, I was there; it is true that this Father did not mince his words.—What did he say specifically about the King of France?—That he is a tyrant and a persecutor of the Church. <note>The Jesuits were suppressed and expelled from France in 1764, shortly before this voyage, leading to great animosity between the Order and the French Crown.</note> But they must be forgiven: it is the result of the resentment these Fathers feel over their expulsion from France.

Hardly had he finished speaking when two of the three Jesuits from Montevideo entered the room where we were. After greeting us, one of the two Jesuits addressed me and expressed his surprise that I had not come to their hospice <note>A residence or guesthouse for members of a religious order.</note>: I have told the reason to the Father Chaplain, I replied; and he can tell it to you.—Oh! My answer was not to his liking; he did not add another word, bowed to us, and withdrew.

The Jesuits are not men to be discouraged by an epigram; they sought—

sought to connect themselves even more with our Frenchmen; one evening M. de Belcourt, who had taken lodgings in the city, found himself in the company of an unknown man, perhaps in disguise, and who spoke a French-Gascon <note>A dialect from Southwestern France, often associated in literature with clever or boastful characters.</note>. The latter replied that policy might find its advantage there: that if he wished, through this same policy, to sacrifice himself for the good of the State, it would perhaps be appropriate to listen to these proposals. M. de Belcourt then told him that, in the event he took this path, it would be necessary for him, M. de Bougainville, to give him a Certificate, stating that he went only with his consent, and for the presumed good of the State.

The next day, the same unknown man renewed the same proposals to M. de Belcourt with more urgency, telling him to decide promptly: that he should not worry about taking his clothes and effects; that they would provide him with everything necessary; and so that the Spanish Government would have no knowledge of it, they would lead him by unknown paths to the place where he would be established. M. de Belcourt asked him what were the place and the proposed advantages; but the unknown man having refused to specify anything, and even to better hide his game, no doubt, having spoken to him in a tone little favorable to the Jesuits, M. de Belcourt declared to him that he would not yield to his solicitations. 

But, as he had to fear retaliation, he kept on his guard. That same evening, at nightfall, he found himself so closely pressed by three men that he felt obliged to draw his sword, and to carry it out of the scabbard, to make a passage for himself, had they surrounded him: which they did not do. I will conclude what I have to say regarding the Jesuits with some reflections on the work of Mr. Muratori <note>Lodovico Antonio Muratori (1672–1750), an Italian historian who wrote "Il Cristianesimo felice nelle missioni de' padri della Compagnia di Gesù nel Paraguai," a work generally favorable to the Jesuit missions.</note> regarding Paraguay.

This Author worked only from the Memoirs provided to him by the Society <note>The Society of Jesus, or the Jesuits.</note> or by friends of these Religious men—people who were interested in not informing the public of everything that happens there. Spanish Officers of great integrity, sent by the Court of Madrid to Paraguay at the time of the division of the respective possessions of the Courts of Spain and Portugal <note>Likely referring to the Treaty of Madrid in 1750, which sought to settle border disputes between the two empires in South America.</note>, have assured me that all the printed materials they have seen regarding the conduct of the Jesuits in that Country—both concerning the Indians and the interests of these two Crowns—were written with a great deal of restraint in favor of the Jesuits. They told me that one of these Fathers, one of the principal figures of that Country, had made the following reply in his presence to one of the Spanish General Officers, who was expressing his surprise at the obstacles that his Society was placing in the way of the execution of the arrange-

> *“I have much more reason to be astonished*

I leave it to the reader to imagine what the conduct of the Jesuits must be, given such principles. It is certain that the Indians of Paraguay obey only the Jesuits, whether in peace or in war. Recently, when the Spaniards besieged and took from the Portuguese the Colony of the Blessed Sacrament <note>Colônia do Sacramento, a disputed territory in modern-day Uruguay.</note>, which is about thirty leagues from Montevideo, the Spaniards had in their aid about a thousand Indians, at the head of whom was a Jesuit Father who commanded them as chief, and without whose orders these Indians would not have moved a step, nor fired a single shot. ### CHAPTER IX.
---

->Reunion of the *Sphinx* & the *Eagle*.<-

On Saturday the 31st, we sighted a ship in the open sea, and it was first judged by the course it was taking that it was bound for Buenos Aires. But, as we were expecting from day to day the corvette *Sphinx* <note>The *Sphinx* was a 20-gun corvette accompanying Bougainville's ship, the *Aigle* (Eagle), on this expedition to colonize the islands.</note>, which had been given a rendezvous at the Rio de la Plata <note>The River Plate, the wide estuary between modern-day Argentina and Uruguay.</note>, we soon suspected that it was her. As the ship advanced, she was observed with greater attention; and finally, we were confirmed in this pleasant idea. M. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville (1729–1811), the French explorer leading the expedition.</note> immediately dispatched the longboat to enable them to sail up the river without danger; the Officers who manned it were given rockets and powder to carry out signals, and they departed around seven o'clock. However, the night became pitch black, the winds contrary, and the sea heavy; so that, having not perceived their signals, we fell into great anxiety. The *Sphinx* had recognized...

M. de la Giraudais <note>Nicolas-Pierre Duclos-Guyot de la Giraudais, the captain of the *Sphinx*.</note> had been forewarned of the errors in the Maps regarding the position of the Brazilian coast; but although we were on our guard, we had very nearly run aground on a shoal that is not marked on the French Charts. This shoal lay on its route, just as it had been encountered on ours: the Abrolhos <note>The Abrolhos Bank is a hazardous area of coral reefs and shoals off the coast of Brazil, notorious among 18th-century mariners for being poorly charted.</note> also do not have on the Charts all the extent they have in reality; all this gave us great reason to be worried about the delay of its arrival, especially after the stay we had made at St. Catherine’s Island <note>Santa Catarina Island, a frequent stopping point for supplies and repairs on the coast of Brazil.</note>.

coast of Brazil; because, despite the mistrust they had of the Charts, they had struck the Abrolhos at the time when they thought they were still at least thirty leagues away. They found themselves upon them in the middle of the night; fortunately the weather was calm, and the rock upon which they struck was of clayey stone.

The *Sphinx* having come to a stop on this rock, those aboard, to avoid the unfortunate consequences of a shipwreck, hastily put the longboat and the canoe to sea; and after having thoroughly inspected the ship, they were somewhat relieved of their anxiety when they saw that it was not damaged.

Another difficulty. It was necessary to pull the *Sphinx* off this rock: as soon as day broke, they saw themselves surrounded by similar reefs; and at half a quarter of a league they perceived a ship without masts, lying on its side. Judging then that being on the Abrolhos, they were not far from land, M. de la Giraudais dispatched the boat toward the coast to get help. They encountered several Pi-

Each cloud that rose from the horizon brought a new squall <note>Original: "grain"—a sudden, sharp increase in wind speed often accompanied by rain, typical of tropical and maritime weather.</note>, always more violent than those which had preceded it. All these assaults combined, which significantly stirred up the waters, formed waves that grew larger and larger and delayed our progress. Despite the sea and the headwind, we had already reached within musket-shot <note>A common contemporary measurement for distance, roughly 150 to 250 yards.</note> of the *Sphinx*; but in the profound darkness that reigned then, we did not perceive it; we saw only a small boat that was bearing down on us. As the waves swept it toward our side with violence, we soon recognized our small skiff at the mercy of the waves, but no one was inside. The desire to save it made us change our course; we went to meet it, we reached it, threw two men inside with oars and a grapnel <note>A small anchor with several flukes used for dragging the seabed or securing small boats.</note>, and we prepared to resume our route. The darkness prevented us from distinguishing the land, and we could barely make out the lanterns that had been placed on our two frigates.

Seeing, therefore, that we were moving further and further away instead of approaching them, we decided to head for land, steering toward where we presumed the city might be; for we could only judge its position by two lights very distant from one another. The waves, which came crashing against the boat, had already let in a great deal of water, which we baled out with our hats; we ourselves were drenched, and the boatmen were very exhausted. M. de la Giraudais, after having rowed for nearly an hour, had taken the helm; we did not know where we were, and we had no brandy <note>Original: *eau-de-vie*, literally "water of life." This occurs when the anchor fails to hold the bottom and the vessel continues to drift with the wind or current.</note>. He told the Master Boatman to put his hand on the hawser <note>Original: *hansiere*. A heavy rope used for anchoring or mooring.</note>, to judge by the vibration if we were indeed dragging. The Master Boatman thought at first that the movement he felt was the effect of the jolts the boat received from the waves; but soon after, he recognized his error and gave warning. We pulled in the oars, raised the grapnel, and rowed for nearly a quarter of an hour, constantly sounding, and always finding the same bottom. Finally, a muddy bottom appeared, with seven or eight feet of water. We were going to anchor there, when the boatmen, foreseeing that they would find nothing there for supper, said that since they were already moving, they should continue and go to sleep on land. A moment later, as everyone looked around to get their bearings, we caught sight of a schooner <term>goelette: a small, fast sailing vessel with two or more masts</term>, which we knew was not anchored very far out. The sight of this vessel revived our courage; and such efforts were made that about a long half-hour later, we arrived at the port. The Officer of the guard presented himself to identify us. Another Officer, along with our Master Boatswain <term>Maître Canotier: the petty officer in charge of the ship's small boats and their crews</term>, were sent to inform the Governor of our return to the City; because we had been unable to reach our own ship. Soon after, he appeared in person, and not daring to refuse him, we made our way to the Government House.

This storm made itself felt with more fatal consequences about two cannon-shots' distance <note>A common maritime measurement of the period, referring to the effective range of a ship's cannon, roughly one to two miles.</note> out from our frigates. Lightning struck the Spanish ship *Sainte Barbe*, which had been anchored there for two days to be better positioned to leave the river at the first favorable wind. In this disaster, one man was killed and fourteen wounded; besides that, its mizzenmast <term>mât d'artimon: the mast furthest toward the back of the ship</term> was shattered.

### CHAPTER X.

#### On the Laws, Manners, and Customs of Montevideo.

**M**ONTEVIDEO is, in a sense, a new Colony. Not twenty-five years ago, one saw nothing there but a few huts. It is, however, the only somewhat convenient place for the anchoring of ships traveling up the Rio de la Plata <note>Original: "Rio de la Plata" (River of Silver), the wide estuary between Uruguay and Argentina.</note>. Today it is a small town, which grows more beautiful every day. The streets there are laid out in straight lines <note>Original: "tirées au cordeau," meaning laid out with a surveyor's line for perfect straightness.</note>, and are wide enough for three carriages to pass abreast. A view of it will be found, which I have depicted as it appeared from on board the frigate *l'Aigle* <note>The Eagle</note>, during our anchorage between the mount and the town. See *Plate VI. fig. 2.*

The houses there have only a ground floor under the roof framing. I except only one, situated in the Great Square, belonging to the Engineer who had it built and who makes it his residence. It has one upper floor and a sort of attic, with a

<column-break/>

<page-num>271</page-num>
# TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.

rather long projection, which supports a balcony in the middle of the facade. The plan of this city can be seen in *Plate VI. fig. 3.*

Each middle-class house is usually composed of a hall, which serves as an entrance, a few bedrooms, and a kitchen—the only place where there is a fireplace and where a fire is made. These houses are thus essentially a ground floor fourteen or fifteen feet high, including the roof space. The entrance room of the Governor is a rectangular <term>quarré-long: an archaic term for a rectangle, literally a "long square"</term> hall, which receives daylight only through a single, rather small window, with glazing that is half paper and half glass; the bottom of the window frame is closed by woodwork. This hall may be fifteen feet wide by eighteen feet long. From there, one passes into the Drawing Room, which is almost square, having more depth than width. At the back, opposite the single window that illuminates it, one sees a sort of six-foot platform <note>Original: "estrade," a raised area of the floor often used to denote status.</note> covered with Tiger skins <note>In the 18th century, European explorers often used the term "tigre" (tiger) to refer to the South American jaguar.</note>. In the middle is an armchair for the Governor’s Wife, and on each side six stools upholstered, like the armchair,

<page-num>273</page-num>

come and sit on the stools near her. 

These halls are, moreover, generally speaking, without wooden flooring or tiling. From the interior, one can see the reeds that support the roof tiles. 

The Spaniards of Montevideo are quite idle; they occupy themselves with little more than conversing together, taking maté, and smoking a cigar *(a)*.

---

*(a) Pipes are not used in Montevideo, nor in the Spanish settlements in America. They smoke what the French of the West Indies call "smoking by the end" <note>Original: "fumer en bout."</note>. These ends, which the Spaniards call Cigars, or *Cigales*, or *Sigares*, are small cylinders six to seven inches long and five to six lines <note>A "line" (ligne) was a traditional French unit of measurement equal to 1/12 of an inch, or about 2.25 millimeters.</note> in diameter, composed of tobacco leaves rolled over one another from the base to the tip. Those I saw manufactured in Montevideo are made of only two or three leaves at most. They are rolled very loosely, so as to leave a free passage for the smoke through the spaces between them. Ordinarily, the two ends are tied with a bit of thread, which prevents the leaf from unrolling; and care is taken, when finishing the cylinder, to moisten the final edge that completes the roll with a little very thin flour paste. One lights one end of this cylinder and holds the other in the mouth to then inhale the smoke, just as one does with an ordinary pipe.*

A Spaniard never walks without his supply of cigars, which he places in bundles in a kind of small hunting pouch, or a bag made of scented skin, a little larger than our pur- <note>The text likely continues as "portefeuilles" (wallets or cases).</note>

<meta>Continuity: The catchword "nos por-" suggests the author is comparing the cigar pouches to European pocketbooks or wallets.</meta>

Merchants and a very small number of artisans are the only people busily em-

---

<note>This section continues from the bottom of the previous page, likely discussing the Governor's education or social graces.</note>
...ployed. He never fails, especially when rising from the table, to offer cigars to his guests.

The smoke from these is much milder than that drawn through the stem of a pipe. I imagine that the tobacco from which these cigars are made is of a milder species than that which they make into *andouilles* <term>andouilles: tobacco leaves twisted into a thick, rope-like coil or "twist," often shaped like a spindle</term> for taking as snuff; or perhaps they give it a preparation that softens it. This consists, I believe, in soaking the leaf in water for a few hours before rolling it. Our sailors, who smoked the *andouille* tobacco, complained of its acridity and said that the smoke from this tobacco peeled the skin from their throats.

The Spaniards do not give tobacco the same preparation as the Portuguese of Brazil; thus, it is not nearly as good. The Portuguese, in spinning it like a cord—the thickness of which does not exceed an inch in diameter—moisten it with a little seawater mixed with sugar-cane syrup; this keeps it oily and fresh. That of the Spaniards is always extremely dry; the *andouilles* or spindles weigh a pound and a half or two pounds. The Portuguese put their spun tobacco into *Rolles* <note>original: "Rolle"</note>. To do this, they wind it around a piece of wood as thick as a wrist, as is done in France with the type known by the names *smoking tobacco* or *canteen tobacco*. These rolls weigh from ten to two hundred pounds and are wrapped in green or raw hide.

---

Although Brazilian tobacco is perhaps the most excellent there is, no one—at least to French taste—takes worse snuff than the Portuguese of that country. They do not grate it; they cut it into small pieces as if they intended to smoke it in a pipe. They then place it on an iron or copper plate, sup-

<column-break/>

<page-num>275</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header>
<sig>S ij</sig>

-ployed <note>original: "occupés"</note> in Montevideo. There are no visible shops, nor signs to announce them; one is certain to find one upon entering a house situated at the corner formed by the intersection of two streets. The same merchant sells wine, brandy, fabric, linens, hardware, etc.

The terrain surrounding Montevideo is a plain as far as the eye can see. The soil is black and strong, and it produces abundantly as soon as it is given even the lightest cultivation. It lacks only farmers to make it one of the best countries in the world. The air there is healthy, the sky beautiful; the heat is not excessive. Wood, however, is scarce, and can only be found along the rivers.

After that, it is pounded in a mortar and sifted; and to remove the unpleasant burnt smell it acquires by drying in this manner, they mix in various floral scents, each according to their particular taste. The Portuguese preferred the tobacco we had brought from France to their own. M. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville (1729–1811), the French admiral and explorer leading the expedition.</note> presented two pounds of it to the Governor of Saint Catherine Island in a porcelain case mounted in silver.

The Spaniards of Montevideo are dressed much like the Portuguese of Saint Catherine Island; but they quite commonly wear white hats with turned-down brims of an immoderate size.

The women there are quite well-formed in terms of stature and figure; but one could not truthfully tell them that they have a complexion of lilies and roses; their faces are tanned, and commonly their teeth are missing or are not white.

Their clothing consists, on the outside, of a white or colored bodice, without much adjustment; it follows the proportions of the waist, and its basques <term>basques: the extension of a bodice or jacket below the waistline</term> descend four fingers' width onto the petticoat. This petticoat is made of a fabric more or less rich, according to the means or the whim of the wearer. It is bordered with a braid or a fringe of silver, gold, or silk, sometimes in a double row; but without furbelows <term>furbelows: gathered or pleated ruffles used to decorate garments</term>. They do not wear any headdresses of linen or lace. A single ribbon, passed around the head, keeps their hair gathered at the crown; from there, passing over the back of the head, it falls in two or

---

<page-num>277</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS.</header>
<note>The Malouines Islands are now more commonly known as the Falkland Islands.</note>

three braids down the back; sometimes as far as the garter. The longest braids seem the most beautiful to them.

When they go out, they place over their head a piece of fine fabric, white and made of wool, bordered with a braid of gold, silver, or silk. This piece of fabric, which they call an *iquella* <note>original: "iquella"; likely a corruption of the Quechua word *lliclla*, a traditional Andean rectangular shoulder shawl</note> or *mantilla*, also covers the shoulders and arms, and descends to just below the waist. They cross the two ends over the chest, or pass them under the arms, as our French ladies do with their short mantles. When they wear this kind of veil inside the house, they usually do not pass it over the head (a). But, in the streets and at the Church, they arrange it over their head in such a way that one sees only one eye and the nose; and it is then impossible to recognize them.

Women in their own homes enjoy at least as much liberty as in France. They receive company with very good grace, and do not need to be begged to sing—

There is, however, a very lively and
very lascivious dance that is sometimes danced at
Montevideo; it is called the *Calenda*, and the
Black people as well as the Mulattoes <term>Mulattoes: a historical term used to describe people of mixed African and European ancestry</term>, whose
temperaments are fiery, love it
to the point of madness.

This dance was brought to America
by the Black people from the kingdom of Ardra <note>Ardra: a historical West African kingdom located on the coast of present-day Benin</note> on
the coast of Guinea. The Spaniards
dance it just as they do in all their settle-
ments in America, without the
slightest scruple. It is, however,
of an indecency that astonishes those who
do not see it habitually. The
taste for it is so general and so keen that even
children practice it as soon as they
can stand on their feet.

The calenda is danced to the sound of instru-
ments and voices. The performers are arranged
in two lines, one in front of the other,
the men facing the women. The
spectators form a circle around the dan-
cers and the instrumentalists. Someone
among the performers sings a song,
the refrain of which is repeated by the specta-
tors with the clapping of hands. All
the dancers then hold their arms halfway—

raised, they jump, turn, make contortions of the backside, approach within two feet of one another, and retreat in cadence, until the sound of the instrument, or the tone of the voice, warns them to draw closer. Then they strike their bellies against each other two or three times in succession, and move away after spinning to start the same movement again, with very lascivious gestures, as many times as the instrument or the voice gives the signal. From time to time they interlace their arms, and make two or three turns, continuing to strike their bellies, and giving each other kisses, but without losing the cadence.

One can judge how much our French upbringing would be astonished by such a suggestive dance. However, travel accounts assure us that it has such charms even for the Spaniards of America, and that its use is so well established among them, that it even enters into their acts of devotion: they dance it in the Church and in their Processions: even the Nuns hardly fail to dance it on Christmas night on a stage

raised in their choir, opposite the grille <term>grille: a metal screen in a convent that separates the cloistered nuns from the public part of the church</term>, which they keep open to share the spectacle with the people; this sacred *calenda* is distinguished from the profane only because the men do not dance with the nuns.

The Governor and the Military are dressed in the French style, but they neither curl nor powder their hair, just like the women. They also live in great idleness.

The common people, the Mulattoes <term>Mulattoes: a historical term used in this period to describe people of mixed African and European ancestry</term> and the Black people, instead of a cloak, wear a piece of fabric striped in bands of different colors, slit only in the middle to pass the head through. It falls over the arms and covers down to the wrists. At the front and back it descends below the calf of the leg, and is fringed all around. It is given the name <term>Poncho</term> or <term>Chony</term>. All wear it on horseback, and find it much more convenient than the cloak and the overcoat. The Governor showed us one, embroidered in gold and silver, which cost him three hundred and some odd piastres <term>piastre: a silver Spanish coin, often referred to as a "piece of eight"</term>.

# HISTORY OF A VOYAGE

They make them in Chile at a price of two thousand <note>likely referring to a local currency like pesos or reals, indicating that high-quality ponchos were luxury items</note>; and it is from that country that the use of them was borrowed by Montevideo.

The <term>poncho</term> <note>a traditional South American garment made of a single piece of fabric with a central slit for the head</note> protects from the rain, does not blow about in the wind, serves as a blanket at night, and as a rug in the countryside. All these figures can be seen in *Plate XV.*

The way of life of the Spaniards is very simple. The men who are not occupied with trade get up very late, as do the women. They then remain with their arms crossed until they take a fancy to go smoke a <term>cigar</term> <note>Original: "cigale," which means cicada; this is almost certainly a printer's error for "cigare"</note> with their neighbors. One often finds four or five of them, standing at the door of a house, chatting and smoking. Others mount their horses and go for, not a stroll across the plains, but a turn around the street. If the whim takes them, they dismount, join the company they encounter, chat for two hours without saying anything, smoke, take some <term>maté</term> <note>an infusion made from the leaves of the yerba maté plant, shared socially</note>, and get back on their horses. It is generally very rare for a Spaniard to walk, and one sees as many horses in the streets as men.

---

# TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS.

During the morning, the women remain seated on a stool at the back of their parlor, having under their feet first a reed mat on the floor; and over this mat, indigenous cloaks or "tiger" skins <note>The term "tiger" was commonly used by early European travelers to describe the South American jaguar</note>. There they play the guitar or some other instrument, accompanying themselves with their voices, or take some <term>maté</term>, while the Black women <note>Original: "Négresses," referring here to enslaved African or Afro-descendant women</note> prepare dinner in their quarters.

Around twelve-thirty or one o'clock, dinner is served, consisting of beef prepared in various ways, but always with plenty of chili pepper and saffron. Sometimes they serve mutton stews, which they call *Carnero* <note>Spanish for mutton or ram</note>, and sometimes fish, but rarely poultry. Game is abundant there; but the Spaniards are not hunters; the exercise would tire them. The dessert is composed of preserves.

Immediately after dinner, masters and slaves alike take what they call *la Siesta* <note>a midday rest or nap taken during the hottest part of the day</note>, which is to say they undress, lie down, and sleep for two or three hours. The laborers, who live only by the work of

their hands, they do not deny themselves these hours of rest. This significant portion of the day lost is the reason they perform little work, and this is what makes labor excessively expensive. Perhaps this inertia also stems from the fact that money is very common there.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It is not surprising that they are indolent. Meat costs them only the trouble of killing, skinning, and butchering the bull to prepare it. Bread is very cheap there. Bull and cow hides serve them for making bags of all kinds and for covering part of their houses. These hides are so common that one finds scattered scraps of them here and there along the less-frequented streets, in the squares, and on the garden walls.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;One finds few of these gardens cultivated, although every house has its own. I saw only one that was fairly well maintained, no doubt because the gardener was English. Consequently, vegetables are rare there. The one most cultivated is <term>saffron</term> or <term>safflower</term> <note>Original: "Séfran ou Carthame"; the author likely refers to the Safflower plant (Carthamus tinctorius), used as a cheaper alternative to saffron for coloring food</note>, for soup and sauces.

<column-break/>

It is common for the Spaniards to have a mistress. Those who have children by them grant them a kind of legitimacy by publicly acknowledging that they are the fathers. Then these children inherit from them, much like legitimate children. There is no shame attached to <term>illegitimacy</term> <note>Original: "bâtardise"</note>; because the laws authorize such births to the point of giving even illegitimate children the title of <term>Gentlemen</term> <note>Original: "Gentilshommes"; referring to the status of a hidalgo, or a person of noble birth and social standing</note>: and such laws seem more consistent with humanity, in that they do not punish an innocent child for the crime of its father.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The ceremonies of religion are much the same as in Madrid. Throughout the entire <term>Mass</term> <note>the central ritual of Catholic worship</note>, an inhabitant plays the <term>harp</term> in a gallery, no doubt to take the place of an organ. I saw no particular demonstrations of devotion there, other than that of striking one’s breast five or six times, from the beginning of the <term>Canon</term> <note>the most solemn part of the Mass liturgy</note> until after the <term>Communion</term>. The <term>Rosary</term> <note>a traditional Catholic prayer involving meditation on the life of Christ while counting beads</note> is still very much in use there; and it is almost the only prayer made in Montevideo. They also have great devotion to the **Scapular of Mount Carmel**; both men and women wear them. By means of the scapular and the *Avillas*, they believe themselves protected from all perils and secure in their eternal salvation. These *Avillas* that one sees hanging from their necks are a kind of <term>sea chestnut</term> <note>Original: "chataigne de mer"; likely a "sea bean" or drift seed, often used as amulets in folk medicine</note>, resembling a flat, round bean about the width of a small crown and <term>two and a half lines</term> <note>A "line" (ligne) is an old French measurement equal to approximately 2.25 millimeters or 1/12th of an inch</note> thick. The skin is grainy and very finely textured, like shagreen, with a light chestnut color; a black band runs almost all the way around the circumference. I gathered many of these on the seashore at **St. Catherine’s Island** without knowing what they were, and I have seen several mounted in silver at a goldsmith's shop in **Montevideo**. He told me that, worn around the neck, they protect against "bad air" and sorcerers.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There is only one clergyman in the city who gave us a great welcome; he was knowledgeable not only of what the King of Portugal had done against the **Jesuits** in his states, but also of what the Parliaments of France and the Government had decreed against this Society. He even begged me to give him a written summary of what is depicted in the famous painting found at the Jesuits of **Billom in Auvergne** <note>The author refers to a controversial painting found in 1762 which was used as propaganda against the Jesuits, depicting them in a way that suggested they sought world domination</note>, during the inventory made of the furniture and property of those Fathers after the condemnation and suppression of their Order in 1762 and 1763, and the <term>secularization</term> <note>The legal process of releasing members of a religious order from their monastic vows to live as secular priests or laymen</note> of its members. I satisfied his curiosity regarding this authentic monument of "Jesuit folly." This priest is a man of good sense, generally beloved. He has about thirty slaves whom he loves like his own children. He raises them well and then gives them their freedom, along with forty or fifty bulls, to enable them to live independently. The parish of this good priest, along with his private income, may be worth nearly four thousand <term>piasters</term> <note>The piastre, or Spanish dollar, was a silver coin of high value in colonial trade</note> to him.

### 288 HISTORY OF A VOYAGE

Finding myself one day at the Governor's house, I expressed my astonishment that the inhabitants of **Montevideo** did not even think to provide themselves with shade in their gardens and public squares by planting trees that would serve both utility and pleasure. He told us that this decoration was not entirely lacking in the country, and that he himself had planted a lovely grove at a country house he owned about two <term>leagues</term> <note>A league (lieue) was a common unit of distance; the French land league was roughly 4 kilometers or 2.5 miles</note> from the city. He proposed at the same time an excursion to go there on horseback the following afternoon. We accepted the ride with the intention of seeing the country and verifying what he and so many others had told us of the astonishing and marvelous qualities of the horses of **Paraguay**.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the day of the excursion, **Mr. de Bougainville**, the principal officers, and I went to the Government House, where we found horses ready. The Governor's wife, dressed in a <term>riding habit</term> <note>Original: "en Amazone." In this period, a woman's equestrian outfit was referred to as an "Amazon" dress</note> and wearing a gold-trimmed hat turned up in the military style, took the lead of the party on a superb horse whose excellence matched its appearance. We

### TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS 289

arrived after a full hour at the Governor’s grove. This delightful enclosure consists of apple, pear, peach, and fig trees, planted in paths, though somewhat irregularly, with the exception of the central one, which is nearly half a league in length. A fairly substantial stream winds through the orchard; the paths are very rustic, owing to the high and low plants that grow there without cultivation. **Lemon balm** <note>Original: "méliſſe" (*Melissa officinalis*), a fragrant herb in the mint family</note> especially grows there in abundance. The trees were so laden with fruit that most of the branches, unable to bear the weight, were already broken; and all this fruit, it is said, is excellent. We could not judge for ourselves, as they were not due to reach maturity until the end of February; they had, nonetheless, a very fine appearance.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;One could make a charming promenade of this orchard, but the Governor does not have work done there because he intends to return to Europe, where he plans to settle permanently.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It was in this grove that I became acquainted with a **Franciscan** named **Father Roch**, who was the tutor to the son

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We dined several times at the Governor’s house, where he always gave us meals as splendid as the country could permit; however, the dishes were prepared according to local custom; that is to say, most were cooked with refined beef fat, which serves them in place of butter and oil; and seasoned with so much <term>pimento</term> <note>Likely chili peppers, common in the region</note> and <term>safflower</term> <note>A plant used to produce a yellow-orange dye and flavoring, often used as a cheaper substitute for saffron</note> that the meats were entirely covered in them. Care was taken, however, not to put these spices on every dish. Spanish and Chilean wines served as our drink; the plates and dishes were of silver; there were also some of porcelain. A very short cloth covered the table, and the napkins were a little smaller than average handkerchiefs, naturally fringed; or to speak more accurately, frayed at the two

<column-break/>

### TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS 291

ends. The Spaniards ordinarily drink only water during the meal, and at the end, a large glass of wine is brought to everyone, even without it being requested. When we asked for water and wine, they were brought one after the other, and it was necessary to drink them separately. The wine of **Chile** has the color of a potion of <term>rhubarb and senna</term> <note>Both were common medicinal plants used as laxatives; the author is comparing the wine's brownish-red color and harsh flavor to medicine</note>; its taste is quite similar. It takes this flavor perhaps from the soil, or perhaps also from the tarred goatskins in which it is transported. Hardly any other wine is drunk in all of **Paraguay**. One soon becomes accustomed to that taste; and a few days after making it one's regular drink, one finds it good. It is very warming to the stomach. But whether by taste or whim, the Spaniards preferred the French wine that we brought there.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the 1st of January, we went to **Montevideo** to pay our New Year's compliments to the Governor; we did not know that this ceremony is deferred in that country to the sixth of the month, the day of the <term>Epiphany</term> <note>The Christian feast day celebrating the visit of the Magi; also known as Three Kings' Day</note>. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;...that after this nomination, he was to go with his entire entourage to the Parish Church, which they call the Cathedral; we arrived there at half-past twelve. He appeared in the midst of the new Officers of Justice, all holding large white rods in their hands, which they used like staves to support themselves as they walked.
