The ceremony ended, as in Europe, with a Mass and a large dinner.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As Montevideo is not at all populated, desertion is encouraged among foreign troops; during our stay, we lost six sailors and one colonist destined for the Malouines Islands <note>The Falkland Islands</note>; the Governor, at the request of M. de Bougainville, who promised ten <term>piastres</term> <note>A Spanish silver coin, also known as a "piece of eight"</note> for every deserter brought to him, sent Dragoons <note>Cavalry soldiers</note> in pursuit of them; but they brought back no news of them. I even think that had they been promised a hundred, they would not have

arrested a single one; it is in the interest of Spain that many men remain in the country to populate it.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;No foreigner is permitted to sell merchandise in Montevideo; however, despite the difficulties in landing them and the dangers faced in selling them, several of our officers and crew members, who had prepared <term>venture cargo</term> <note>Original: "pacotilles" — small quantities of goods that sailors were historically allowed to carry for private trade</note> in the hope of selling them at the Isle of France <note>Modern-day Mauritius</note> and the East Indies where they thought we were going, disposed of them here. As our ship was the first to arrive in the country since the peace <note>Refers to the end of the Seven Years' War in 1763</note>, everything sold very well. The guards confiscated only a few packages carried imprudently, and M. de Bougainville appeared to loudly approve of this strictness; this persuaded the Spaniards that he did not authorize any smuggling.

## CHAPTER XI.

### *Regarding some details about the Indians of Paraguay.*

One day while we were at the Government house, four Indians came to present themselves; as soon as the Governor saw them entering his courtyard, he had the doors to his apartments closed. We asked him the reason: "If they entered this hall," he told us, "it would be tainted for eight days. They exhale an odor that clings even to the walls." This smell comes from the fact that they rub their bodies with a foul-smelling oil <note>This likely refers to animal fats or fish oils mixed with botanical extracts, used both for ritual purposes and as a practical defense against tropical insects</note> to protect themselves from insects.

These Indians, finding the doors closed, approached the window where we were, and one of them drew from a small bag of tiger-skin <note>original: "peau de Tigre" — in South America, the "tiger" refers to the jaguar (*Panthera onca*), the continent's largest feline</note> a written and folded paper, which he presented. The Governor took it and read it. It was written in the Spanish language. It was a certificate from several Spanish governors who declared

...that one of these Indians was of the race of Caciques <note>A term for indigenous leaders or chieftains in Latin America</note>, and was himself currently a Village Chief. The Governor returned it [the certificate] to him, and the Indian asked by signs for a sheet of paper to substitute for the one that previously wrapped the Certificate, because it was torn in the folds from old age: they gave it to him. Likely these Indians were ignorant of the Spanish language; for they did not utter a single word of it. An Officer told us that they had spoken the language of Pará <note>Likely referring to the region of Pará in Brazil; the author suggests they spoke a Tupi-Guarani dialect common to that area</note>, mixed with that of the Indians from the surrounding lands. For clothing, they had nothing but a kind of cloak composed of several deerskins with their hair, sewn together to form a long rectangle, such as a table napkin might be. It was fastened near the shoulders with two straps; and produced the effect seen in *fig.* 4. of *Pl.* VI. The side of the skin that touched the flesh was white, and painted in red and blue-grey, with squares, lozenges, and triangles. These Indians come into the City quite often, in groups, and also bring their wives. Their dwellings

<meta>catchword: are</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>297</page-num>
# TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.

are not distant from Montevideo by more than six or seven leagues <note>A league at this time was roughly 2.5 miles or 4 kilometers</note>. They come there to drink wine and brandy. Having no use for coined money among themselves, they give small bags of tiger-skin <note>original: "peaux de Tigre" — in South America, the "tiger" refers to the jaguar (*Panthera onca*)</note>, their cloaks, and sometimes the skins of the fierce animals they have killed; but more commonly those they have sewn together to cover themselves. They give them away for almost nothing; for they deliver one of these kinds of cloaks composed of eight deerskins for one *real* <term>A Spanish silver coin; the "réau" in the French text</term>, which is worth twelve and a half *sols* <note>The sol or sou was a French coin of the Old Regime</note>, French money. A bag of tiger-skin, fourteen or fifteen inches long and a foot wide, costs only half a *real*. When one wants to have these cloaks from the Indians, it is enough to take it with one hand and present a *real*, or a half-*real*, with the other. The Indian immediately unties the strap, takes the piece of silver, gives you the cloak or the small bag, and goes completely naked to the nearest merchant to drink wine or brandy.

The women are occupied with the cultivation of <term>manioc</term> <note>A starchy root vegetable, also known as cassava or yuca</note>, and its preparation to make <term>cassava</term> <note>A flatbread or flour made from the manioc root</note>. Their household duties consist only of sewing together the skins of deer or other beasts, with which the men and women cover themselves, and preparing their meals for themselves; as for the men, they spend their lives hunting, fishing, or riding horses; thus they make excellent horsemen. The elders preside over each hamlet, and remain in their dwellings with the young boys and girls who do not yet have the strength to perform arduous labor. Their entire form of government consists of respecting their Elders.

They are extremely skillful in the handling of <term>snares</term> <note>original: "lacs" — here referring to "bolas," a traditional South American throwing weapon made of weights on the ends of interconnected cords</note>, spears, and the bow: they rarely miss their mark with the snares, even on horseback and while running at full gallop. hardly escape them. In their private quarrels, they make use of these <term>snares</term>, and a half-spear. The only way to render their skill useless is to lie down on the ground, or to press oneself against a tree or a wall.

These <term>snares</term> are made of bull's leather cut from around the hide. They twist this strap; they make it supple by dint of greasing it, and lengthen it by pulling it, until it is only half a finger in width. It remains so strong that a Bull cannot break it, and it resists more than a hemp rope, which would be even less supple and could not be used for the same purpose.

One can hardly obtain the skins of Tigers and other fierce beasts except through the Indians. They are, however, not expensive, although quite rare in Monte-video. One can get one of the most beautiful for two or three piastres. They carry in these pouches the <term>cassava</term> <note>A bread or flour made from the manioc root, a staple of the region</note>, which serves as their food, and the heads of their arrows, which they do not fit onto the end of the reed until they are ready to shoot. This head has the shape and width of a Laurel leaf, with two very elongated ends. They insert it into the reed by one end or the other indifferently, because this head is pointed and sharp on both sides. These arrows are all the more deadly because, as the head is not firmly attached to the reed, it remains in the wound when one tries to withdraw the arrow.

When they wish to strike an animal, they pursue it, holding the bridle of their horse with one hand and the <term>snare</term> <note>original: "lac" — referring here to "bolas," a traditional South American hunting weapon consisting of weights attached to interconnected cords</note> with the other; they throw it at the animal's neck, legs, or horns. If the animal is particularly furious or ferocious, they attack it in a company of three or four; each person snares a limb, and then they separate, one going to the right and another to the left. This tightens the snares, and gives

<column-break/>

<page-num>303</page-num>

# TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS. 303

the ease to a third person to approach the animal without danger, and to kill it with his half-spear.

### CHAPTER XII.
### Natural History of Montevideo.

[ The horses of Paraguay are famous in the New World; they also form the main wealth of the inhabitants of Montevideo; they serve the Whites, the Mulattoes, and the Blacks, and their number equals at least that of the people. ] <note>This bracketed section highlights the social structure of the Spanish colony, where horses were essential to all classes, including enslaved Africans and people of mixed heritage.</note>

Despite the value placed on these quadrupeds, one could nonetheless call Montevideo the "hell of horses"; they are often made to work for three days in a row without drinking or eating; they are treated like the camels of Arabia.

These horses are nevertheless excellent; they have preserved the vivacity of the Spanish horses from which they descended; they are extremely sure-footed and are of a surprising agility. Their gait is so brisk and so extended that it equals the greatest trot and the short gallop of our own horses. Their step consists of lifting at the same time the foot from

<meta>catchword: front</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>305</page-num>

# TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.

the front and that of the back; and instead of placing the back foot in the spot where they had placed the front one, they carry it much further, parallel to and even beyond the front foot of the opposite side; this makes their movement nearly twice as fast as that of ordinary horses, and much smoother for the rider. They are not distinguished by their beauty; but one can boast of their lightness, their gentleness, their courage, and their endurance. The inhabitants make no provision of hay or straw to feed these animals. Their only resource is to have them graze in the fields all year round. It is true that the weather there is never cold enough to freeze either the rivers or the plants.

The horses are not shod. The harnesses are also very different from those used in Europe. They first place upon the bare horse a thick, soft cloth of a loose weave, which they call <term>schuaderos</term> <note>original: "schuaderos" — likely a corruption of the Spanish "sudadero," a sweat-cloth or saddle blanket</note>; over this a girth, then a fairly strong leather, the width of the saddle, which extends over the rump and serves as a housing. They call it <term>carné</term> <note>original: "carné" — likely referring to the "carona," a large piece of raw hide used in South American saddlery to protect the horse and the gear</note>.

<sig>V</sig>
<meta>footer: Volume I.</meta>

<summary>This page begins a chapter on the natural history of Montevideo, focusing on the remarkable endurance, unique gait, and hard treatment of the local horses. It describes the traditional South American equestrian equipment, noting how the climate allows for year-round grazing without the need for stored fodder.</summary>

<keywords>Montevideo, horses, Paraguay, Spanish horses, horse gait, sudadero, carona, colonial South America, animal husbandry, horse tack</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the detailed description of equestrian life in Montevideo, transitioning from the physical traits of the horses to their specialized tack and the clothing worn by riders, specifically the poncho. It then shifts focus to the local wildlife, beginning with the region's large felines.</meta>

<page-num>306</page-num>
<header>306 HISTORY OF A VOYAGE</header>

Upon this leather is placed the saddle, made like the pack-saddles of our baggage horses, and over it one or more sheepskins with the wool still attached, sewn together and painted in one or several colors. This is the *peilhon* <note>original: "peilhon"; a regional term for the saddle-cloth or decorative sheepskin cover.</note>. Finally, a second girth or belly-band is used to secure the entire assembly onto the horse. The stirrups are small and narrow, because the riders only place the tip of their shoe in them; and those who go barefoot only place their big toe in them.

The bit of the bridle is made of iron, all in one piece, and without bosses <note>Decorative studs or plates on the side of a bit.</note>. The reins are composed of several small straps, interlaced in the form of cords like those on a clock or a bell-pull, and are at least six and a half or seven feet in length, because they serve at the same time as a whip. A semi-circle of iron, forged from the same piece of the bar, through which the horse's lower jaw is passed, produces the same effect as a curb chain. The part of the *carneros* <note>Spanish for "rams" or "sheep," referring here to the heavy sheepskin leather assembly.</note> that covers the saddle and rests on the croup is usually embossed with floral patterns.

When a Spaniard is on horseback, he wears the *ponchos*, which is more convenient

<column-break/>

<page-num>307</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALVINAS ISLANDS. 307</header>

than a cloak, both for the rider and for his mount.

The *ponchos* is, as I have said, a piece of fabric in the shape of a bed blanket, and is two or three ells <note>The French "aulne" was a unit of measure approximately 1.2 meters long.</note> long by two ells wide. It is worn both on horseback and on foot. The less wealthy people and the Black inhabitants do not take it off except when going to bed.

On horseback, this garment is fashionable even for both sexes, without distinction of rank. One distinguishes a Governor from a slave only by the fineness, the lightness, and the richness of the *ponchos*. Horsemanship is so common in Montevideo that one sees in the women as much skill and agility as in the men; which makes the history of the Amazons <note>The mythical tribe of female warriors; the author suggests the sight of skilled South American horsewomen makes these legends seem plausible.</note> believable to a certain extent.

[ There are many ferocious animals in Montevideo; the Tigers <note>original: "Tigres"; the author refers to the Jaguar (*Panthera onca*), which early European explorers often called tigers.</note> above all are very common there, and in general they are larger and more ferocious than those of the deserts of the Sahara and Biledulgerid <note>Biledulgerid (Bilad al-Jarid) is a historical name for the region of North Africa bordering the Sahara, known as the "Land of Dates."</note>; this observation gives rise to some doubts regarding the singular idea of *Mr. de Buffon* and of the author of the *Philosophical Researches on the* ] <note>The author is challenging the theory of "American degeneracy" proposed by the naturalist Buffon and Cornelius de Pauw, who argued that animals in the New World were smaller and weaker than their Old World counterparts.</note>

<sig>V ij</sig>

Americans <note>The author refers here to the inhabitants or the nature of the Americas</note>, that the animals of the New World are all degenerate, as if nature in these climates were exhausted, and its generative power altered; a single fact well-established by a Naturalist is enough to bring down all the systems of a Philosopher <note>This is a likely critique of the "Degeneracy Theory" proposed by the famous French naturalist Buffon, who argued that American species were smaller and weaker than those in Europe.</note>.

Despite the ferocity of the Tigers of Montevideo <note>original: "Tigres de Monte-video"; likely referring to jaguars or pumas, which were often called tigers by early explorers</note>, people sometimes succeed in taming them at a young age. The Governor had one raised in the courtyard of his Palace; it was tied near the entrance gate with a simple strap. The Dragoons <note>term: Dragoons (cavalry soldiers)</note> and the Servants joked with it, without it giving any sign of its natural ferocity. They turned it, pulled it, and tumbled it about, just as one would do with a domestic cat. Seeing that it might please Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville, the famous French explorer leading the expedition</note>, the Governor had it carried on board and gave it to him. A cage was built there of planks six inches thick, and it was kept for about eight days. At the end of this time, it began to roar from time to time, and especially at night. It was then feared that it might become furious. Furthermore, fresh meat was required to

<column-break/>

# TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS <note>The Falkland Islands</note>
<page-num>309</page-num>

feed it; and we did not have enough to spare. These considerations determined Mr. de Bougainville to have it strangled. It was then only four months old: and its height, on its legs, was two feet three inches. One can judge the size it would have acquired in its full natural growth.

One day while Mr. Frontgouffe and I were busy botanizing <note>original: "herboriser"; the act of collecting plants for study</note>, we heard a plaintive cry coming from a heap of stones and rocks surrounding one of the fountains of Montevideo; we were no more than 7 to 8 fathoms <note>term: toises (a French unit of length roughly equal to 1.9 meters or 6.4 feet)</note> away from it. We first took this cry for that of a cat trapped in these stones, having escaped from a dwelling that was half a quarter of a league <note>term: lieue (league); a league was roughly 4 kilometers</note> distant. As we approached the fountain, this cry seemed to us to be that of a child. We were getting closer when Mr. Frontgouffe said to us: "Let us not advance; it is not the cry of a child, it is that of a Caiman <note>A relative of the alligator found in Central and South America</note>. I remember hearing similar ones more than once in our Islands. We are not strong enough against such a ferocious animal." There are indeed some in

—to the ship. Along the way, we encountered many Curlews <note>long-billed shorebirds</note> flying in groups. They allowed themselves to be approached within pistol range; but we were armed only with sticks.

Being one day in a canoe with the Governor, we smelled a foul odor, similar to that which a corpse would exhale. I first imagined that it came from the decay of some Bull killed and abandoned on the shore; but the Governor disabused me and assured me that it was an exhalation from the urine of an animal named *Zorillos* <note>the skunk; from the Spanish 'zorrillo'</note>, when angry or pursued by some hunter.

The *Zorillos* is the size of a Weasel, a little less long, with tawny fur, lighter under the belly, which is almost gray; two white lines extend along the back, and form from the neck to the tail an almost oval figure. This tail is well-furnished with hair, and the animal almost always holds it erect, as the Squirrel does. When it feels itself pursued, or when it becomes irritated, it releases its urine which infects the air for more than half a league. We smelled this odor two—

The *Zorillos* <note>Spanish for "little fox," referring here to a skunk.</note> is perhaps the same as the "Stinking Beast" or "Child of the Devil" of Canada, whose urine produces nearly the same effects. The *Chinche* <note>Another regional term for a skunk.</note> of the southern parts of America also has much in common with the *Zorillos*.

Another animal very common in the surroundings and toward Buenos Aires is the *Tatu-apara*, which we call the Armadillo; the Spaniards call it *Armadillo*, and the Portuguese *Encubertado* <note>Literally "the covered one," referring to its shell.</note>. As it is very well known, I will not provide a description of it. Ximenez <note>Likely Francisco Ximénez, a 17th-century naturalist and monk.</note> says that the scales of the Armadillo, reduced to powder and swallowed at the weight of one drachm <note>A unit of apothecary weight, about 3.9 grams.</note> in a decoction of sage, provoke a sweat so beneficial that it cures venereal diseases; that it causes thorns to exit from all parts of the body; and, according to Monardes <note>original: "Monades"; referring to Nicolás Monardes, a Spanish physician and botanist.</note>, book 15, page 552, the small bones of the tail of this animal cure deafness.

Several of our sailors bought parakeets at Montevideo. They cost up to two piastres <note>A Spanish silver coin, also known as a piece of eight.</note>. Their plum-

<column-break/>

# TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS

-age is entirely green, except for that of the neck, the stomach, and a little of the belly, which is a beautiful silvery gray. The beak is short, very curved, and flesh-colored; their size is that of a thrush, but they have a very long tail. Gentle, affectionate, but very lively, they easily learn to speak, pronounce well, and enjoy company. The more noise one makes, the more they raise their voices.

They are raised and kept in Montevideo in cages made of bull's hide; these are a type of box, flat on the bottom, oblong, convex on top, pierced with three holes on each side, and with a hole at one end open enough to allow the bird the freedom to pass only its head through. At the other end is an opening that closes with two flaps, having the shape of a carriage gate. It is through there that the parakeet is introduced.

We were assured that the lifespan of this bird is only one year when it is kept in a cage. Is this the truth, or a simple opinion based on expe-

Of the eight that we had on board, six perished from disease or by falling into the sea; a seventh escaped, they say, upon our arrival at Saint-Malo, and the eighth died in my room three days after my return to Paris. <note>The author is likely referring to exotic birds or small monkeys collected during the expedition.</note>

There are few singular shells in Brazil; however, the Governor presented Mr. de Bougainville with a superb paper nautilus <note>original: "nautile papyracée"; a thin-shelled octopus species</note> which had been sent to him from Rio de Janeiro: he also claimed to have found a similar one on the coast of Maldonado Island; it is probable that many others would be found if there were a trade for them at Montevideo.

The vegetable kingdom deserves a little more attention in this country. I found at the home of a Spanish Officer, who was raised in France, a small garden of curious or medicinal plants which the country produces, and of which I learned the names and properties.

The plant named *Méona* <note>likely a species of Euphorbia</note> greatly resembles wild thyme; but the leaf is round and of a less brownish green; the stem is red and creeping: it takes root at every node, and gives a milk

<page-num>315</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header>

white like spurge <note>original: "tithymale"</note>. The seed comes in a spiral, bristly pod; this pod contains only one yellowish seed, which has almost the shape of a kidney. It throws out many branched stems from its root, which spread in a circle on the ground, like those of knotgrass <note>original: "renouée"</note>. Taken as an infusion like tea, it cures, they say, the retention of urine, as if by a miracle.

*Ebreno*, or *Mio-mio* <note>Baccharis coridifolia, known for being toxic to livestock</note>, is a plant whose stem is almost creeping, and hardly rises more than half a foot. The leaf is smaller than that of fennel; its flower is herbaceous and comes in clusters, roughly in an umbel; the root is reddish on the outside, having, like the plant, the taste of aromatic parsnip. It is taken as an infusion against inflammations <note>original: "fluxions"</note> and colds. I would believe it to be a species of *Meum* <note>Spignel or Baldmoney</note>.

Moté has a stem about a foot and a half high, straight, round, branched, and hairy, of a slightly reddish gray. The leaves are an inch to an inch and a quarter long, only three to four lines <note>A "line" is an old unit of measurement, roughly 2.25mm</note> wide, of a whitish green, hairy like the stem. The flowers are born one by one along the branches,

Mr. Simoneti told me that after six months of treatment by the physicians and surgeons of the army for a wound he had received in his side, near the kidneys, which had turned into an ulcer, he was cured in a short time solely by the application of the leaves of this plant.

The *Cachen-laguen* or *Canchalagua*, which is called *Cachinlagua* in Chile <note>original: "Chily"</note>, resembles in every way the small Centaury of Europe <note>Centaurium erythraea, a common medicinal herb</note>. It is slightly shorter than ours. Six or seven whole, dried plants are infused cold in a glass of water throughout the night, or from morning until evening. One then gargles the throat with this infusion and swallows it, and is soon cured of a sore throat. Fresh cold water is poured again over the dregs <note>original: "marc"</note> and left to infuse for the same amount of time;

<page-num>317</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header>

the gargling and swallowing are repeated: this is started a third time. Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville, the expedition leader</note> and Mr. Duclos, our Captain, have experienced this with success more than once. When the infusion is made hot, like tea, it is very heating; but it purifies the blood well. This plant is very renowned in Chile, from where it is obtained. I believe it to be a better febrifuge <note>a medicine used to reduce fever</note> than the European variety. Might the latter not have the same properties for sore throats?

*Mechoacan* <note>likely a variety of Bindweed or Ipomoea</note> is the name that the Spaniards of Montevideo give to a plant which does not resemble at all the one sold in our shops under the same name. That of Montevideo, which is very common there as well as in the surroundings of Buenos Aires, is a small creeping plant whose root runs underground like liquorice. It is whitish and as slender as the quill of a writing pen. From this root emerge rather short branches, lying on the ground, sparsely furnished with leaves, and only at the tips, with small leaves almost sim-

Another plant that they esteem infinitely is the *Guaycuru* <note>Likely *Limonium brasiliense*, known for its potent tannins.</note>. It bears a leaf of a beautiful green, somewhat thick, emerging in great numbers from the root, which is of a brownish-red, shiny on the outside, and reddish within, like that of a strawberry plant. From the middle rises a stem to the height of half a foot, as thick as the quill of a hen's feather, solid, without leaves, of a grayish-green, dividing at the top into a dozen small branches which bear at their summit very small grassy flowers, without scent, and forming together a kind of parasol.

This plant, its root especially, is

<page-num>319</page-num>
->TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS<-

one of the most powerful astringents in Botany; and experience has proven that it is perfect for drying and promptly healing ulcers, scrofula <note>A form of tuberculosis affecting the lymph nodes of the neck, historically called the "King's Evil."</note>, and for stopping dysentery.

The *Payco* <note>Also known as *Epazote* or *Dysphania ambrosioides*, widely used in South American folk medicine.</note> is a plant which, from its root, throws out many creeping branches, which then divide into several others. The leaves are about three lines <note>A "line" is an archaic measurement equal to 1/12th of an inch.</note> long by two wide, notched in the shape of a saw, thick and attached without a stalk to the branches. The flower is so small that it is confused with the seed that succeeds it; the branches are almost entirely covered by them. At first glance, one would take it for rupturewort <note>original: "turquette" or "herniole" (Herniaria glabra).</note>, if the branches were shorter. The whole plant is of a tender green, sometimes reddish, as is the stem; when it approaches maturity, it exhales a scent of lemon that is beginning to rot. It is excellent for stomach ailments and indigestion. Its decoction is sudorific <note>Induces sweating to break a fever.</note>, and it is highly praised against pleurisy. One chews a length of one of the green stems as long as the little finger, and swallows the saliva

The anti-venereal <note>treatment for sexually transmitted infections</note> properties of the *Colaguala*—which others call *Calaguela*—are highly praised. It grows in sterile and sandy soils. Its height is seven to eight inches. Its stem is a collection of various small branches that push their way through the sand or gravel. They are only two or three lines <note>an old unit of measurement, approximately 1/12th of an inch</note> thick, and are full of nodes spaced a short distance from one another, covered with a film that detaches on its own when dry. The leaves are very small, few in number, and grow directly from the stem.

The *Colaguala* is regarded as an admirable specific <note>a medicine intended for a particular disease</note> for dissipating abscesses in a short amount of time. Three or four doses—that is to say, three or four pieces in a simple decoction <note>a concentrated liquor resulting from heating or boiling</note> or infused in wine—are sufficient within the interval of a day. Being "hot in the first degree" <note>referring to the Galenic system of humorism where medicines were classified by qualities of heat, cold, moisture, or dryness</note>, it would become harmful if taken in excess. This root,

A Franciscan named Father Roch, who was said to be very well-versed in medicine, told me that he used *Colaguala* against epilepsy as well as against venereal diseases; when its use did not perfectly cure the former of these two maladies, he had recourse to the following remedies, and always with happy success. He made the patient drink, during the course of the day—and especially two glasses on an empty stomach, a good half-hour apart—a pint or two pounds of water with which a girl in puberty or a woman had thoroughly washed her private parts upon rising from bed (*a*). The same Franciscan, being with us at the Governor's country house, showed me another plant which he calls *Carqueja* <note>original: "Carqueja"; a medicinal herb common in South America, often used for digestion</note>, and which he says is admirable, taken as an infusion like tea, to dissolve curdled blood in the body, to purify it, and to remove all obstructions. But it must be used with great moderation, because it agitates the blood extremely, especially the root.

The *Carqueja* grows like a small shrub, about a foot high, naturally shaped into a rounded head. It has no leaves distinct from its stem, which closely resembles that of the broom <note>original: "genêt"; a shrub of the Genista family</note>, in whose class I think it can be placed. This stem divides into many others to form the head. They are very flexible and very thin.

In Montevideo, as well as in Brazil, *séfran* is cultivated in gardens, and in abundance; because they cover almost all dishes, and even the soup, with its flower. ### CHAPTER XIII.
Particular observations on the herb of Paraguay <note>Known today as Yerba Mate (Ilex paraguariensis)</note>.

THIS famous plant is the principal source of wealth for the Spaniards, the Indians, and above all, the Jesuits. Thus, the idea I am going to give of it interests men other than Naturalists.

Mr. d'Ulloa <note>Antonio de Ulloa (1716–1795), a Spanish scientist and naval officer who wrote extensively on South America</note> has given some details on the herb of Paraguay; but he speaks only according to the Missionaries of the society <note>The Society of Jesus, or Jesuits</note>, for the Jesuits have never allowed anyone but their brothers to penetrate into this rich country.

"It is claimed," says Mr. d'Ulloa, "that the sale of this herb was at first so considerable, that luxury was soon introduced among the conquerors of the country, who had at first found themselves reduced to pure necessity. To sustain an excessive expense, the taste for which is always increasing, they were obliged to have re-

# HISTORY OF A VOYAGE

covered with a cowhide. The *Caacuys* <note>The finest grade of Yerba Mate, made from the youngest buds</note> cannot be preserved as long as the other two species, whose leaves are transported to Tucuman, Peru, and even to Spain. It does not endure transport well. It is even claimed that this herb, when taken on-site, has a certain bitterness that it lacks elsewhere, which increases its medicinal virtue as much as its price.

The manner of taking the *Caacuys* is to fill a vessel with boiling water and to throw in the leaf, which has been powdered and reduced to a paste. As it dissolves, the small amount of earth that may have remained floats enough to be skimmed off. The water is then passed through a cloth; and having let it rest a little, one sips it with a straw <note>original: "chalumeau"; likely referring to a bombilla or reed pipe</note>. Ordinarily, no sugar is added; but one mixes in a little lemon juice, or certain pastilles with a very sweet scent. When it is taken to induce vomiting, one adds a little more water and lets it become lukewarm.

The great manufacture of this herb is at the *Villa*, or the new *Villarica*, which is near the mountains of *Maracayu*,

<column-break/>

# TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS <note>The Falkland Islands</note>

situated to the East of Paraguay, toward twenty-five degrees twenty-five minutes of Southern latitude. This district is praised for the cultivation of the tree; but it is not on the mountains that one must look for it, but in the marshy lowlands that separate them.

From there, for Peru alone, they extract up to one hundred thousand *arrobes* <note>A Spanish unit of weight</note>, each weighing twenty-five pounds of sixteen ounces (standard weight), and the price of an *arrobe* is seven *écus*, or twenty-eight French *livres*, which totals two million eight hundred thousand *livres*.

However, the *Caacuys* has no fixed price, and the *Caamini* <note>The second-highest grade of mate</note> sells for double the price of the *Caaguazu* <note>The most common, coarsest grade of mate</note>. At Montevideo, while we were there at anchor, the latter was sold for twenty-five *livres* or five *piastres* per *arrobe*. The Governor procured one for us at this price.

The Indians established in the provinces of Uruguay and Parana, under the government of the Jesuits, have sown the seeds of the tree, which they transported there from Maracayu, and which have hardly degenerated at all. They resemble those of ivy.

<header>HISTORY OF A VOYAGE</header> <page-num>330</page-num>

But these Indians do not produce the herb of the first kind; they keep the *Caa-mini* <note>The finest grade of Yerba Mate, made from leaf tissue without veins or stems.</note> for their own use, and sell the *Gaaguazu* or *Palos* <note>Lower grade mate containing stems (palos).</note> to pay the tribute they owe to Spain.

The Spaniards believe they find in this herb a remedy, or a preservative against all evils. Everyone is in agreement regarding its aperient <note>Laxative or opening</note> and diuretic qualities; but I would not wish to be a guarantor for the Jesuits for all the properties they attribute to it. I would believe that the most proven quality, and yet the one they praise the least, is that it procures for them, every year, an incredible sum of money.

It is told that, in the early times, some people having used it to excess, it caused them a total alienation of the senses, from which they did not recover until several days later. But it appears certain that it often produces opposite effects, such as procuring sleep for those subject to insomnia, and waking those who fall into lethargy; of being both nourishing and purgative. The habit of using it makes it necessary,

<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header> <page-num>331</page-num>

and often it even makes it difficult to contain oneself to moderate use; since it is asserted that excess intoxicates, and causes most of the discomforts that one attributes to strong liquors.

According to Mr. d'Ulloa <note>Antonio de Ulloa (1716–1795), a Spanish scientist and naval officer who wrote extensively on South America.</note>, the herb of Paraguay is called ***Maté*** in Peru. To prepare it, he says, one puts a certain quantity of it into a gourd mounted in silver, which is also called ***Maté***, or ***Totumo***, or ***Calabacito*** <note>Small gourd</note>.

One throws into this vessel a portion of sugar, and one pours a portion of cold water over the whole, so that the herb in paste form may soak: then one fills the vessel with boiling water; and as the herb is very fine, one drinks through a pipe large enough (this straw is called a ***Bombilla***) to allow passage for the water, but too small to allow it for the herb. As the water diminishes, one renews it, always adding sugar, until the herb ceases to float. Then one puts in a new dose of herb: Often one mixes in lemon juice, or bitter orange, and fragrant flowers. This liquor is ordinarily taken on an empty stomach: however

<page-num>332</page-num>
-># HISTORY OF A VOYAGE<-

many also use it in the afternoon. It is possible that its use is healthful; but the manner of taking it is extremely disgusting. However numerous a company may be, everyone drinks through the same tube which is passed around. The *Chapetons* <note>original: "Chapetons"; a term used for Spaniards born in Europe</note> (European Spaniards) do not think much of this drink; but the Creoles <note>descendants of Europeans born in the Americas</note> are passionately greedy for it. They never travel without a provision of Paraguay herb; and they do not fail to take it every day, preferring it to all kinds of food, and eating only after having taken it.

Some, says Frézier <note>Amédée-François Frézier (1682–1773), a French engineer and explorer</note> (*Account of the Voyage to the South Sea*, page 228) call the Paraguay herb, *Saint Bartholomew's Herb*, because they claim that this Apostle was in these Provinces, where he rendered it healthful and beneficent from the poisonous state it was in before. Instead of drinking the tincture separately, as we drink that of tea, they put the herb into a cup, made of a gourd mounted in silver, which they call *Mate*. They add sugar to it, and pour hot water over it, which they drink immediately,

<column-break/>

-># TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS<- <page-num>333</page-num>
without giving it time to infuse, because it turns black like ink. To avoid drinking the herb which floats on top, they use a silver straw, at the end of which is a bulb pierced with several small holes: thus the liquor that one sucks through the other end is entirely freed from the herb. They drink in a circle with the same straw, adding hot water over the same herb as they drink. Instead of the straw or *Bombilla* <term>Bombilla: a metal straw with a filtered end used for drinking mate</term>, some push the herb aside with a silver separator pierced with small holes. The repugnance that the French have shown toward drinking after all sorts of people, in a country where there are many persons afflicted with venereal diseases, has led to the invention of the use of small glass straws which they are beginning to use in Lima. This liquor, to my taste, is better than tea: it has a rather pleasant herbal smell. The people of the country are so accustomed to it that there are none, even among the poorest, who do not drink it at least once a day.

The trade of the Paraguay herb, adds this Author, is carried out at *Santa-Fe*, where

it arrives via the River Plate <note>original: "la riviere de la Plata"</note> and by carts. There are two types: one called *Yerva de Palos* <note>literally "herb of sticks," a coarser grade containing stems</note>, and the other, finer and of better quality, named *Hierba de Camini* <note>a premium grade of mate made from selected leaves</note>. This latter kind is harvested from the lands of the Jesuits. The greatest consumption of it occurs from La Paz to Cusco, where it is worth half as much again as the other kind, which is sold from Potosí to La Paz. Every year, more than 50,000 *arrobes* <note>an *arrobe* or *arroba* is a Spanish unit of weight, roughly 25 pounds</note> leave Paraguay for Peru; that is to say, 1,250,000 pounds in weight of both herbs, of which at least a third is *Camini*, not counting about 25,000 *arrobes* of the *Palos* variety for Chile. One pays for each package, which contains six or seven *arrobes*, four *reales* in *Alcavala* tax <note>a Spanish sales tax</note>; and the costs of transport over more than six hundred leagues double the price of the first purchase, which is about two *piastres*; so that it ends up costing five *piastres* per *arrobe* in Potosí, or twenty-five French *livres*. This transport is usually done by carts carrying one hundred and fifty *arrobes* from Santa Fe to Jujuy, the last city of Tucumán; and from there to Potosí, which is

<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header> <page-num>335</page-num>

still a hundred leagues away, it is transported on mules. I have noted that the use of this herb is necessary in the mining regions and in the mountains of Peru.

I saw in Montevideo the truth of the accounts of these two authors; and indeed, at whatever hour of the day you present yourself in a house, you find someone taking *maté*, and they never fail to offer you some, even in the greatest heat; for they have been told that this infusion is refreshing, that it aids digestion, etc.

Usually, the vessel in which one takes the *maté* is mounted on a stand and attached to a tray. I have seen similar ones in almost every house. Some inhabitants, however, had only the vessel itself, adorned with silver, held in the hand without the tray. There are also *Bombilles* <note>straws used for drinking mate, typically made of metal with a filtered end</note>, of which the end that soaks in the liquid has the shape of an oyster shell, which would be fitted to the tube by the top of the hinge.

->## CHAPTER XIV.<-

->### Departure for the Falkland Islands.###<-

Upon our arrival at Montevideo <note>The capital of modern-day Uruguay, then a Spanish stronghold.</note>, we had brought to the Governor the compass devised by Captain Mandillo, a Genoese, for finding longitude: we wished to make the observations there that we had been unable to undertake on the ship throughout the entire crossing, even during calms; because the defect of this compass is that the slightest movement prevents the needles from settling: and during a calm, there is always a more or less strong rolling motion. Despite all the care we had taken to preserve this compass, the humidity of the sea air, which penetrates everywhere, had weathered the needles, which had acquired a bit of rust near the center and the sliders. They had lost that equilibrium so necessary, and even their magnetism in part. We worked to remove the rust from them; and we restored their magnetism:

<page-num>337</page-num>
->### TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.###<-

magnetism: but we departed without having made our observations; and even the difficulty of transport, joined with the fear of losing the favorable moment to exit the Rio de la Plata <note>The "Silver River," the wide estuary between Argentina and Uruguay.</note>, prompted Mr. de Bougainville to leave this machine at the Governor’s house; he only requested that he have it sent to him in France when he himself returned to Europe. Thus, this Genoese compass served us for no experiments.

It was on the 16th of January that we set sail toward the Falkland Islands <note>original: "Isles Malouines." The French name "Malouines" is the origin of the Spanish "Malvinas."</note>. We were already in the open sea when we caught three superb butterflies, which, by the varied colors of their wings, closely imitated the plumage of the most beautiful parrot of Brazil. I have drawn them in Plate VII, figure 4.

We also fished in these waters for a singular fish: our sailors give it the name of "Galley" <note>Likely the Portuguese Man o' War (Physalia physalis).</note>. It is a species of bladder that one can place in the genus of those which Naturalists call Holothurians <note>term: Holothurians. Usually refers to sea cucumbers, though the author is using it here to describe gelatinous zooplankton.</note>, which, without having the appearance of a plant or a fish, nonetheless possess a true life, and move themselves in the manner of animals, by a movement which is their own, from one place...

from one to another, independently of the help of the wind and the waves, upon which one sees these bladders carried like little ships. Those who do not observe this bladder-like appearance with curious and enlightened eyes would take it for a piece of slime swollen with air floating on the surface, carried away by the waves and the winds; but the sailor who had fished it out, having brought it to me, I had plenty of time to observe it. I noticed there a <term>peristaltic movement</term><note>A wave-like muscle contraction, typically associated with digestion</note>, such as that which <term>Anatomists</term><note>Scientists who study the bodily structure of living things</note> attribute to the intestines and the stomach. I was on the point of lifting it from the bucket with my hand, when Mr. Duclos, our Captain, stopped my arm, and made me aware of the danger there would be in touching such a fish. I contented myself, therefore, with observing it with my eyes, and painting it.

The Captain’s foreboding was verified that very same day. A cabin boy, having fished out a second <term>galley</term><note>original: "galere"; here referring to the Portuguese Man o' War, named for its resemblance to a galley ship under sail</note>, had the imprudence to take it with his hand; a moment later he cried out that he felt a sharp pain over the entire back of the hand and at the wrist: he shook it very promptly to rid himself of the galley, but it

<page-num>339</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header>

was too late. We ran to his cries; he was weeping, stomping his feet, and saying that it felt to him as if he had his hand in a burning brazier. We soaked it for him in oil; we applied a <term>compress</term><note>A pad of absorbent material used to apply pressure or medication</note> soaked in this liquid, and he still felt the same pain for more than two hours; but it diminished imperceptibly.

The galley is an oblong bladder, flattened underneath, rounded in its contour, but as if blunted at its extremities; it is from there that those threads start, the touch of which becomes so painful. One of these extremities is more rounded than the other; the latter is a little elongated. That which forms the base or point of support for this bladder is ruffled at its edges. The whole is a very delicate membrane, transparent, and approaching the shape of those half-globes that rise on the surface of the waters during a summer rain, especially when it falls in large drops: it is always empty, but swollen like a balloon. This membrane has fibers, some circular, others longitudinal, by means of which the peristaltic contraction movement is formed.

At its most elongated end, it contains a small amount of very clear water, which a small membranous partition <note>original: "cloifon membraneufe"</note> prevents from spilling into the rest of the cavity. The fiber that runs from front to back, passing over the back, is raised and wavy on the edges, pleated like a beautiful crest, of a vivid color of purple-blue-green, and extended in the manner of a sail: it lowers, raises, and turns itself, as if to trim itself according to the wind. From the two ends of the ruff <note>original: "fraize"; likely referring to the fringe or base of the crest</note>, colored like this species of sail, come out filaments of different lengths; two very short ones are as thick as a strong quill, which then divide into several others less thick, but much longer; and these into others still longer and thinner, numbering eight in all: their length is about one foot; but they are not all equally long. These interlaced cords have, near the body, the appearance of a net, of which the meshes are unequal. These legs have a sort of joint <note>original: "articulations"</note> formed by small circular rings, in which one also notices a movement of contraction. All

<page-num>341</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINA ISLANDS.</header>
these filaments are like hanging tassels, composed of cords of a purplish and greenish azure, nearly transparent, and of various lengths, whose edges appear serrated and fire-colored, and flaxen-gray <note>original: "gris de lin"; a pale violet or lavender-grey</note>, intermingled from space to space.

The largest galleys <note>term: "galeres" — a historical name for the Portuguese Man o' War (Physalia physalis), named for its resemblance to a sailing ship</note> that I have seen were about seven inches long at their base, by five inches high. It would be quite difficult to determine exactly the color of this singular animal. The bladder <note>original: "veffie"; the gas-filled float of the creature</note> is clear and transparent like the purest crystal; but its edges, its back, and its legs have, so to speak, the colors of the Rainbow, or of a sulfurous flame. We saw a great quantity of them on our route, and especially in the channel that forms Saint Catherine Island in Brazil; and I believe it is common in these waters. If the simple touch of this animal causes so much pain, what judgment must one make of that which it produces in the body of fish, or other animals that have devoured it? What is surprising, says Father Labat <note>Jean-Baptiste Labat (1663–1738), a French clergyman and travel writer known for his descriptions of the West Indies</note>, is that it corrupts and poisons the flesh of fish—

On the 25th there was a great agitation in the sky and on the sea: the rolling was so constant and so strong that it caused the death of a goat, two sheep, and three cows; other animals were sickened by it, as were the horses we had embarked at Montevideo.

On the 29th, the wind was succeeded by a great storm; the sea was so heavy that the waves fell upon the forecastle <note>The forward part of the upper deck.</note>, and threatened at every moment to swallow the ship.

On the 31st, from six o'clock in the morning, we perceived land to the East, and on approaching we believed we saw Islands: the shape of these Islands, arranged in a triangle, as they say those named the Sebaldes <note>The Sebald Islands, now known as the Jason Islands, part of the northwestern Falklands.</note> are, and the proximity we thought ourselves to be at, made us believe at first that we were going to land there. According to our position taken at noon, we found them placed, in the French Map by Bellin <note>Jacques-Nicolas Bellin (1703–1772), a prolific French hydrographer.</note>, thirty leagues too far to the West; and our observation disillusioned us all the more in this regard, as it agrees with that of Father

<column-break/>

<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header> <page-num>343</page-num>
Feuillée <note>Louis Feuillée (1660–1732), a French Minim friar, explorer, and astronomer.</note>, and with a manuscript Map from the Naval Depository, given by Mr. de Choiseul to Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville, the leader of the expedition to colonize the Falklands.</note>, before our departure from Paris. *See these Islands*, as they appeared to us at two leagues distance, with the Cape at East-South-East, *Plate VII. fig. 1.*

In coasting this land we soon found ourselves half a league from two flat Islands which, at first sight, appeared to us covered with underwood; but it was only a large rush with flat and wide leaves, known by the name of *glayeux* <note>Likely Tussac grass (Poa flabellata), which dominates the coastal landscape of the Falklands.</note>, as we recognized upon landing on other lands, the surface of which had also deceived us. Each plant of *glayeux* forms a mound raised two and a half feet or thereabouts, from which rises a tuft of green leaves to a nearly equal height. We perceived no wood; the ground appeared dry and arid: perhaps the summer heat had dried up the vegetation.

On the first of February we skirted the coast, and soon we were surprised by a calm; however, the tide was carrying us toward the shore over a rocky bottom. In this predicament, from which we could not extract ourselves for lack of wind, we took soundings with the intention of anchoring if the bottom was found to be good. There were eighteen to twenty fathoms, but it was a rocky bottom; anxiety then redoubled aboard our ship. The *Sphinx* <note>The consort ship accompanying Bougainville’s frigate, the *Aigle*</note> found itself in the same perplexity as we did; and we were already thinking of means to save our lives, should we go to wreck upon those rocks which the Sailors call *Carpenters* <note>original: "Charpentiers"; a common nautical term for jagged rocks that can "rebuild" or break a ship apart</note>. Fortunately, around eight o'clock, the wind freshened ever so slightly from the landward side; and our Captains, attentive and skillful in taking advantage of the slightest benefit that presented itself, maneuvered so adroitly that we moved away from the land. The crew felt the danger in which we were engaged so clearly that even during the most stormy times, and during the very tempest that we endured near the Maldonados <note>A reference to the Maldonado islands or coast near present-day Uruguay</note>, they had not

<column-break/>

<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header> <page-num>345</page-num>
maneuvered with as much promptness and precision. It was a curious spectacle to see everyone at his post, holding in his hand the rope he was to work; everyone with a face upon which were painted anxiety and fear, mixed with hope; everyone in the deepest silence, eyes fixed on the Captain, and ears attentive to obey the first command. The two Captains and the Lieutenants, everyone indeed, were busy watching—some toward the open sea, others toward the land—to observe if they could see the slightest *breeze* <note>original: "brise"</note> rise and make the surface of the water shimmer, which was almost as smooth as a mirror. This man held out his cheek, that one his hand, a third exposed his hand wetted toward the side where he imagined the wind was beginning to blow, in order to feel the slightest impression of it. Finally, the much-desired breeze, though very weak, arose; fear gave way to joy and satisfaction; and so as not to find ourselves in the same predicament again, we moved out into the open sea.

sighted the great bay of the Malouines Islands <note>The Falkland Islands; named "Malouines" by French navigators from Saint-Malo.</note>.

<header>CHAPTER XV.</header>

<header>Landing at the Malouines Islands.</header>

THE ENTRANCE to the bay is admirable, and
we entered it with full sails,
as if into the finest port in Eu-
rope.

This bay, of which one can see the plan and the
figure in Plate VIII, can contain at least
a thousand vessels; one sees to the West
Islands and Islets, sheltered from all winds, and
where the vessels are in greater safety than
in the port of Brest itself.

We put the canoe and the longboat to
sea, and we landed to the South of the
bay: Messrs. de Bougainville, de Nerville,
de Belcourt, Lhuillier, Donat, Sirandré,
and myself. During the crossing a
prodigious quantity of a single species of black
and white birds passed in nu-
merous flocks, only five or six feet
above our heads. We killed a few
of them. Those that fell wounded
dived when we tried to seize them.

Upon landing, the expedition leaders, including Bougainville, begin exploring the local wildlife and geography.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the expedition's first landing and exploration of the terrain in the Falkland Islands.</meta>

<page-num>348</page-num>
<header>HISTORY OF A VOYAGE</header>

Before landing, we shot at bustards <note>original: "outardes"; likely the Upland Goose, which early French explorers identified as bustards</note>, geese, and ducks, which did not fly away at our approach. They walked near us as if they had been domesticated.

Deceived by the distance, we had imagined we would find dry and arid ground; but when we had set foot on land, we found everywhere grass, a foot or a foot and a half high, even upon the summits of the heights themselves, which we had great difficulty climbing due to the obstacle this plant opposed to our progress.

We ascended in groups, while some detached themselves to hunt both on the heights and along the coast. We exhausted ourselves greatly scaling these heights: there were no paths through this grass, which has likely vegetated there since this earth first existed. One sank into this hay up to the knee; and the soil, almost black, was nothing but a mold of plants rotted year after year, which sprang back under the feet because of the roots that were interlaced there. Fortunately, we

<page-num>349</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS <note>The French name for the Falkland Islands, derived from the port of Saint-Malo.</note></header>

had provided ourselves with small wicker-covered bottles of brandy and some sea biscuits, which were a great resource to us for strengthening ourselves against the heat and fatigue.

Our hunters returned in the evening, laden with geese, bustards, ducks, and teal <note>original: "farcelles," an archaic spelling of "sarcelles"</note>. I had wandered away from the others and had gone alone a full league along the coast, above the place where the canoe had landed. I shot at two ducks four or five feet from the shore. Not daring to risk getting into the water to retrieve them, I imprudently drew them in with the end of my gun. The quantity of game made me hurry to reload, without paying attention to the fact that there might be some drops of water in the barrel. The powder was dampened enough by it to no longer catch fire; and having no worm <note>term: "tire-bourre" — a corkscrew-like tool used to extract wadding or unspent charges from a muzzle-loading firearm</note>, I decided to go back and rejoin the canoe. I had hardly gone twenty paces when I encountered a very well-beaten path in the grass, eight or nine inches wide, which ran along the coast ten or twelve feet from the water's edge. I then imagined that the Island was inhabited, if not

by men, or at least by four-footed animals that frequented that district. But what animals? Were they ferocious, or were they not? I might encounter some of them on my path. I was alone, with a musket <note>original: "fufil" (fusil) - a flintlock firearm</note> which I could not use. I felt a bit of anxiety.

I fixed the bayonet to the end of the musket, and nevertheless continued my way along the path, curious to know where it led. About two hundred paces from where I had first taken it, the path entered a cluster of about a hundred of those sword-lilies <note>original: "glayeux" - likely referring to the tall, blade-like foliage of the Tussac grass common in the Falklands</note> which I have mentioned. I did not dare to plunge into them; but passing nearby, I stopped for a few minutes, looking attentively and listening to see if I could hear anything moving. No movement, no noise.

I continued my way, picking up the path beyond that point, until I met the canoe. Seeing that night was approaching and that the various groups of observers and hunters had not yet returned, the crew had come to meet us to pick us up. It was almost full of game, and we were forced, because of the night,

On the 4th, starting at six o'clock in the morning, we went to explore the bottom of the bay, which had appeared to us to be a large river when we had observed it the day before from the heights. On the 8th, our Captain's children took it upon themselves to set some hooks from the back of the ship, and caught a great quantity of fish with a delicate taste, but which were only eight to nine inches long; they had red eyes, gills edged with a golden color, and all the fins of the same color; their skin was smooth like that of a tench <note>A freshwater fish of the carp family, known for its small scales and slimy skin</note>. I do not know their name.

We sent some Officers from the *Sphinx* to look for wood: they reported back to us that the trees we thought we had seen in the distance were only a type of grass like flat-leafed rushes, which our Sailors know by the name of "glayeux" <note>likely referring to *Gladiolus* or iris-like sword grass</note>; and that the mounds served as a lair for sea lions <note>original: "loups marins," literally "sea wolves"</note>. They had killed three among others, as thick and large as our canoe, as well as a species of wild dog, which closely resembles a large kind of fox, and which was mistaken for a lynx <note>original: "loup-cervier"</note>.

Mr. de Bougainville, for his part, did not

<column-break/>

# TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS

find but three very dry trees on the shore, one of which was almost as thick as a wine barrel. As he saw none standing in all the terrain he had traversed, it is to be believed that these trees were brought there from Tierra del Fuego <note>original: "Terre de Feu"</note> or the surrounding areas by the waves and currents, which carry toward the East. The Officers in his party were, so to speak, attacked by a sort of wild dog; it is, perhaps, the only ferocious four-footed animal in the Malouine Islands <note>The Falkland Islands</note>: perhaps also this animal is not ferocious, and only came to present itself and approach us because it had never seen men. The birds did not flee from us; they approached us as if they were tame. We saw in this country neither reptiles nor venomous beasts.

We dispatched our canoe at that time to a neighboring Island to look for Penguins, which abound there like ants in an anthill. It returned a few hours later with one hundred and sixty of these wingless birds, from whose stomachs we made a salt-cure.

By dint of visiting this unknown land, we had cause to admire the beauty of nature, even in the places she seems to have most neglected; in the part of the Island that we saw, the terrain everywhere presents a very pleasant aspect. Mountains, or rather heights that we called mountains, surround plains as far as the eye can see, intersected by small elevations and hills that connect by gentle slopes. At the foot of each flows and winds a stream, larger or smaller, which empties into the sea through the many inlets of the bays. The one where we anchored penetrates more than six leagues <note>A league is approximately 3 miles or 4.8 kilometers.</note> into the land, and naturally forms a good harbor capable of anchoring more than two thousand ships. One finds good bottom everywhere, with Islands, Islets, and peninsulas numbering about a dozen, which provide shelter from the most violent winds; to the point, perhaps, of never having a heavy sea there.

The entrance to this bay is at least two leagues wide, and is narrowed by a fairly large Islet, and quite

<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header> <page-num>357</page-num>
distant from the South-East point, as can be seen on the Map.

This great bay, which was discovered about a fortnight before our departure from the Island, was examined and partially followed by Mr. de Belcourt and Mr. Martin, who made a journey <note>original: "caravane" - here meaning an overland expedition or trek.</note> there of two or three days. As we desired a more complete knowledge of its extent, Messrs. de Saint-Simon and Donat set out a few days after the return of the two Officers I have just named. Having first reached the place where it comes closest to the settlement—that is to say, two short leagues away—they traveled along its coast to its far end. They then crossed to the opposite shore and followed it for about ten leagues. Streams and a considerable river having formed an obstacle to the continuation of their march, given the difficulty of crossing it, they decided to climb the mountain that appeared to them the highest, from which they thought they could discover the entrance of this bay and the rest of its course. They then judged that it reached at least fifteen leagues into the land, and that it

The coast of this bay offers, they say, excellent soil and a pleasant appearance. It is watered every quarter-league by streams and small rivers, one of which, coming from the West, appeared to them to be about sixty feet wide. They counted twenty-six fairly large islands in the part of this bay that they saw.

Is there really a strait that divides these islands and communicates from North to South, as some Navigators have imagined? Might this bay not have led them to conjecture it? Perhaps they only perceived the entrance; or not having dared to venture into it, because of its great depth into the land and its great width, they may have judged that it formed a strait <margin>(a)</margin>.

<margin>(a) In the second voyage, it was recognized that this strait does indeed exist; and that its entrance on the North side is at the place we had named *la Conchée* <note>The Shell-bed</note>.</margin>

### CHAPTER XVI.
#### Establishment in this new country.

It was on the 17th of February that we embarked in the large boat <note>original: *grand canot*</note>, with a tent and our beds, to establish ourselves on land and camp on a hill, almost at the bottom of the bay.

Immediately after our landing, we worked to set up our tent in the place that appeared most convenient to us, a good musket-shot <note>A common historical measurement of distance, roughly 200-300 yards</note> from the sea. The place where we established ourselves was exposed to the North, which makes the South of the country, relative to the Equator <note>The author is noting that in the Southern Hemisphere, the sun is in the North at midday</note>. Below, and a short pistol-shot from the tent, flowed a small stream of fresh water, very good for drinking. Facing the tent was a hill similar to the one on whose slope the tent was pitched. A few steps from there, a hole was dug in the ground to serve as a kitchen; and we burned heather there, as there was no other wood. We also tried those large clods...

Those who proposed to remain in these Islands to begin the establishment of the new colony thrilled with joy at this; and with good reason, as this peat <note>The author refers to "tourbe," a combustible organic soil used as fuel in the absence of trees on the islands.</note> is from that moment dry and ready to burn; and being extremely abundant, one can load canoes with it every day and bring it to the settlement.

While we were working in this manner to create our settlement, means were being taken on board to move deeper into the bay, as much to be within closer reach of us as to put our frigates in greater safety.

After these trials, the two Acadian families <note>Refugees from Nova Scotia who had been displaced during the Great Upheaval (Grand Dérangement) and were recruited for this colonization effort.</note> whom we had brought to settle on this Island in order to populate it were embarked in the longboat. They disembarked around nine o'clock in the morning with all their clothes, furniture, and necessary utensils; provisions and gunner-tents <note>Large, sturdy canvas tents typically used by naval artillerymen.</note> to house those of the crew who were to remain on land to work on the settlement. As our tent at that time was not sufficient to house us all, we erected new ones and divided ourselves; I re-

<page-num>363</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header>

-mained alone with Messrs. de Bougainville and de Nerville <note>Bougainville was the expedition leader; de Nerville was his cousin and the appointed governor of the new colony.</note>.

The domestic animals that we offloaded were in a sorry state; they appeared to us either crippled or languishing. They were abandoned to their fate on the shore, and those who could not stand on their feet were dragged to the grass. The next day, having gone to see if they were dead or alive, we were not a little surprised to find neither horses nor sheep, and the cows with their calves dispersed across the countryside. 

One could not have imagined that, having been so sick the day before, they would have gained enough strength in one night to run through the fields; and it was feared that sea lions <note>original: "loups marins" (literally sea wolves), the common term for seals or sea lions at the time.</note> or some fierce beasts unknown to us had devoured them; but the carcasses of the mare and her foal, which were still visible on the shore, dispelled this fear.

I had seen the first plan; and based on my representations, several changes had been made: I therefore believed I could, with the same liberty, give my opinion on the choice of the site. I represented that during heavy rains and the melting of snow, the water descending abundantly from the hillside would flood the lodgings and might perhaps knock them down—if not all at once, then at least over time, after undermining the foundations. The slope is indeed somewhat steep in this spot. Mr. Lhuillier proposed, as a remedy for this inconvenience, to dig a trench above to catch the waters and divert them; but this means did not seem sufficient to me; the trench was not capable of stopping the impetuosity of a torrent: moreover, the water that would have remained there, filtering little by little through the earth, would have

<column-break/>

->TO THE MALOUINES ISLANDS.<- <page-num>365</page-num>
brought into the apartments a dampness very harmful to the health of those who would have inhabited them, as well as to the provisions and furniture. At first, they did not seem to yield to my opinion. Mr. Lhuillier defended his own; and had already had some earth cleared in the spot to which he had given preference. But, after all reflections were made, he decided on another location, a good musket-shot away, situated on the same hillside, but where the slope is very gentle. From that very moment, workers were set to digging the foundations. The sailors from the two frigates were employed for this; and Mr. de Bougainville paid their daily wages for the work, independently of their salaries as sailors.

After having taken our precautions to defend ourselves from the rigors of the seasons, it was necessary to think of guarding ourselves against the unforeseen attack of the enemies of the French name; and it will soon be seen if our measures were well-concerted.

->CHAPTER XVII.<-
*A Fort is constructed, and an*
*Obelisk is erected.*

On the 25th, Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville (1729–1811), the celebrated French explorer who led this expedition to colonize the Falklands.</note> proposed to the Officers, both of the land and sea forces, that they work to erect a Fort upon the height that forms the hill where the housing for the new colonists of the Malouine Islands <note>The French name for the Falkland Islands, derived from the port of Saint-Malo.</note> had been built. All with one unanimous voice, we agreed to raise it with our own hands, and to bring it to its perfection without the help of the rest of the crew; at that instant the site was chosen and the plan of the Fort was traced by the engineer.

During that time, some of us went to choose tools to execute our project; others hunted, to provide food for the workers. Until now, game has been killed in such great abundance that it has more than sufficed for the nourishment of the crews of the two frigates. We have more than once

<column-break/>

->TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.<- <page-num>367</page-num>
reflected on how singular it was that we had come to establish ourselves on land, in a desert and unknown country, without other provisions than bread, wine, and brandy; without anxiety for the morrow, and persuaded that hunting would provide enough for the nourishment of more than one hundred and twenty people disembarked and camped under tents. Furthermore, not only have we not lacked for it, but we have been very abundantly provided with it. We gave, however, to each mess <note>original: "plat" — a naval term for a group of sailors who eat together.</note> (as the number of seven men joined to eat together is called in naval terms) at least one bustard and one goose, or one goose and two ducks, or two geese, or two bustards; and some of those diving water-birds, which we name *Snipe* <note>original: "Becfics"</note> or *Noddies* <note>original: "Nigauts" — likely referring to the Striated Caracara or a species of booby/tern, though the author describes them as divers here.</note>, the figure of which is seen in *Plate VIII. fig. 2*, and of which I shall speak hereafter.

At about three o'clock in the afternoon, we assembled at the place where the Fort had been traced, and it was agreed to name it the *King's Fort*, or the *Royal Fort*. Everyone worked with all their heart, and with an incredible ardor; in such a manner that
