<page-num>368</page-num>
<header>HISTORY OF A VOYAGE</header>
By that very evening, they had already dug a portion of a ditch six feet wide and one foot deep: the example set by the Commander <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville</note> inspired everyone.

On the 2nd of March, four pieces of cannon were offloaded to arm the Fort we were raising; it was proposed to add four more to be taken from the *Sphinx* <note>One of the two ships in the expedition</note>, and six swivel guns <term>pierriers: small cannons used for firing stones or grapeshot, often mounted on pivots</term>.

As it had been resolved to erect a Pyramid in the form of an Obelisk in the middle of the Fort, I proposed to place upon its point the bust of Louis XV, and I took it upon myself to execute it in terracotta. I had seen a grey clay on the shore of a cove which had seemed suitable to me for this purpose; and I set out to go find it. Having found it, I had it loaded into a boat along with some peat. But the boat ran aground because of its too-heavy load, and some was removed to set it afloat. We had been deceived by the ebb tide; for the sea, which is not very regular in those bays except during the new and full moon, had not risen as high as we had calculated. Nearly an hour passed before the boat was put

<page-num>369</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header>
back afloat. In order not to overburden it, Mr. Duclos and I took the course of returning by land, following the coast. We traveled nearly a league <note>About 2.5 to 3 miles</note> over the pebbles, shingles, and rocks that border this coast. The boatmen had orders to come and pick us up at the narrows <term>goulet: a narrow entrance or passage to a harbor or bay</term>, where we had told them we would wait for them. We had much difficulty in getting there, through foggy and very windy weather. Having waited for them there for three-quarters of an hour in vain, and in great darkness, we thought that the receding sea and the violent wind, which was against them, would have prompted the boatmen to put back to the ships. We were resolving to finish the journey by land by going around the bay, which is at least three-quarters of a league across, when we heard the boat approaching; we hurried to get in. But we had hardly rowed five or six fathoms <note>About 30 to 36 feet</note> when the wind became extremely violent; the waves swelled, and the receding sea, aided by the wind which was against us, formed an obstacle that we could not overcome. In an hour and a half...

<summary>The settlers begin constructing a fort and an obelisk featuring a bust of King Louis XV, while the author recounts a dangerous excursion to gather clay that resulted in being stranded by the tides and wind.</summary>
<keywords>Falkland Islands, Fort Saint Louis, Louis XV, construction, maritime navigation, tides, terracotta, Louis-Antoine de Bougainville</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>The narrative continues from the previous page describing the maritime difficulties encountered by the expedition near the coast of the Falkland Islands.</meta>

<page-num>370</page-num>
<header>HISTORY OF A VOYAGE</header>
With difficulty, despite all our efforts, we barely moved upstream ten fathoms <note>original: "toises"; a French unit of length equal to about 6.4 feet</note>. The sea became terrifying; each wave broke with fury against the boat and partially entered inside; we were already all soaked. Tired of struggling in vain against the waves, and seeing that we were in great danger of running aground on the rocks that line the shore—where the waves and the wind were making us drift despite all our efforts—Mr. Duclos said that it was necessary to return to the point of the narrows <note>original: "goulet"; a narrow entrance to a harbor or bay</note> and run aground there. In less than three minutes, despite the oars and the rudder, we saw ourselves thrown toward the shore, about four fathoms <note>original: "brasses"; a measurement of depth or length equal to about 5.3 feet</note> from land. The sea, then furious, was going to break the boat into pieces, and we ourselves were at risk. Our Captain said that it was necessary to jump into the water, and he leapt in first. I followed him at the moment a very large wave came to break against the boat, covered it entirely, and by the jolt caused me to fall into the water; I got up quickly and, very exhausted, took the path back to the settlement.

Upon arriving, I learned that the Lieutenant of the *Sphinx* <note>The name of one of the expedition's ships</note>, who had been out hunting to

<page-num>371</page-num>
<header>TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.</header>
discover the source of the river, had returned. He told us that three or four leagues from the camp, to the Northwest, he had found an immense bay, the entrance of which he could not discover, nor even the bottom of it from the heights: that this bay appeared to him to be at least eight to ten leagues deep into the land; and that from distance to distance he had seen rivers and islands there. We were charmed by this discovery, and we resolved to go and survey it.

Meanwhile, the Fort was progressing, and Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville, the famous French explorer and leader of this expedition to colonize the Falklands</note> proposed to mount the cannons there, which were on their carriages <note>original: "affuts"</note> at the bottom of the hill. From that very moment, we set ourselves to the task of executing this project. For this purpose, we laid planks on the ground to make what is called a bridge, and to prevent the wheels of the carriages from sinking into the earth. By strength of arms alone, and without other instruments or machines than crowbars <note>original: "pinces"</note>, levers, and ropes, we succeeded in bringing up one cannon, despite the height and the steepness of the hillside. Having put it in place, as it was nearly time to finish work

<summary>The crew survives a dangerous shipwreck near the shore, after which they learn of a newly discovered bay and begin the arduous task of hauling cannons up a steep hill to fortify their new settlement.</summary>
<keywords>shipwreck, navigation, Falkland Islands, Louis-Antoine de Bougainville, fortification, exploration, Sphinx (ship), artillery transport</keywords>

<page-num>372</page-num>
->HISTORY OF A VOYAGE<-
of the day, we loaded this cannon and fired it to serve as a signal. We then shouted *Long live the King* <note>original: "Vive le Roi"</note> seven times; and the workers who were occupied with the construction of the lodgings responded to our acclamations.

From that time on, every morning at five o'clock and every evening at seven-thirty, a field cannon firing a one-pound ball was discharged, and a bell was rung to call the men to work and to signal its end. Consequently, the work was soon as advanced as if two hundred laborers had been employed there.

While we were thus occupied on land, the few people remaining aboard the frigates did not stay idle. Every day they embarked items for the Camp: cannons, cannonballs, provisions, utensils, etc.

While work was proceeding with such activity, I had baskets filled with loam <note>original: "terre glaise"</note>, puddled <term>puddled: a process of working clay or loam to make it watertight or workable for modeling</term> with clay for lack of sand suitable for this purpose, and I made my arrangements to work at my ease on the King's bust. I began by

<column-break/>

->TO THE FALKLAND ISLANDS.<- <note>original: "Isles Malouines"</note> <page-num>373</page-num>
modeling the bust; and having no iron bar to support the clay on the pedestal, I substituted a wooden roller. That very evening, the head was roughly sketched out.

But the next day, running to my work, I was not a little disconcerted to see cracks and fissures in it, even though the earth had been very well puddled. I asked Mr. Guyot if he had seen any very fine sand along the coast, so that I might correct the defect of this earth by puddling it again with that sand. They left an hour later for the Camp and gave an account to Mr. de Bougainville <note>Louis-Antoine de Bougainville, the expedition leader</note> of the embarrassment caused to me by the poor quality of this earth. To prevent any useless attempts, this Officer determined to substitute a fleur-de-lis <note>The lily flower symbol of the French monarchy</note> for the bust.

We continued to carry provisions into the Fort for the needs of the new colony, and on the 21st, the first stone of the pyramid was laid.

A singular fact occupied our attention at that time. We had transported about a dozen pigs, both male and female. Among this number was a...

Someone noticed that the castrated pig
usually preceded the return of the others
by about half an hour, went roaming around
the litter, and arranged the hay; that he
pulled some out with his teeth to carry to
the bed, and filled the spots where it was
missing. The others having arrived, they
lay down together, and he only joined
them last. When one of them did not find
themselves at ease, they would get up, and
taking it out on the castrated pig, would
bite him and force him with their teeth to
go find more hay and to reinforce the litter.
The females especially were very delicate
regarding this matter. This phenomenon is
capable of interesting other people besides
Naturalists <note>The author suggests this animal behavior shows a complex social hierarchy or even a form of "servitude" among the swine.</note>.

On the 24th, we went to look for the ma-
jority of our domestic animals which had
escaped. We succeeded in encircling
three horses with ropes and leading them
to the camp: but they soon freed them-
selves by knocking over their handlers,
leapt over the ropes, and fled so far away
that we gave up pursuing them.

We were more fortunate regarding the
cows and the heifers. They had likewise
spread out and dispersed into the country-
side; but having caught a small calf,
we led it near the camp and tied it there
to a stake: its mother, having heard it
bellowing in the evening, came back to
let it suckle, and the others followed her.

After having returned this way for two or
three days in a row, these animals formed
the habit, and returned punctually every
evening to the stable that had been pre-
pared for them.

to erect was not lost from sight; we laid
the first stone of the base, and placed

(a)

This fort was named Fort Saint-Louis. It is situated on high ground that is not overlooked by the neighboring heights. It commands all the surroundings, and especially the entrance to the cove, at the back of which is the new settlement. This entrance is rightly named the "Goulet" <note>The Narrows</note>; for at high tide, it is only a strong pistol-shot wide.

to sculpt in stone the double fleur-de-lis <note>The lily flower was the royal emblem of the French monarchy.</note> which was to be placed on the tip of the pyramid, and to make two wooden medallions, one representing the bust of Louis XV, and the other the arms of France, which were to be applied to the two opposite sides of the pyramid.

When everything was ready for the ceremony of taking possession of the Malouine Islands <note>Now known as the Falkland Islands.</note>, we embarked in our canoes and a fishing boat to go to the fort. Upon our landing at the Goulet <note>The "Goulet" refers to the narrow entrance or "throat" of the harbor.</note>, the fort saluted us with several shots. A troop of the inhabitants, determined to remain in the new colony, were under arms at the Goulet, and they led us to the fort to the sound of the tambourine.

Everyone being assembled at the fort, the pyramid was uncovered; then I solemnly intoned the *Te Deum* <note>original: "Te Deum" — a traditional Latin hymn of praise and thanksgiving: "Thee, O God, we praise."</note> which was sung in partial choir. We shouted "Long live the King" seven times, and twenty-one cannon shots were fired. Mr. de Bougainville then showed the King's patent, which establishes a Commander in the new colony, and handed it to Mr. de Nerville, who was immediately

<page-num>385</page-num>
<header>TO THE MALOUINE ISLANDS.</header>
received and recognized as such. Mr. de Bougainville also proclaimed in the name of the King the other officers, who were also equally recognized.

An altar had been set up in the fort at the very foot of the pyramid. I intended to say Mass there, to make the ceremony of taking possession more august and more solemn; but the wind blew there with such force, despite the tent that had been erected, that it was judged appropriate to stick to the ceremony I have just described.

[ Thus France has acquired a legitimate right to the sovereignty of the Malouine Islands. They were not taken from any men: it is a conquest that industry has made over nature ].

End of the first Volume.

<summary>This passage describes the formal ceremony of France taking possession of the Falkland Islands, including the unveiling of a monument to Louis XV and the appointment of colonial officials. The author emphasizes that the land was uninhabited, framing the settlement as a victory of human industry over the elements rather than a conquest of other people.</summary>
